- the same abstract-GSOS distributive law as §34.3u;
- a kernel-pair / coequalizer factorization of the period fold;
- a Banach contraction theorem for consolidation;
- allocation maps with ε ∘ A₁ = ε;
- a rank argument on a classification DAG.

Each item was read against the current frontier, products such as Ω × Ω.
- **Conservation / chain complex ↔ Sperner parity.** The finite Cut proof *is* a conservation law: `Sperner.odd_goods` counts good chains mod 2 by door pairing, and `captured` applies it to finite tables. A parity invariant needs finite support. On infinite lattices that support is exactly what the reset (§34.3s–t) manufactures, by forcing finitely many cell values. The analogy reproduces the existing architecture (finite support, then parity) and offers no new route to finite support on Ω × Ω.
- **Allocation, ε ∘ A₁ = ε ↔ the reset.** A reset is an operator on bifunctions that preserves the invariant "monotone and witness-free", as an allocation preserves ε. This is an interpretation only.
- **μ4 / B2 (termination by DAG rank, cascade bound = height) ↔ the Dershowitz–Manna induction of §34.3t.** Generators after k rounds lie strictly atop a chain ⊥ = m₀ < m₁ < … < m_k (each new generator is m ⊔ ℓ > m). So on a lattice of finite height h the induction ends after at most h + 1 resets: a cascade bound, quantitative but not new in kind.
- **Kernel pair / coequalizer (sampling is valid only with a characterized kernel) ↔ descent of Cut along quotients.** A counterexample would fold to a finite one along a congruence that the witness relation reflects (the "rational" remark of §34.3u). Ω has no lattice congruence onto a finite lattice that keeps distinct chains apart, because joins across chains are ⊤. So this does not reach Ω × Ω.
- **§17 multi-tier consolidation (Beck–Chevalley interchange) ↔ coordinatewise resets on L₁ × L₂.** A reset with anchors A₁ × L₂ is structurally valid in a chain-finite complete product: its joins are finite, over (a, x₂) with a ∈ ↓x₁ ∩ A₁. Running it, and then a second-coordinate reset with anchors L₁ × A₂, keeps first coordinates in a finite join-closed set. The induction breaks at the second stage: its anchor rows (x₁, a₂) are infinitely many, so the non-bad anchors, and with them the new generators, need not be finite. This is the fibred form of the obstruction isolated in §34.3u item 2.
- **Contraction theorem, DL coherence diagrams, time-indexed ledgers.** No order-theoretic counterpart, or already covered by §34.3u.

On finite lattices every one of these devices is vacuous for testing: they act on a witness-free F, and on finite lattices none exists (`cl_finite`). So no SAT experiment discriminates here.

**Verdict.** The framework does not help with the frontier. Its one structural echo, conservation as a parity invariant needing finite support, restates why the proof architecture is "manufacture finite support, then count", and why Ω × Ω is hard: neither one-sided reset manufactures it there.

## 34.3w New: the two-sided reset, and the frontier shrinks to Ω × C₃

Prompted by an outside proposal: "pin signatures" and a "finite-fibre theorem" for L × K with K finite. Both aim at the cells where one coordinate escapes while the other is pinned. Checking them gave three results.

**1. The frontier is smaller than Ω × Ω.** Ω × C₂ is covered (§34.3t), but **Ω × C₃** fails the neighbourhood hub condition on both sides at 8 of its 22 corners on the truncations, all of the form (z, 1), with 1 the middle of C₃. Ω × M₃ fails at 15 of 23 corners. Ω × C₃ fails the box criterion at [⊥, ⊤] too (`experiments/localize/products_check.py`, run on these families). So the finite-fibre theorem is exactly the missing piece, and **Ω × C₃** is the smallest test case: chain-finite, of unbounded height, with exactly three hubs (⊥, 0), (⊥, 1), (⊥, 2) and three co-hubs.

**2. Pin signatures, sharpened.** For x ∈ D and y ∉ D, monotonicity gives w(y) = F(⊥, y) ≤ c ≤ F(x, ⊤) = v(x) with c = F(⊥, ⊤) the corner. The signatures are therefore forced by c's position. On Ω × Ω:
- At an interior corner, v and w take finitely many values, and all escape lies in strips Ω × [a, b] or [a, b] × Ω.
- At c = (⊤, ⊥), every value lies in a finite interval [a(y), ⊤] × [⊥, b(x)]. The two coordinates decouple: the column fixes the first, the row the second. But infinitely many such intervals occur.

**3. The two-sided reset** (`lean/Mordor/TwoSided.lean`, standard axioms only).
- `reset_core_dual` resets columns to meets of co-anchor columns, the order dual of `reset_core`. `reset_core` now also records that anchor rows are unchanged.
- **`two_sided`.** On a lattice with finitely many hubs and finitely many co-hubs, reset rows with anchors {⊥} ∪ hubs, then columns with co-anchors {⊤} ∪ co-hubs. Under no witness the result G is monotone and witness-free, and every value lies in an interval [w, v] where:
  - w = G(⊥, y) ≠ ⊥ ranges over a finite set W: meets of bottom-row values, or the ideal of a non-co-hub;
  - v = G(x, ⊤) ≠ ⊤ ranges over a finite set V: joins of anchor values, or the filter of a non-hub.
- **`cut_two_sided`.** If moreover all intervals [w, v] with ⊥ < w ≤ v < ⊤ are finite, every cut has a witness.
- **`cut_two_sided_capture`.** It is enough that each infinite such interval lies inside D (v ∈ D) or inside U (w ∉ D). Its values are then captured by v, respectively w. **The only obstruction left is a cut that *crosses* an infinite interval: w ∈ D, v ∉ D.**

**Application to Ω × K, K finite** (lattice facts checked on truncations, `experiments/products/strips_check.py`):
- The hubs are (⊥, k) and the co-hubs (⊤, k), so `two_sided` applies.
- The infinite intervals [w, v] with ⊥ < w ≤ v < ⊤ are exactly the strips [(⊥, k), (⊤, k′)] with ⊥ < k ≤ k′ < ⊤ in K:
  - Ω × C₃: only [(⊥,1), (⊤,1)];
  - Ω × C₄: [1,1], [1,2] and [2,2];
  - Ω × M₃: one strip per atom.
- So **Cut holds on Ω × K for every cut that crosses no strip**. On Ω × C₃: for every cut except those with (⊥, 1) ∈ D ∌ (⊤, 1).

**The crossing case on Ω × C₃** (hand argument, not formalized). Write D̂ = {a : (a, 1) ∈ D}, a proper cut of Ω. Suppose the reset G has G((⊥,1), ⊤) = (⊤, 1) and G(⊥, (⊤,1)) = (⊥, 1).
- Every row (a, 1) with a ∈ D̂ then has G((a,1), ⊤) = (⊤, 1); every column (b, 1) with b ∉ D̂ has G(⊥, (b,1)) = (⊥, 1).
- So the slice cells ((a,1), (b,1)) take values (p, 1) with second coordinate exactly 1.
- The slice problem Ĥ(a, b) = π₁ G((a,1), (b,1)) on Ω with cut D̂ has a witness, since Ω is end-finite (`cut_of_end_finite`). It lifts, because the value's second coordinate is 1.

Here, unlike the general products of §34.3u, slice witnesses do lift: strip confinement pins the second coordinate. What remains open is the sub-case where the bottom strip row or the top strip column leaves the strip. There the slice values can have second coordinate 0 or 2, and the projection loses the witness.

**Assessment of the proposal.**
- *Pin signatures:* correct, and sharper in the corner-sandwich form above.
- *Finite-fibre theorem:* the right target. It is now reduced in Lean to crossing strips, and for Ω × C₃ to one sub-case.
- *Paired rank:* the natural measure for iterating `two_sided` on strips is the Dershowitz–Manna order on the finite multiset of crossing intervals. It needs a region-relative two-sided reset, whose obstacle is that joins of anchor values leave the region.
- *Counterexample search:* by the fold remark of §34.3u, any branch-shifting scheme must be aperiodic.

## 34.3x New: CL on L × C₃ for every end-finite L (Ω × C₃ settled)

The crossing case left by §34.3w closes. `lean/Mordor/Strip.lean`, standard axioms only, no truncation evidence needed.

**Theorem** (`Strip.cut_prod_three`, `Mordor.semi_of_prod_three`). Let L be a nontrivial complete lattice whose proper principal ideals and proper principal filters are all finite. Then every cut of every monotone F on L × C₃ has a witness, and semi-CL and CL hold on L × C₃. In particular **Ω × C₃**, the smallest open case of §34.3w, satisfies CL.

**The mechanism: one crossing strip** (`cut_one_strip`, `semi_of_one_strip`). Hypotheses:
- finitely many hubs and co-hubs;
- every infinite interval [w, v] with ⊥ < w ≤ v < ⊤ lies inside one interval [s, t];
- [s, t] is relatively end-finite;
- [s, t] satisfies the modularity condition "(g ⊓ t) ⊔ s = s and (g ⊔ s) ⊓ t = t never hold together".

Then Cut holds. The proof runs the two-sided reset (§34.3w).
- *No crossing.* `cut_two_sided_capture` applies.
- *Crossing* (s ∈ D, t ∉ D). Three lemmas:
  - **`core_finite`.** On core cells (x ≥ s, y ≤ t), a value z ∈ [s, t] lies below m = G(x, t) ⊓ t ≠ t (else (x, t) is a witness) and above n = G(s, y) ⊔ s ≠ s. Also m ≥ (G(s, t) ⊓ t) ⊔ s and n ≤ (G(s, t) ⊔ s) ⊓ t. By the modularity condition one of these two corner bounds is proper, and relative end-finiteness then leaves finitely many core values. This is the end-finite argument with s as a soft bottom and t as a soft top.
  - **`strip_contra`.**
    - *Off the core a strip value cannot be part of a witness:* z ≤ x forces x ≥ s, and y ≤ z forces y ≤ t.
    - *Lower or raise them:* where x ≱ s, lower z to s ⊔ ⋁{outside values ≤ z}; where x ≥ s and y ≰ t, raise it to t ⊓ ⋀{outside values ≥ z}. The result is still monotone on the cells D × U and witness-free, and now has finitely many values, because the values outside [s, t] are finitely many: they lie in finite intervals.
    - *No crossing hypothesis needed:* the lemma does not even use s ∈ D or t ∉ D.
  - **`extend_mono`.** A bifunction monotone on D × U extends to a globally monotone one with the same cell values, via F′(x, y) = ⋁{F(x′, y′) : x′ ≤ x, y′ ≤ y, (x′, y′) a cell}. So `cut_of_values_in_finite` applies.

For L × C₃ (`cut_prod_three`) take s = (⊥, 1) and t = (⊤, 1). Each hypothesis follows from end-finiteness of L:
- hubs ⊆ {⊥} × C₃ and co-hubs ⊆ {⊤} × C₃;
- an infinite interval needs first coordinates ⊥ and ⊤, hence second coordinates in [1, 1];
- [s, t] ≅ L is relatively end-finite;
- the modularity condition reduces to ⊥ ≠ ⊤ in L.

**Scope.** One strip covers exactly the fibres K whose middle K ∖ {⊥, ⊤} is a single element. The next cases:
- K = M_n (middle an antichain) gives several pairwise incomparable strips. A multi-strip version (crossing strips modified, non-crossing ones captured) should handle these.
- K = C₄ gives nested strips [1,1], [1,2], [2,2]. Their union [(⊥,1), (⊤,2)] ≅ L × C₂ is not relatively end-finite, so this needs recursion.
- Ω × Ω, and finite posets, remain open.

## 34.3y New: several separated strips — CL on L × Mₙ

`lean/Mordor/Strips.lean`, standard axioms only.

**`cut_strips`.** Hypotheses:
- finitely many hubs and co-hubs;
- every infinite interval [w, v] with ⊥ < w ≤ v < ⊤ lies in one of finitely many strips [s, t];
- each strip is relatively end-finite and modular at s, t;
- the strips are *separated*: no element of one strip lies below an element of another.

Then Cut holds. The proof runs §34.3x's argument on all strips at once.
- **Crossing strips** (s ∈ D, t ∉ D) are lowered or raised to finitely many values. The guide posts are the finitely many values outside every strip. Separation guarantees that a value in one strip is never compared with a value in another, so one finite set of guide posts serves all crossing strips (`pick` selects a value's unique strip).
- **Non-crossing strips** keep their values, which may be infinitely many. They are captured by t when t ∈ D, and by s when s ∉ D. `capture_contra` on the monotone extension (`Strip.extend_mono`) then closes the proof.

**Corollary** (`cut_prod_antichain`, `Mordor.semi_of_prod_antichain`). Let L be a nontrivial complete lattice with finite proper principal ideals and filters, and K a finite complete lattice whose middle K ∖ {⊥, ⊤} is an antichain (K = Mₙ; C₃ = M₁). Then Cut and CL hold on L × K. The strips are [(⊥, k), (⊤, k)], one for each middle element k:
- separation is exactly the antichain condition;
- each strip is ≅ L, hence relatively end-finite;
- the modularity condition reduces to ⊥ ≠ ⊤ in L.

**Examples:** Ω × M₃, Ω × M_n.

**Still open:**
- **Ω × C₄**, the first fibre K with comparable middle elements. Its infinite intervals [1,1], [1,2], [2,2] are nested, not separated, and their union [(⊥,1), (⊤,2)] ≅ L × C₂ is not relatively end-finite. Inside that union, the would-be relative strips [(⊥,2), (⊤,1)] are empty. This suggests a recursion depth of one: a two-sided reset relative to the core of [(⊥,1), (⊤,2)], whose only escapes are the finitely many values outside it.
- Ω × Ω.
- Finite posets.

## 34.3z Outside proposals read again; L × C₄ reduced to one configuration

A second outside review (fresh session) proposed four directions. Checked:

**1. Finite-support crossing.** The finite-support lemma (ACC ⟹ every supremum is attained on a finite subset) is Mathlib's `WellFoundedGT.isSupFiniteCompact`. `wellFoundedGT_iff_isSupFiniteCompact` shows it is in fact *equivalent* to ACC. Its consequence is correct: if box cells (x_i, y_i) have values with ⋁ z_i = t, then x* = ⋁ x_i ∉ D, else (x*, ⋁ y_i) is a witness. But it constrains only realized values, and only through join-closure. It is the join gap of §31.1 (R(F) join-closed) seen from one box, so it adds no new lever. The proposed "finite crossing lemma" is the open problem itself.

**2. Capture-or-ratchet.** A finite table, a pivot, and a well-founded rank on the active boundary: this is exactly the architecture of `IdealFinite.key`, with Dershowitz–Manna on region generators (§34.3t). The missing piece the proposal names, a rank once boundaries are infinite in both directions, is the frontier.

**3. L × C₄ (the recommended intermediate target).** Worked by hand; *not formalized*. Write s_k = (⊥, k), t_k = (⊤, k), and S_k = [s_k, t_k] ≅ L for the strips. After the two-sided reset, every infinite interval lies in S₁ ∪ S₂. Cuts with s₁ ∉ D or t₂ ∈ D cross nothing (§34.3w). Otherwise there are three configurations:
- **(B) only S₁ crossed** (s₂ ∉ D, so S₂ ⊆ U). *Wall argument:*
  - Every column y ≰ t₁ satisfies y ≥ s₂ ∉ D. So a value z ∈ S₁ at a cell with x ≥ s₁ and y ≰ t₁ lies above δ = G(s₁, s₂), and δ ≰ s₁ forces δ₁ ≠ ⊥. Hence z ∈ ↑δ ∩ S₁, a finite set.
  - These values therefore need no inflation. The values in S₁ at core cells are finite (`core_finite`), and the values at cells x ≱ s₁ are lowered as in §34.3x.
  - S₂ is captured by s₂. Every other value lies in a finite interval.
- **(C) only S₂ crossed** (t₁ ∈ D, so S₁ ⊆ D). Symmetric: the row wall t₁ ∈ D lies above every row x ≱ s₂.
- **(B∧C) both crossed**, i.e. s₁, s₂ ∈ D and t₁, t₂ ∉ D. Remains open. Lowering and raising per strip conflict only on cells with x₂ = 1 and y₂ = 2.
  - There a lower-strip value needs to stay ≰ x, an upper-strip value needs to stay ≱ y, and neither wall applies.
  - *Rigorous negative remark:* no pair of value maps fixes this. Take u inflationary on S₁ and d deflationary on S₂ with u(z) ≤ d(z′) whenever z ≤ z′. The pair z = (a, 1), z′ = (a, 2) forces u(a, 1) = (a, 1) for every such a, so u cannot have finite image. Decoupling nested strips must use the cells, not just the values.
  - The configuration is a coupled "thick cut" between layer 1 (rows: D̂₁) and layer 2 (columns: the complement of D̂₂), with D̂₂ ⊆ D̂₁, where D̂_k = {a : (a, k) ∈ D}.

So L × C₄ is reduced to the cuts with s₁, s₂ ∈ D and t₁, t₂ ∉ D (hand argument for (B) and (C); formalizing would need a multi-strip variant with walls).

**4. Counterexample search on Ω × Ω.** Agreed as a stress test. By the fold remark (§34.3u) a counterexample must be aperiodic, and on any Ω × K for finite K it must cross nested strips (§34.3y). The thick-cut configuration above is the first place where a branch-shifting pattern is not yet ruled out.

## 34.3z′ L × C₄, doubly crossed cuts: what is known, what fails

Hand analysis, *not formalized*. Notation: s_k = (⊥, k) and t_k = (⊤, k); S_k = [s_k, t_k] ≅ L; D̂_k = {a : (a, k) ∈ D}. The open configuration of §34.3z is s₁, s₂ ∈ D and t₁, t₂ ∉ D, with G the two-sided reset.

**1. Localization to one block of cells (holds).** Give the strip modifications of §34.3x–y one common finite guide set: the values outside S₁ ∪ S₂, plus the core values of S₁ and of S₂. All of these are finite. The modification:
- lower values in S₁ where x₂ = 0;
- raise them where x₂ ≥ 1 and y₂ ≥ 2, except at cells with x₂ = 1 and y₂ = 2;
- lower values in S₂ where x₂ ≤ 1 and y₂ ≤ 2, except at those same cells;
- raise them where y₂ = 3;
- leave the remaining harmless values unchanged at first, then lower or raise them in the same way.

A raised value lies below a lowered one at comparable cells only when both cells have x₂ = 1 and y₂ = 2. So, keeping the values on

  **Q = {cells with x₂ = 1, y₂ = 2}**

unchanged, the result is monotone on cells, witness-free, and has finitely many values outside Q. On Q only two kinds of value can take part in a witness:
- values in S₁ ∩ D, which can lie below the row;
- values in S₂ ∩ U, which can lie above the column.

Values in S₁ ∩ U and S₂ ∩ D are harmless there. **A counterexample on L × C₄ must therefore carry infinitely many such values on Q**, whose rows are D̂₁ × {1} and whose columns are (L ∖ D̂₂) × {2}.

**2. Value maps cannot finish Q (holds).** Raise the values in S₁ ∩ D and lower those in S₂ ∩ U by maps u, d with finite images. Then u(a, 1) ≤ d(b, 2) is needed for every comparable pair of values (a, 1) ≤ (b, 2) on comparable cells of Q, with a ∈ D̂₁ and b ∉ D̂₂.
- With a common finite guide set this requires a finite Γ ⊆ L separating every such pair: a ≤ γ ≤ b for some γ ∈ Γ.
- In Ω no such Γ exists once D̂₂ meets infinitely many chains, since each chain needs its own separator.
- This holds *even when the cut is not thick* (D̂₁ = D̂₂). §34.3z located the conflict only at thick elements; in fact it occurs at all such pairs.

Q needs an argument that uses the cells. For non-thick rows and columns the walls x⁺ = (x₁, 2) ∈ D and y⁻ = (y₁, 1) ∉ D sandwich Q values between values at core cells. But the upper bound G(x⁺, y) can itself be an unbounded value in S₁, or t₁. So the sandwich does not close.

**3. The box cannot be cut out (holds).** Every strip value lies in the box [s₁, t₂] ≅ L × C₂, and box cells take only finitely many values outside it ("escapes"). L × C₂ satisfies Cut (§34.3y with K = C₂), but a box-with-escapes lemma is **false**. Take any escape e ∈ D with e ∨ s₁ ∉ D, put it at every box cell, and the box has no witness:
- e ≤ x would force e ∨ s₁ ≤ x ∈ D;
- y ≤ e is impossible since e ∈ D.

So the contradiction must use cells outside the box. This is the join gap (§31.1) in finite form: e ∨ s₁ leaves D.

**4. Evidence for truth.** Two hand attempts at a counterexample on Ω × C₄ with a thick cut (D̂₂ = {⊥}, D̂₁ = {⊥} ∪ atoms) each died on an end-finite slice argument:
- The only upper row s₂ must avoid witnesses on the box [s₂, ⊤] ≅ Ω × C₂, which Cut on Ω × C₂ forbids.
- Column (⊤, 0) forces a monotone map on Ω ∖ {⊤} with g(c) ≰ c, impossible in Ω, since it fails at the top of the chain containing g(⊥).

Status: L × C₄ is open exactly for doubly crossed cuts. A proof has to handle the block Q with cell-dependent modifications, or with a parity argument on a finite table that also contains cells outside [s₁, t₂].

*Resolved in §34.3z″:* the corner G(s₁, t₂) decides Q, and a down-closed zone rule replaces the strip modifications altogether.

## 34.3z″ New: CL on L × K for every finite K — the corner lemma (Ω × C₄ settled)

Prompted by an outside research brief (sixth round) whose main question was: *what mechanism makes the infinitely many dangerous values on the block Q contradictory, now that finite capture, independent strip resets and box localization are exhausted?* The brief asked for a cell-dependent argument that uses cells outside the box. The answer is short, needs neither the reset nor strips, and covers every finite fibre. `lean/Mordor/Layers.lean`, standard axioms only.

**Theorem** (`Layers.cut_prod_finite`, `Mordor.semi_of_prod_finite`). Let L be a complete lattice whose proper principal ideals and filters are finite (e.g. Ω), and K any finite complete lattice. Then every cut of every monotone F on L × K has a witness, so semi-CL and CL hold on L × K. This subsumes L × C₃ (§34.3x) and L × Mₙ (§34.3y), and settles **Ω × C₄**, Ω × Bₙ, Ω × N₅, and so on.

Write s_k = (⊥, k) and t_k = (⊤, k). Suppose F has no witness for D.

**1. Corner lemma.** Let (x, y) ≤ (x′, y′) be cells with values z = F(x, y) and z′ = F(x′, y′). Suppose z₂ ≤ x₂ (so s_{z₂} ≤ x) and y′₂ ≤ z′₂ (so y′ ≤ t_{z′₂}). Put k = z₂, k′ = z′₂ and h = F(s_k, t_{k′}). Both s_k ∈ D and t_{k′} ∉ D hold, so (s_k, t_{k′}) is a cell.
- h₁ ≠ ⊤. Otherwise r = F(x′, t_{k′}) ≥ h has r₁ = ⊤, and r ≥ z′ gives r₂ ≥ k′. So t_{k′} ≤ r, a witness.
- e = F(s_k, y) has e₁ ≠ ⊥. We have e ≤ z, so e₂ ≤ k. If also e₁ = ⊥, then e ≤ s_k, a witness.
- Since e ≤ h and e ≤ z, z₁ ∈ ↑e₁ with e₁ ∈ ↓h₁ ∖ {⊥}.

So z lies in the finite set Γ = ⋃_{k,k′ ∈ K} {w : ∃ e ≠ ⊥, e ≤ F(s_k, t_{k′})₁, e ≤ w₁} (taken over the pairs with F(s_k, t_{k′})₁ ≠ ⊤). Γ is fixed before any modification. Only the case k = k′, c = d appeared before, as `core_finite`.

**2. Zones from cells.** A value z ∉ Γ at a cell may be lowered to λ(z) = s_{z₂} ⊔ ⋁{γ ∈ Γ : γ ≤ z} when z₂ ≰ x₂: then λ(z) ≥ s_{z₂} is not below x, and λ(z) ≤ z is not above y. It may be raised to ρ(z) = t_{z₂} ⊓ ⋀{γ ∈ Γ : γ ≥ z} when y₂ ≰ z₂. The choice:

  **lower z at (x, y) iff some cell (x′, y′) ≥ (x, y) carries a value z′ ∉ Γ with y′₂ ≤ z′₂.**

- *Lowered cells form a down-set,* so a raised value never lies below a lowered one.
- *Lowering is legal.* If z₂ ≤ x₂, the corner lemma puts z in Γ.
- *Raising is legal.* If y₂ ≤ z₂, the cell itself triggers lowering.
- *Monotone.* Values in Γ are left alone and act as guide posts. λ and ρ are monotone, and λ ≤ id ≤ ρ.
- *Finitely many values.* The values of λ and ρ are the joins s_k ⊔ ⋁Q and the meets t_k ⊓ ⋀Q, with k ∈ K and Q ⊆ Γ.

`Strip.extend_mono` and `Pin.cut_of_values_in_finite` finish. With K trivial this is `cut_of_end_finite`.

**What the brief's directions became.**
- *Direction 1 (cell-dependent argument on Q):* this is it. On §34.3z′'s block Q the corner is G(s₁, t₂), and its first coordinate decides the case. If it is ⊤, Q carries no S₂ values. If it is ⊥, Q carries no S₁ values. Otherwise Q carries finitely many strip values. The rows and columns used, s₁ and t₂, lie outside Q, as the brief anticipated.
- *Direction 3 (coupled reset):* the zone rule is the coupling. Strips, crossing and capture all drop out. The only coupling needed is that the lowered cells are closed downward. By an exhaustive SAT check over all cut types (`experiments/products/zones_check.py`), zones that depend only on the layers (x₂, y₂) and use the pairwise box exemption are infeasible already for C₅. Zones that depend on the corners of G succeed for C₃–C₆ (`zones3.py`). The down-closure rule above is what that search pointed to, and it needs no case analysis at all.
- *Directions 2 and 4 (Sperner tables, minimal counterexample to join-closure):* not needed for products with a finite factor. They remain the candidates for the general frontier.

**Finitely many layers in use is enough.** The proof uses finiteness of K only through the layers z₂ of values. `Layers.cut_prod_layers` takes any complete K and assumes, under no witness, that cell values have second coordinates in a finite set. This is the form to aim at for Ω × Ω.

**Ω × Ω: where it stands** (hand analysis, not formalized). Run the same construction with the roles of the coordinates exchanged:
- lower to (z₁, ⊥) ⊔ ⋁{γ ≤ z} and raise to (z₁, ⊤) ⊓ ⋀{γ ≥ z};
- take the guide set Γ′ to be all corner-lemma outputs, now an infinite union.

Joins and meets over Γ′ still exist, and monotonicity and witness-freeness go through unchanged. If the corner family

  {F((k, ⊥), (k′, ⊤))₂ : (k, ⊥) ∈ D, (k′, ⊤) ∉ D} ∖ {⊤}

is finite, the second coordinates of the guides lie in a finite set. Then so do the second coordinates of all modified values, and `cut_prod_layers` finishes. Symmetrically for the family F((⊥, k), (⊤, k′))₁. **A counterexample on Ω × Ω therefore needs both corner families infinite.** *(Superseded by §34.3z‴: one family is always bounded, and Ω × Ω satisfies CL.)* That means F must move the anchors (k, ⊥), (⊤, k′) diagonally, so that corners track the anchors' own coordinates. This is the next target, from either side.

## 34.3z‴ New: CL on L₁ × L₂ for any two end-finite lattices (Ω × Ω settled)

Prompted by an outside brief (seventh round) proposing three programs for Ω × Ω:
1. branch-switching with Ramsey canonicalization and "rectangle amalgamation";
2. compressing interaction types instead of values;
3. a coupled transition system for the diagonal corner families.

None of the machinery turned out to be needed. The corner families of §34.3z″ are always bounded, through one of two anchors chosen by the position of the single corner F(⊥, ⊤). `lean/Mordor/Plane.lean`, standard axioms only.

**Theorem** (`Plane.cut_prod_end_finite`, `Mordor.semi_of_prod_end_finite`). Let L₁, L₂ be complete lattices whose proper principal ideals and filters are finite. Then every cut of every monotone F on L₁ × L₂ has a witness, so semi-CL and CL hold. In particular **Ω × Ω**, the test case named in §34.3t, satisfies CL. This is the first product theorem for Cut with two infinite factors.

**Ingredients** (all in `Plane.lean`):
- **`zone_lo`, `zone_hi`.** The zone construction of §34.3z″ as an abstract lemma. It takes a guide predicate, lowering and raising maps, the "stuck" predicates, and a corner hypothesis. `zone_lo` lowers below non-guide values that are stuck for raising. `zone_hi` is the dual: it raises above values that are stuck for lowering, and its corner hypothesis makes the *upper* value a guide. Monotonicity of the maps is needed only between comparable non-guide cell values.
- **`cut_prod_layers_fst`.** `cut_prod_layers` with the coordinates exchanged, via `cut_of_swap`.
- **`collapse_lo`.** Suppose every corner F((⊥, k), (⊤, k′)) over the cells has first coordinate ≤ m for a fixed m ≠ ⊤. The corner lemma puts every value that cannot be lowered (in the second-coordinate layers) into S × L₂, where S = ⋃_{e ≤ m, e ≠ ⊥} ↑e is finite. Lowering to (⌊z₁⌋_S, z₂) and raising to (⌈z₁⌉_S, z₂) then leaves finitely many first coordinates. The guide set is infinite, but its first coordinates are finitely many: a finite "interaction type" in the sense of the brief's Strategy 2. `cut_prod_layers_fst` finishes.
- **`collapse_hi`.** The dual: every corner has first coordinate ≥ n ≠ ⊥. The corner lemma's upper half puts the upper value z′ into ⋃_{f ≥ n, f ≠ ⊤} ↓f × L₂, and `zone_hi` is used.
- **`cut_prod_cross`.** Suppose every value lies in a finite set, or in a horizontal strip L₁ × T₂, or in a vertical strip T₁ × L₂, with T₁, T₂ finite sets of middle elements. Horizontal values are lowered and raised in the second-coordinate layers, vertical ones in the first. A horizontal and a vertical non-guide value are never comparable: comparability would put the horizontal one in (↓T₁ ∪ ↑T₁) × T₂, a finite set added to the guides. So one zone rule with type-dependent maps works.

**The case analysis on c = F(⊥, ⊤).** Every value satisfies F(⊥, y) ≤ z ≤ F(x, ⊤), with F(⊥, y) ≤ c ≤ F(x, ⊤), F(⊥, y) ≠ ⊥ and F(x, ⊤) ≠ ⊤.
- *c₁ = ⊥, c₂ ≠ ⊤.* Then F(⊥, y) = (⊥, w₂) with ⊥ ≠ w₂ ≤ c₂, so z₂ ∈ ⋃_{e ≤ c₂, e ≠ ⊥} ↑e, a finite set: `cut_prod_layers`. Symmetrically for *c₁ = ⊤, c₂ ≠ ⊥*, and for the cases with the coordinates exchanged (`cut_prod_layers_fst`).
- *c₁, c₂ both middle.* For each coordinate i, a value has z_i ∈ Lo_i = ⋃_{e ≤ c_i, e ≠ ⊥} ↑e when F(⊥, y)_i ≠ ⊥, and z_i ∈ Hi_i = ⋃_{f ≥ c_i, f ≠ ⊤} ↓f when F(x, ⊤)_i ≠ ⊤. At least one of each pair holds. So z lies in a finite box (Lo ∪ Hi)₁ × (Lo ∪ Hi)₂, or in the horizontal strip L₁ × (Lo₂ ∩ Hi₂), or in the vertical strip (Lo₁ ∩ Hi₁) × L₂: `cut_prod_cross`.
- *c = (⊥, ⊤).* If the element (⊥, ⊤) is in D, it is a row. Every corner satisfies F((⊥, k), (⊤, k′)) ≤ F((⊥, ⊤), ⊤) =: M, and M ≥ c forces M₂ = ⊤, hence M₁ ≠ ⊤: `collapse_lo` with m = M₁. If (⊥, ⊤) ∉ D, it is a column. Every corner of the other orientation satisfies F((k, ⊥), (k′, ⊤)) ≥ F(⊥, (⊥, ⊤)) =: N, and N ≤ c forces N₁ = ⊥, hence N₂ ≠ ⊥: `collapse_hi` on the swapped product with n = N₂.
- *c = (⊤, ⊥).* Symmetric, through the anchor (⊤, ⊥).

**The brief's programs, in this light.**
- *Strategy 1 (rectangle amalgamation).* As stated it cannot work from monotonicity alone. The mixed cell (x₁ ∧ x₂, y₁ ∨ y₂) is comparable to neither (x₁, y₁) nor (x₂, y₂); it lies above (x₁ ∧ x₂, y_i) and below (x_i, y₁ ∨ y₂). Any amalgamation therefore has to pass through values at further cells. In the proof that role falls to the fixed anchors (⊥, ⊤) and (⊤, ⊥), which are where cross-branch meets and joins land. No Ramsey thinning is needed, because the corner families are bounded outright, not merely canonicalized.
- *Strategy 2 (profiles).* Realized by `collapse_lo`/`_hi`: the guide set is S × L₂, infinite, but the modification reads only ⌊z₁⌋_S and ⌈z₁⌉_S, which take finitely many values. This is the "generalization of `cut_prod_layers` to finitely many interaction types" the brief asked for. The finite type is the first coordinate's floor or ceiling in S.
- *Strategy 3 (diagonal dynamics).* The diagonal obstruction of §34.3z″ was an artefact of looking at the corner families without the anchor (⊥, ⊤). Once c is (⊥, ⊤) or (⊤, ⊥), that anchor bounds one family, as a row or as a column, so no recurrence or escape analysis arises.

**An eighth outside brief** was written against `dcec27d`, before this section. Its Programs 1–3 (homogeneous defects, loop certificates, finite-cover versus infinite-matching) all target "both corner families infinite and diagonally coupled". By the case analysis above that configuration does not arise: when c ∉ {(⊥, ⊤), (⊤, ⊥)}, no corner family is needed at all, and when c = (⊥, ⊤) or (⊤, ⊥), one family is bounded through that anchor. Its Program 4 asks for a bridge from stabilization of the orbit r_n to join-closure of R(F). That bridge is the frontier statement itself (`cutMaps_iff_joinClosed`, §34.4a). The extra lever `orbit_tight` needs top-closedness, which fails already on Ω × Ω: D = ↓(a, ⊥) ∪ ↓(⊥, b), with a, b in the middle, misses its join (a, b) ≠ ⊤. On a top-closed lattice, two incomparable elements always join to ⊤, so there the bridge only says that ⊤ ∈ R(F) as soon as R(F) contains two incomparable elements. That is a restatement, not a new route.

**Next frontier.** Ω × Ω × Ω, and more generally products in which one factor is not end-finite: the case analysis uses end-finiteness of *both* factors for the sets Lo and Hi. Beyond products: chain-finite lattices with infinite proper principal ideals and filters, not covered by the box criterion.

## 34.3z⁗ Ω³: where the plane argument stops (hand analysis, ninth outside brief)

A ninth outside brief, written against `0dacf0c`, proposed four programs:
1. a three-coordinate corner calculus for Ω³;
2. face transfer and cube consistency;
3. a finite-separator theorem for unresolved boxes;
4. common-guide gluing.

Checking Program 1 against `Plane.lean` shows why the two-factor proof does not iterate, and isolates the lemma that every route to Ω³ goes through. None of this is formalized.

**1. The corner lemma needs a one-dimensional fibre.** Take fibre coordinates C and keep the rest as layers. For a stuck pair (z below z′), the corner argument gives e ≤ z with e_C ≠ ⊥_C and h ≥ e with h_C ≠ ⊤_C. If |C| = 1 these are the same coordinate, and z_C ∈ ↑e_C with e_C ∈ ↓h_C is finite. If |C| ≥ 2 they can be different coordinates: e = (e₁, ⊥, ·) and h = (⊤, h₂, ·) bound nothing. So every collapse step must flatten one coordinate at a time, keeping all the others as layers.

**2. Slabs of different orientation meet in lines.** On Ω³ the sandwich F(⊥, y) ≤ z ≤ F(x, ⊤) through c = F(⊥, ⊤) pins a coordinate of each value only cell by cell. These are the brief's nine defect labels. For instance, with c = (⊥, c₂, c₃) and c₂, c₃ ≠ ⊤, each value has z₂ ∈ Lo₂ or z₃ ∈ Lo₃, depending on the cell. In `cut_prod_cross` a horizontal and a vertical value were comparable only inside a finite set. In three dimensions, z₁ ∈ T₁ and z ≤ z′ with z′₂ ∈ T₂ only gives z ∈ T₁ × ↓T₂ × L₃, an infinite line. The cross theorem does not lift.

**3. Every route funnels into one lemma.** The signatures c = (⊥, ⊥, c₃) with c₃ ≠ ⊤, and (⊤, ⊤, c₃) with c₃ ≠ ⊥, and their permutations, pin one coordinate of every value to a finite set. Collapsing the Ω-coordinate of Ω × Ω² (`collapse_lo` with fibre Ω and layers Ω²) does the same. Both leave

  **(G1)** Cut on L₁ × L₂ × K, with L₁, L₂ end-finite and K arbitrary, when the cell values have K-coordinates in a finite set:

the plane theorem relative to finitely many layers, as `cut_prod_layers` is `cut_of_end_finite` relative to finitely many layers. The mixed signatures need G1 several times at once, glued along slabs.

**4. What G1 has to handle (K = C₂).** Lowering and raising in the K-coordinate are legal for every value except those *stuck on both sides* (z₃ ≤ x₃ and y₃ ≤ z₃). By the layers (x₃, y₃) of the cell, these are:
- the two core regions: (0, 0) with z₃ = 0, and (1, 1) with z₃ = 1;
- the whole thin region (x₃, y₃) = (1, 0).

In each region the no-witness condition reduces to one on the projection to L₁ × L₂. Examples: at (1, 0), z ≤ x iff (z₁, z₂) ≤ (x₁, x₂), and y ≤ z iff (y₁, y₂) ≤ (z₁, z₂). So these are slice problems whose witnesses lift (the brief's face transfer holds exactly there). But the thin region's rows D̂₁ and columns L∖D̂₀ do not form a cut. A thin "cut" has no Cut lemma by itself: on C₃ with rows {0}, columns {2} and F(0, 2) = 1 there is no witness. So the slices cannot be settled one at a time. For each layer pair (k, k′) the stuck pairs sit in the box s_k ≤ x, y′ ≤ t_{k′}, with s_k = (⊥, ⊥, k) and t_{k′} = (⊤, ⊤, k′). There they obey the plane sandwich e ≤ z ≤ z′ ≤ r with e_{12} ≠ ⊥, r_{12} ≠ ⊤, and corner h(k, k′) = F(s_k, t_{k′}). So each of the finitely many layer pairs falls into one of the plane theorem's cases: box or strips, a pinned coordinate, or anti-diagonal. The open step is to choose one zone rule for all pairs at once. This is the brief's "cube consistency" (Program 2), made concrete.

**Programs 3–4** (box separators, common guides for general chain-finite lattices) need a well-founded refinement measure that the brief does not supply. Not pursued.

**Next target:** G1 for K = C₂, i.e. Ω × Ω × C₂, then G1 in general, then Ω³ by gluing slabs.

## 34.3z⁵ G1: the layer pass, and what is left (anti-diagonal boxes)

Work towards G1 (§34.3z⁗): Cut on Π × K, with Π = L₁ × L₂ end-finite and the values' K-coordinates in a finite set T.

**1. The layer pass** (`lean/Mordor/KPass.lean`, `KPass.kpass`, standard axioms; any complete Π, K). Call a value z at (x, y) *K-lowerable* if z₂ ≰ x₂ in the layer coordinate, and *K-raisable* if y₂ ≰ z₂.
- **The pass.** Lower to (⊥, z₂) every cell all of whose lower cells carry K-lowerable values. Raise to (⊤, z₂) every remaining cell all of whose upper cells carry K-raisable values. Keep the rest.
- **What it gives.** The result is monotone and witness-free on cells, so the zone lemma is not needed for this step. Every kept value z lies in a **plane sandwich** e ≤ z ≤ r, e ≤ h ≤ r, with e_Π ≠ ⊥ and r_Π ≠ ⊤, around a **base corner** h = F((⊥, k), (⊤, k′)), where k, k′ are layers of cell values.
- **Why.** A kept cell has a stuck-lo cell below it and a stuck-hi cell above it; their anchors give e and r.

So all the infinitely many values that remain sit around finitely many base corners, as all values of the plane theorem sat around the single corner F(⊥, ⊤). When T is a chain, choosing k as the largest stuck-lo layer below the cell and k′ as the smallest stuck-hi layer above it makes the corner a **monotone function of the cell**.

**2. One layer.** |T| = 1 is a slice. With all values in layer t:
- if (⊥, t) ∉ D or (⊤, t) ∈ D there is a trivial witness;
- otherwise F̂(p, q) = π F((p, t), (q, t)) on Π with the cut D̂_t has a witness by `cut_prod_end_finite`, and it lifts because the layer is pinned.

**3. No anti-diagonal corner: G1 holds (hand argument, not formalized).** Classify each kept value by the Π-class of its base corner h. The classes (⊥, ⊥) and (⊤, ⊤) are impossible, because e_Π ≠ ⊥ and r_Π ≠ ⊤.
- **h₁, h₂ both middle:** as in the plane theorem, the value lies in a finite box, or has z₂ in a finite set of middle elements, or z₁ in one.
- **h = (⊥, middle):** e₁ = ⊥ forces e₂ ≠ ⊥, so z₂ ∈ Lo₂(h₂). This set avoids ⊥ but may contain ⊤.
- **h = (⊤, middle):** r₁ = ⊤ forces r₂ ≠ ⊤, so z₂ ∈ Hi₂(h₂). This set avoids ⊤ but may contain ⊥.
- **h = (middle, ⊥) or (middle, ⊤):** symmetric, giving vertical families.

Now run one zone pass with two types:
- horizontal values: lower to (⊥, z₂, z₃) ⊔ guides, with corner lemma fibre L₁;
- vertical values: lower to (z₁, ⊥, z₃) ⊔ guides, with corner lemma fibre L₂.

Mixed comparable pairs are handled as follows.
- **Pairs caught by a finite set.** A horizontal z ≤ a vertical z′ puts z in ↓T₁ × T₂ × T when z′₁ ≠ ⊤, and z′ in T₁ × ↑T₂ × T when z₂ ≠ ⊥. Both sets are finite and join the guides. The case vertical ≤ horizontal is symmetric.
- **The leftover pairs.** These are z₂ = ⊥ below z′₁ = ⊤, and dually z₁ = ⊥ below z′₂ = ⊤. For them, lowering and raising are monotone automatically: (⊥, ⊥, ·) ⊔ g ≤ (⊤, ⊥, ·) ⊔ g′, and (⊤, ⊥, ·) ⊓ g ≤ (⊤, ⊤, ·) ⊓ g′.
- **Their stuck pairs.** These sit around the base corner G((⊥, ⊥, z₃), (⊤, ⊤, z′₃)), where z₂ = ⊥ forces e₁ ≠ ⊥ and z′₁ = ⊤ forces r₂ ≠ ⊤. That corner is not anti-diagonal, so either h₁ ≠ ⊤, which puts z in the finite set Lo₁(h₁) × {⊥} × T, or h₂ ≠ ⊥, which puts z′ in the finite set {⊤} × Hi₂(h₂) × T. Both sets are guides.

So the zone lemma applies and the values become finite.

**4. What is left: anti-diagonal boxes.** Suppose T is a chain, so the corner map is monotone. The cells with corner class (⊥, ⊤) then form a convex set A. Below A there are only (⊥, middle) cells, carrying horizontal values; above A only (middle, ⊤) cells, carrying vertical values. Dually for (⊤, ⊥). Inside A, the plane theorem would collapse one coordinate globally:
- collapsing coordinate 1 (keep z₂, floor z₁) is compatible with the horizontal values below A, but not with the vertical values above it;
- collapsing coordinate 2 is the reverse.

The plane theorem's anchor (⊥, ⊤) becomes the layered anchor a = (⊥, ⊤, k), which can **escape in the layer**. If a ∈ D, M = F(a, (⊤, ⊤, k′)) ≥ h has M₂ = ⊤. But witness-freeness only gives M₁ ≠ ⊤ *or* k′ ≰ M₃. In the second case M = (⊤, ⊤, m₃) with m₃ < k′, and no coordinate is bounded. Such an escape points to the smaller layer pair (k, m₃). This suggests recursion on layer pairs, well-founded because T is finite, but it is not yet made into a proof.

**5. Layer merging: Cut(Q) ⟹ Cut(Q × C₂), except for thick cuts** (`lean/Mordor/Merge.lean`, standard axioms). Q is *any* complete lattice on which Cut holds (`Merge.CutHolds`), and C₂ = `Fin 2`. Write D̂_i = {p : (p, i) ∈ D}, so D̂₁ ⊆ D̂₀.
- **`cut_prod_two_of_eq`** (D̂₀ = D̂₁). Ĝ(p, q) = π F((p, 1), (q, 0)) on the cut D̂₀. A witness for Ĝ lifts directly, because the row sits in layer 1 and the column in layer 0.
- **`cut_prod_two_of_bot`** ((⊥, 1) ∉ D, so D̂₁ = ∅). Let A = F((p, 0), (q, 0)). Read the layer-0 rows against the layer-0 columns on the cut D̂₀: Ĝ = π_A where A sits in layer 0, and Ĝ = π_A ⊔ π F((p, 0), (⊥, 1)) where it sits in layer 1. This is monotone, because the layer-0 region is closed downward.
  - The value at the column anchor (⊥, 1) must sit in layer 0 and escape its row, so Ĝ ≰ p always holds where A is in layer 1.
  - A witness q ≤ Ĝ there lifts to the layer-1 column (q, 1), whose value lies above both A and the anchor value and sits in layer 1.
- **`cut_prod_two_of_top`** ((⊤, 0) ∈ D): the dual, with layer-1 rows, a meet, and the row anchor (⊤, 0).

What remains is exactly the **thick cut**: (⊥, 1) ∈ D and (⊤, 0) ∉ D, with a nonempty gap D̂₀ ∖ D̂₁. This is the configuration that L × C₄ had in §34.3z′, now with an arbitrary Cut lattice Q. There the corner lemma closed it for end-finite L.

On the cut D̂₀, merging reads gap rows only at layer 0. A Cut-witness for Q that does not lift is then a *near-witness*: a gap element p and a column q ∉ D̂₀ with F((p, 0), (q, 0)) ≤ (p, 1). The value lies below the layer-1 copy of its row, which is a column rather than a row. Dually on the cut D̂₁. Turning near-witnesses into witnesses is the join-closure gap of §34.4a in miniature.

A general product theorem Cut(Q) ⟹ Cut(Q × C₂) would follow from the standing conjecture (by Theorem A, Cut(Q) forces Q to be chain-finite), so it is a fair target. For Q = L₁ × L₂ it is G1 with two layers.

**6. The thick cut on L₁ × L₂ × C₂: reduction to layer escapes** (hand analysis, not formalized). Write s₁ = (⊥, ⊥, 1) ∈ D and t₀ = (⊤, ⊤, 0) ∉ D.
- **Anchor pass.** For a cell (x, y), take the row anchor s_{x₂} (with s₀ = ⊥) and the column anchor t_{y₂} (with t₁ = ⊤). The sandwich F(s_{x₂}, y) ≤ z ≤ F(x, t_{y₂}) fails to be proper (Π-part ≠ ⊥ below and ≠ ⊤ above) only at a **layer escape**: x₂ = 0 and F(⊥, y) = (⊥, ⊥, 1), or y₂ = 1 and F(x, ⊤) = (⊤, ⊤, 0).
- **What the pass does.** The first set of cells is closed downward and the second upward, and the two never meet in a comparable pair. Replacing their values by the constants (⊥, ⊥, 1) and (⊤, ⊤, 0) is monotone and witness-free.
- **What remains.** Every other value sits in a proper sandwich around one of four corners c_{ij} = F(s_i, t_j), one per region (row layer i, column layer j), with c₀₀ ≤ c₀₁, c₁₀ ≤ c₁₁.

**Moves that carry over from the plane theorem.**
- *Layers.* Fibre L₁ with layers (z₂, z₃), or fibre L₂ with layers (z₁, z₃): `cut_prod_layers` as is.
- *Cross with layers.* As in item 3, provided no region corner is anti-diagonal.
- *Global collapses.* Any (⊥, ⊤)-anti-diagonal region corner forces c₀₀'s first coordinate to be ⊥ and c₁₁'s second to be ⊤. That leaves two candidate collapses:
  - collapse of coordinate 1 under the bound M = F((⊥, ⊤, 1), ⊤), when (⊥, ⊤, 1) ∈ D. Here M₂ = ⊤, so M₁ ≠ ⊤ unless M = (⊤, ⊤, 0);
  - collapse of coordinate 2 (dual form) under N = F(⊥, (⊥, ⊤, 0)), when (⊥, ⊤, 0) ∉ D. Here N₁ = ⊥, so N₂ ≠ ⊥ unless N = (⊥, ⊥, 1).

  The other two collapse forms fail outright.

**Residue.** Up to duality, the configurations that survive are:
- **(A)** (⊥, ⊤, 1) ∈ D, and the anchor value M = F((⊥, ⊤, 1), ⊤) escapes to (⊤, ⊤, 0). Then every row with first coordinate ⊥ produces only layer-0 values.
- **(C)** The anti-diagonal anchor lies in the gap: (⊥, ⊤, 0) ∈ D but (⊥, ⊤, 1) ∉ D. Then collapse of coordinate 1 is bounded on layer-0 rows and collapse of coordinate 2 on layer-1 columns. Every stuck pair (z ≤ z′) has z₃ = 0 or z′₃ = 1, because z₃ ≤ z′₃. But the two collapse maps are not monotone against each other across layers.
- **(A–B)** Anti-diagonal corners of both types, c₀₁ ~ (⊥, ⊤) and c₁₀ ~ (⊤, ⊥). Then c₀₀ = (⊥, ⊥, 1) and c₁₁ = (⊤, ⊤, 0) are themselves layer escapes, and only the two mixed regions carry values.

All three are **layer escapes at anchor values**: the Π-part reaches ⊥ or ⊤ while the layer coordinate jumps. That is what the plane theorem never had to face.

**7. One move for everything: the generalized collapse** (hand argument; solver-checked case analysis). Collapse coordinate 1 with *floor sets that depend on the value's layer*: A⁰ ⊆ A¹ for lowering to (⌊z₁⌋_{A^{z₃}}, z₂, z₃), and *separate* ceiling sets B⁰ ⊇ B¹ for raising to (⌈z₁⌉_{B^{z₃}}, z₂, z₃). Both maps are monotone because A grows and B shrinks with the layer.

Run the zone lemma with no guides. A stuck pair z ≤ z′ has (z₂, z₃) ≤ (x₂, x₃) below and (y′₂, y′₃) ≤ (z′₂, z′₃) above. It cannot occur if

  **(G-C1)** z₁ ∈ A^{z₃} or z′₁ ∈ B^{z′₃}.

The reason: then lowering fixes z, so z ≤ x is a witness; or raising fixes z′, so y′ ≤ z′ is a witness. Afterwards coordinate 1 takes finitely many values, and `cut_prod_layers` with fibre L₂ finishes. G-C2 is the same with the coordinates exchanged.

Each stuck pair can be discharged from either side and in any of three ways:
- its own finite coordinate (a vertical or finite value);
- the `Layers` corner, when both values are horizontal;
- a layered anchor bound, depending only on its layer pair (k, k′):
  - above: (⊥, ⊤, k) ∈ D and F((⊥, ⊤, k), (⊤, ⊤, k′))₁ ≠ ⊤;
  - below: (⊤, ⊥, k′) ∉ D and F((⊥, ⊥, k), (⊤, ⊥, k′))₁ ≠ ⊥.

So the layers no longer have to agree on one global anchor.

**Check** (`experiments/products/layered_collapse.py`, `run2`). Abstract anchor tables are built over {⊥, ⊤}² × layers. Each anchor value is recorded by the class of each coordinate (⊥, middle, ⊤) and its layer, subject to monotonicity and no witness at anchor cells. Value types come from the region corners of the anchor pass. The solver asks for tables where both G-C1 and G-C2 leave a stuck pair undischarged.
- **One layer:** none. The generalized collapse alone reproduces the whole plane theorem, i.e. the layers, cross and collapse cases of §34.3z‴.
- **Two layers, thick cuts:** 9 anchor cut types remain.

Every remaining failure is a **layer escape at an anchor value**. For example, D ∩ anchors = {⊥, (⊥, ⊥, 1)}, with F(⊥, (⊤, ⊥, 0)) = (⊥, ⊥, 1) and F(⊥, (⊥, ⊤, 0)) = (⊤, ⊥, 0). There, columns with first coordinate ⊤ give only layer-1 values, columns with second coordinate ⊤ give only first coordinate ⊤, and the undischarged pairs sit at cells with no ⊤ coordinates.

*Formalized:* `lean/Mordor/GenCollapse.lean`, `GenCollapse.gen_collapse` (standard axioms). It works for any complete β, K, a monotone label K → Λ, floor sets that grow with the label and ceiling sets that shrink with it. Under the stuck-pair condition it gives a monotone, witness-free bifunction that keeps the second coordinates and has floors or ceilings as first coordinates.

*A finite test that does not decide* (`experiments/products/escape_trunc.py`). Truncate M_ω to N atoms, and let values also use one extra atom standing for all the others. The map a_j ↦ o is monotone and preserves every comparison with truncated coordinates, so UNSAT would prove Cut for the cut. But the test is SAT for every N, even for the plane cut, where Cut holds. Witnesses can always hide beyond the truncation, so a single escape atom gives no information.

*Shift-equivariant ansatz* (`experiments/products/shift_ansatz.py`). On M_ℤ × M_ℤ (× C₂), with the shift automorphism a_i ↦ a_{i+1}, the SAT search for an equivariant witness-free F is UNSAT already at window 1, both for the plane cut and for the residue-1 thick cut. The reason is simple: equivariance forces anchor cells (no atom coordinates) to take shift-invariant values, i.e. values in {⊥, ⊤}² × C₂, and Cut on that finite lattice already gives a witness. So a counterexample must break shift-symmetry at the anchors, which is exactly what the residue patterns do (anchor values in branches or layer escapes). Equivariant constructions are ruled out, and the experiment says nothing about non-equivariant ones.

*Reach reformulation of the thick case.* By `cutMaps_iff_R`, a thick counterexample is a proper closed set R (closed under a and b⁻¹) of Q × C₂ with ∅ ≠ R₁ ⊊ R₀. Write G_{ij}(p, q) = π F((p, i), (q, j)) for the four slices. Fixed-point comparisons give three inclusions:
- a_{G₀₀}(R₀) ⊆ R₀;
- b_{G₁₁}⁻¹(R₁) ⊆ R₁;
- a_{G₁₀}(R₁) ⊆ R₀ and b_{G₁₀}⁻¹(R₁) ⊆ R₀.

No single slice is closed on one set, so Cut(Q) cannot be applied to a slice directly. `Merge` repairs this at the level of cells, using the anchor (⊥, 1) or (⊤, 0). The thick case is where neither anchor exists.

*Tenth outside brief* (against `40debbf`). It made three proposals.
- **Cell-labelled collapse.** Now proved as `GenCollapse.gen_collapse_cell` (standard axioms): floor and ceiling sets chosen by a label of the *cell*, monotone on comparable active cells. It does not reach the residue, however. The discharge sets come from anchor bounds, and those depend only on the value layers of the pair. Moreover, a finite label set cannot separate generic cells (no ⊤ coordinates), which all see the same anchors. Every residue pair sits at generic cells.
- **Thick-slice amalgamation.** The four slice inclusions together with G₀₀ ≤ G₀₁, G₁₀ ≤ G₁₁ (and the other pointwise orders) are *consistent* already on Q = C₃ (`experiments/products/slice_amalgam.py`). Example: R₁ = {0}, R₀ = {0, 1}, G₀₀ = G₀₁ ≡ 0, with G₁₀ and G₁₁ jumping to ⊤ on rows outside R₁. So no amalgamation can work from the slice inclusions alone. The contradiction has to use the layer bits of F at the cells, which is what `Merge` does with its anchors.
- **Escape-transition graph.** Not pursued. Its edges would be the anchor-level implications already enforced in `layered_collapse.py`, and the residue survives them.

*Counterexample search with a broken symmetry* (`experiments/products/shift_anchor_ansatz.py`). On M_ω × M_ω × C₂, call F *eventually shift-equivariant* if it commutes with the shift a_i ↦ a_{i+1} on every cell whose atom indices are all ≥ T, while cells with a smaller index, including all anchor cells, are free. Values may use atoms within a fixed offset of the cell's indices, and the free cells may use any atom in the window.
- The search is UNSAT as soon as the window exceeds T by two (T = 2, offset 1; T = 3, offset 2), for six thick cut types, including D̂₁ = {⊥} with D̂₀ = {no ⊤ coordinate}, and D̂₁ = {first coordinate ⊥}.
- It is SAT only for windows too small to contain a shifted cell together with its shift.
- The plane cut behaves the same way, as it must.

So a counterexample, if one exists, is not eventually periodic in this sense. It would have to break the shift symmetry at infinitely many scales. This is evidence, not proof, since the ansatz restricts the free cells to atoms inside the window.

*Cores and a canonical-Ramsey model* (`experiments/products/{core_thick, canonical}.py`).
- **Cores.** The minimal UNSAT cores of the eventually-equivariant search use mostly cells over the fixed anchor atom (coordinates in {⊥, a₀, ⊤}), plus a few cells over one shifted atom whose equivariance limits where the anchor values can escape. That is the ansatz at work, not a general mechanism.
- **Canonical model.** By the Erdős–Rado canonical Ramsey theorem, a counterexample on M_ω × M_ω × C₂ restricts, on an infinite set S of atoms, to a canonical one. There each value coordinate is ⊥, ⊤, a fixed outside atom, a cell atom, or a fresh atom determined injectively by a sub-tuple. Merging all fresh generators into one and all constants into one only adds equalities, which never hurts monotonicity, so the canonical problem is finite and exact. UNSAT would prove Cut.
- **Result.** The model is SAT even for the plane cut, where Cut is a theorem. Restricting to cells over S lets the witnesses sit at cells whose coordinates are fresh atoms.

Like the single escape atom, this confirms the pattern. Every finite or locally canonical abstraction can push witnesses onto new atoms. The proofs that work (end-finite, plane, layers) get finiteness globally, from corner bounds. For the thick residue, no corner bound exists.

**Next:** settle residues (A) and (C), or find their common mechanism. Both involve an anchor value whose Π-part is extremal while its layer jumps, which is the situation `Merge` exploits when the anchor is a column of layer 1.

## 34.3z⁶ Eleventh outside brief: generated supports, and a depth-2 closure conjecture

The eleventh brief (ChatGPT, written against c76b874) proposes three programs.
1. Closed-support canonicalization.
2. Counterexample-guided refinement of the canonical model.
3. Formalizing the thick-cut anchor pass, then repairing near-witnesses residue by residue.

**Program 1, the transfer lemma, is correct and needs no closure.**
- On M_ω, any set S of atoms gives a complete sublattice M_S.
- The retraction φ_S fixes S ∪ {⊥, ⊤} and sends every other atom to one new atom o. It preserves monotonicity, since atom-to-atom maps are monotone on M_ω.
- It also preserves witness-freeness: o ≤ x_i holds iff x_i = ⊤, which holds iff v ≤ x_i, for any atom v ∉ S and any x_i ∈ M_S.
- So a counterexample on M_ω² × C_n restricts to cells over any S, with values in M_S ∪ {o}. On the infinite (⊓, ⊔, F)-closed support this is §34.3o's reduction.
- The content is therefore not closedness. It is what the frontier symbol o is allowed to do, and that has a finitary form.

**Generation levels** (`experiments/products/depth_support_gen.py`, SAT, D a solver variable).
- Put ⊥ and ⊤ at level 0. L₁ is the set of atoms occurring in values at cells over level-0 points. L_{k+1} is the set of new atoms in values at cells whose coordinates have level ≤ k.
- In a counterexample every L_k is finite.
- Its restriction to cells over L₁ ∪ … ∪ L_d, with all other value atoms sent to o, is a model of the depth-d instance (|L₁|, …, |L_d|):
  - cells of level < d take values in their own levels plus one;
  - only cells over L_d may use o.
- So: **if every depth-d instance is UNSAT, Cut holds.** The tested sizes are finitely many, so the results are evidence, not proof.

| lattice | depth 1 | depth 2 | depth 3 | depth 4 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| M_ω, M_ω × C₂, M_ω × C₃ | UNSAT (≤ 6 atoms) | — | — | — |
| M_ω² (plane) | SAT | UNSAT, 15 sizes up to (6, 2), (4, 4) | | |
| M_ω² × C₂ | SAT | UNSAT, all sizes up to (4, 2), (2, 4), (3, 3)⁺ | | |
| M_ω² × C₃ | SAT | UNSAT up to (2, 2) | | |
| (2-chains)² and (2-chains)² × C₂ | SAT | UNSAT up to (2, 1), (1, 2) | | |
| M_ω³ | SAT | SAT up to (2, 2) | SAT (1,1,1), (2,1,1), (1,2,1) | UNSAT (1,1,1,1)⁺ |

⁺ further sizes are listed at the end of this section. "(2-chains)" is the horizontal sum of 2-element chains, the Ω-like test case.

Sanity checks:
- With either half of the witness condition dropped, depth 2 is SAT.
- Allowing o at level-1 cells (depth 1 plus spare atoms) is SAT.
- So exact closure of level 1 is what forces the witness.

**Reading.**
- **Depth 1 is SAT, even for the plane.** No contradiction follows from the cells over {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ alone when values there may be fresh. That is all an anchor argument sees:
  - the anchors are level-0 cells;
  - their values are L₁;
  - layered collapse and the KPass sandwiches constrain level-1 cells.

  This is the precise sense in which every anchor-based bound of §34.3z⁵ had to fail on the thick residues. A proof must use cells over the atoms that level-1 values introduce. The plane proof does exactly that: its guides are the corners F((⊥, k), (⊤, k′)) with k ∈ L₁, i.e. level-1 cells, and their atoms are level 2.
- **Depth 2 is UNSAT, uniformly, for two M_ω-factors and any finite layer chain.**

> **Conjecture DC2.** For every n and every k, m: no witness-free (F, D) on cells over ({⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ ∪ L₂)² × C_n has values at cells over L₁ in L₁ ∪ L₂ and values at cells over {⊥, ⊤} in L₁. Equivalently: every cut of M_ω² × C_n has a witness at a cell of generation depth ≤ 2.

DC2 implies Cut, hence CL, on M_ω² × C_n, including the thick cuts. It is a finitary statement. A proof would be a plane-style guide argument with guides taken from level-1 cells, in every layer pair.

**Which level-1 cells must be closed (Program 2, sound version).**
- Keep level 0 fully closed.
- At level 1, closing only the *fibre cells* suffices for UNSAT, at every size tested ((1,1), (2,2) for n = 1, 2; (3,2) for n = 2):
  - cells with at most one atom in total;
  - cells whose two points carry the same atom at the same coordinate, with the other coordinates in {⊥, ⊤}.
- Closing only one-atom cells, or pairs with the atoms at different coordinates, is SAT.
- Relaxing level 0 instead is not a sound test. An open level-0 cell may carry L₂ atoms, and an early run that forgot this gave spurious UNSATs.
- **Single-atom edge closure fails** (`edge_closure.py`). Fix one atom a, close the one-coordinate edge cells over {⊥, a, ⊤}, and let all other cells use L₂ ∪ {o}. This is SAT for every m ≥ 1, already on the plane.
- So the witness is not forced locally around one atom. It needs L₁ closed as a whole.

**M_ω³ needs more depth.**
- Depths 2 and 3 are SAT at the sizes tried.
- Depth 4 with one atom per level is UNSAT.
- An eventually shift-equivariant M_ω³ (σ a_l = a_{l+1} above a threshold T; F and D commute with σ on cells meeting level ≥ T) is UNSAT on windows (T, d) = (2, 3), (2, 4), (3, 4). So no periodic counterexample shows up in M_ω³ either.
- If the pattern holds, the depth needed grows with the number of M_ω factors (1, 2, 4?). That matches the reduction of Ω³ to a plane relative to finitely many layers (§34.3z⁗): each extra infinite factor costs at least one more level of guides.

**Program 3 (anchor pass in Lean)** is not done this round.
- The value-layer pass is already `KPass.kpass`; the cell-layer version remains hand-argued (§34.3z⁵ item 6).
- The depth-1 result says this pass cannot close the thick cut on its own. It is worth formalizing only together with a level-2 guide family.

Further sizes: M_ω² × C₂ at depth 2 is also UNSAT at (5, 2) and (3, 4).

**Next:** prove DC2 for the plane, by hand or in Lean, as a depth-2 restatement of `Plane.lean`. Then add layers. The fibre-cell result says which level-1 values must serve as guides.

## 34.3z⁷ DC2 as a terminating check: blocks, bounds, and where it stops scaling

§34.3z⁶ tested DC2 at sample sizes. To verify it outright, each |L₁| = k needs one finite check with |L₂| up to a proved bound. This section builds that check (`experiments/dc2/`).

**Blocks (`dc2_eager.py`, with the lazy variant `dc2_lazy.py`).**
- The master problem holds D on points over S₁ = {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ and F on all pairs over S₁:
  - level-0 values lie in {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁;
  - level-1 values lie in {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ ∪ L₂;
  - every L₁ atom occurs at a level-0 cell;
  - L₂ is a pool, and unused atoms are allowed.
- Each L₂ atom b gets a block. It holds the pairs over S₁ ∪ {b} that involve b, and their D-points. Values there lie in {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ ∪ {b, o}. Master values enter projected by π_b, which sends every other L₂ atom to o. Every block clause is guarded by "b is used at a cell".
- Pairs that mention two distinct L₂ atoms are dropped, which is a relaxation.
- *Soundness.* Take a counterexample with |L₁| = k and |L₂| ≤ m.
  - Value non-cell pairs at ⊥. This gives a master model.
  - Its restriction to cells over S₁ ∪ {b}, composed with π_b, is a block model. π_b maps atoms to atoms, and o ≤ x_i ⟺ x_i = ⊤ ⟺ v ≤ x_i for every block coordinate x_i and every v ∉ L₁ ∪ {b}.
  - Value-precedence symmetry breaking on L₁ and on L₂ is sound. Rename atoms by first occurrence, after sending atoms outside L₂ to o.
  - So UNSAT at (k, m) excludes every counterexample with |L₁| = k and |L₂| ≤ m.
- Sanity checks:
  - Dropping either half of the witness condition gives SAT.
  - Skipping the blocks gives SAT. That is depth 1.
- *Fibre closure.* With level 0 fully closed, only the level-1 fibre cells of §34.3z⁶ need to be closed. Other level-1 cells get o, and L₂ is defined at fibre cells only. This shrinks the |L₂| bound.

**Bounds (`bounds.py`, `plane_full.py`).**
- |L₁| is bounded by the depth-0 relaxation: level-0 cells with values from a value-only pool.
- |L₂| ≤ 2 · width of the closed level-1 pairs, taken over all D at once. In M_ω a monotone chain of values meets at most one atom per coordinate.

Two solvers do the check.
- `dc2_eager.py` builds one block copy per pool atom. It is too slow beyond plane k = 2: k = 3 at m = 48 ran for 100 minutes without an answer.
- `dc2_lazy.py` adds a block's minimized core as a lemma quantified over every b, which is sound for the same reason. It agrees with the eager solver wherever both finish, and passes the same sanity checks.

|L₁| has two sound bounds:
- the depth-0 SAT maximum, which is 3 on the plane and at least 13 on M_ω² × C₂ (the run did not finish);
- 2 · the width of the level-0 cell poset for each fixed level-0 pattern of D, which is 4 on the plane and 20 on M_ω² × C₂, thick cuts included.

| lattice | k | \|L₂\| bound (fibre) | result (lazy, minimized cores) |
|---|---|---|---|
| M_ω² | 0 | 0 | UNSAT |
| | 1 | 16 | UNSAT, 33 iterations |
| | 2 | 32 | UNSAT, 42 iterations, 2 s |
| | 3 | 48 | UNSAT, 7 iterations, 4 s |
| M_ω² × C₂ | 1 | 60 | UNSAT, 1,812 iterations, 129 s |
| | 2 | 120 | undecided: about 18,000 lemma families in 7,000 s, cores still about 10 facts |

**Reading.**
- **On the plane DC2 is verified exhaustively.** Every k ≤ 3 = the |L₁| bound is UNSAT at its |L₂| bound. This gives a finite, solver-checked certificate that every cut of M_ω² has a witness at generation depth ≤ 2. Two parts are unformalized: the encoding and the restriction argument above.
- **Shape of the lemmas.** Minimized block cores have 6–10 facts, and each is a sandwich. A typical one, with x = (a, ⊤) ∈ D:
  - b sits in coordinate 1 at the cells ((a, ⊥), ⊤) and ((a, ⊤), ⊤);
  - coordinate 0 is pinned to a through the (⊥, ⊤) and (⊤, ⊥) rows;
  - so the block cell ((a, b), ⊤) has its value squeezed below (a, b), which is a witness.

  A hand proof of DC2 would be a classification of these fresh-atom sandwiches.
- Plane Cut is a theorem (`Plane.lean`), so this calibrates the pipeline end to end: blocks, projection, symmetry breaking, bounds.
- For M_ω² × C₂ the lazy check settles k = 1. It does not scale to the |L₁| bound of 20: master size grows like (k + 2)⁴, and the |L₂| bound like 60k. The reason is that the depth-0 relaxation lets every level-0 cell carry fresh atoms:
  - |L₁| reaches 13 over all cuts;
  - restricted to thick cuts, |L₁| reaches 6–9 for each level-0 pattern of D on (⊥, ⊤, l) and (⊤, ⊥, l) computed so far;
  - the fibre bound on |L₂| grows like 60k.
- Relaxing level-0 closure to the diagonal corners (⊥⊥l, ⊤⊤l′) keeps the plane UNSAT, but makes M_ω² × C₂ SAT. Here this is done soundly: open level-0 cells get {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ ∪ L₂ ∪ {o}. A minimal set of closed level-0 cells still has 18 of the level-0 pairs.
- So with layers, the information that forces a witness is spread over almost all level-0 cells. That matches the three thick residues of §34.3z⁵, which sit at different anchor cells.

**Lemma generality is the bottleneck at k = 2.** Each lemma is quantified over the L₂ atom b, but not over permutations of L₁. It names specific master cells, so lemma families multiply with the number of master cells. Two things would speed this up: quantifying lemmas over L₁ permutations, and lifting a core to a pattern over cell *shapes*, which is what the plane cores visibly are. The second is also the route to a hand proof.

**What would make it finish.** A normalization that cuts down the fresh atoms at level-0 cells before the restriction. `KPass` is the candidate, since it collapses every cell whose layer already escapes to (⊥_Q, l) or (⊤_Q, l). Its collapse condition, however, quantifies over all cells below, and that condition is not visible in the restriction. A version with a local condition, i.e. one depending only on cells over S, would add the constraints the check needs.

## 34.3z⁸ M_ω² × C₂, pattern by pattern: narrowed closures and a case split

§34.3z⁷ stalled because |L₁| can reach 20 when every level-0 cell is closed. Two observations make the check finish. Both are choices of which cells to close. Any choice is sound (L₁ and L₂ are *defined* as the atoms at the closed cells; every other cell of level ≤ 1 gets {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ ∪ L₂ ∪ {o}). The choice only decides which finite instances must be checked.

**Reduction to thick cuts.** `Merge.cut_prod_two_of_bot`, `cut_prod_two_of_top` and `cut_prod_two_of_eq` (Lean) give Cut(Q × C₂) from Cut(Q) unless (⊥_Q, 1) ∈ D and (⊤_Q, 0) ∉ D. For Q = M_ω², Cut(Q) is `Plane.cut_prod_end_finite`. So only thick level-0 patterns remain. These are the memberships of (⊥, ⊤, l) and (⊤, ⊥, l) in D, written as a 4-bit tag p₀p₁q₀q₁. Downset closure leaves 3 × 3 = 9 patterns. Swapping the two M_ω coordinates exchanges p and q, so 1000 ≅ 0010 and 1110 ≅ 1011.

**Narrowing (`chain_search.py`, `chain3.py`, `core_sample.py`, `narrow.py`).**
- *Level 0.* For each pattern, choose a closed set C₀ of level-0 cells of small width w. Candidates are maximal chains, unions of chains, and randomized minimal selector cores. |L₁| ≤ 2w, since along a chain of cells each value coordinate meets at most one atom.
- *Level 1.* Selector cores at small sizes give a few fibre-cell *shapes* in a generic atom a. Examples are ((⊥, a, 0), (⊤, ⊤, 0)) and ((⊤, ⊥, 1), (⊤, a, l)). Each shape is instantiated for every L₁ atom. |L₂| ≤ 2 · width of the instantiated cells. That is 6 per atom for pattern 0011, against 60 for all fibre cells.
- *Case split (pattern 1010).* Let C₀ᴬ be a closed set that fails only at k = 0, where no C₀ᴬ cell carries an atom. Split on the counterexample:
  - Case I: some C₀ᴬ cell carries an atom. Check k = 1, …, 2·width(C₀ᴬ).
  - Case II: every C₀ᴬ cell is atom-free. Restrict those cells to {⊥, ⊤} (`ZERO0`) and close a second set C₀ᴮ.

| pattern | closed level-0 cells (width) | level-1 shapes | k = \|L₁\| checked | result |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0011 | 5 (1) | 7 | 0–2 (m ≤ 12) | **UNSAT, complete** |
| 1100 | 5 (1) | 7 | 0–2 (m ≤ 12) | **UNSAT, complete** |
| 0000 | 7 (2) | 14 | 0–4 (m ≤ 48) | **UNSAT, complete** |
| 1111 | 7 (2) | 14 | 0–4 (m ≤ 64) | **UNSAT, complete** |
| 0010 (≅ 1000) | 9 (3) | 12 | 0–6 | **UNSAT, complete** (k = 5: 300 iterations, 37 min); k = 6 is refuted by the master alone |
| 1011 (≅ 1110) | 9 (3) | 11 | 0–6 | **UNSAT, complete**; k = 6 is refuted by the master alone |
| 1010, case II | C₀ᴬ (6) atom-free; C₀ᴮ: 2 (2) | 0 | 0–4 | **UNSAT, complete**, with no level-1 closure needed |
| 1010, case I | C₀ᴬ: 6 (4) | 16 | 1–8 | **UNSAT, complete** (k = 4: 1,743 lemma families, 85 min; k = 5: 1,896, 3.4 h; k = 6: 1,150, 3.3 h); k = 7, 8 refuted by the master alone (L₂ merged) |

Blocks restricted to pairs with at most one L₁ atom (`BLOCK_MAXL1 = 1`) are a further sound relaxation. They give the same verdicts at about 25% less time, and block size becomes linear in k. A master-only refutation of some k may merge all L₂ atoms into one (m = 1). Without blocks, L₂ atoms are never coordinates, so the merge is sound. Lemma counts fall with k. For 0000 they are 727, 923, 398 and 139 for k = 1–4. Large L₁ over-constrains the master rather than enlarging the search.

**Status.** Every row is closed. By the restriction argument, every cut of M_ω² × C₂ has a witness at generation depth ≤ 2. Non-thick cuts are handled by `Merge` and `Plane`; thick cuts by the nine patterns above. The result rests on the pen-and-paper restriction argument of §34.3z⁶–⁷ and on the encoders, which are tested but not formalized.

**What this does and does not add.** M_ω² × C₂ has edge-height 5, witnessed by the chain ⊥ < (a, ⊥, 0) < (⊤, ⊥, 0) < (⊤, a, 0) < (⊤, ⊤, 0) < (⊤, ⊤, 1). So CL on it is already the theorem `cl_height5` (§34.3h). The present check is an independent re-derivation by a method that never uses height. That independence is the point: the open target Ω × Ω × C₂ has infinite height. On a known case, the run confirms:
- that generation depth 2 suffices;
- that the narrowing and case-split machinery terminates.

It does not settle anything new about CL.

**Cross-checks.** The eager encoding agrees with the lazy solver on every instance where both finish. Those are 0011 at k = 1, 2; 0000 at k = 1; and k = 1 of 1011, of 1010 case I and of 1010 case II. Both are UNSAT in every case. Patterns 1000 and 1110 follow from 0010 and 1011 by the lattice automorphism (u, v, l) ↦ (v, u, l) of M_ω² × C₂, which conjugates F and maps the cut accordingly.

**Towards Ω × Ω × C₂.** Any subset of Ω that contains ⊥ and ⊤ is a sublattice, since incomparable elements meet at ⊥ and join at ⊤. So the restriction step carries over. The retraction must send an element outside S to a gap symbol inside its own chain, between the neighbouring S-elements, and then o ≤ x_i ⟺ v ≤ x_i holds again. The instance lattice is a finite horizontal sum of chains. L₁ and L₂ elements may share chains, so the master must carry the chain relations among pool elements. That is a different encoding, not a parameter change.

A Lean certificate would need a checked format such as LRAT proofs of the final master instances, together with a formal statement of the restriction lemma.

## 34.3z⁹ Generation depth on Ω: chains can be climbed forever

`experiments/omega/omega_direct.py` runs the depth test on Ω^dim × C_n with the exact chain retraction.
- The parameter is a *configuration*: the generated elements placed into chains, each chain a sequence of levels from bottom to top.
- S = {⊥, ⊤} ∪ L₁ ∪ … ∪ L_d is a sublattice, since incomparable elements of Ω meet at ⊥ and join at ⊤.
- An element outside S that lies on a chain meeting S goes to the gap symbol between its S-neighbours; any other element goes to o.
- The gap symbols compare with every S-coordinate exactly as the element did. So each instance is an exact image of a counterexample, with no fuzzy symbols, and UNSAT for all configurations would prove Cut.

Results, all on the plane Ω², where Cut is a Lean theorem (`Plane`):

| depth | configurations | result |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | one L₁ and one L₂ element, all 3 configurations | SAT; with gaps disabled, the separate-chain configuration is UNSAT, reproducing M_ω |
| 2 | two L₁ and one L₂ element, all 7 | SAT |
| 3 | one element per level, all 13 | SAT except the all-separate one, i.e. the M_ω pattern |
| 2, 3, 4, 5 | one chain, levels increasing or decreasing | SAT |

**Reading.**
- On Ω a witness-free partial map of any fixed generation depth exists. It climbs one chain: each level's values sit in the gap just beyond the previous level's element.
- What kills real counterexamples is that every chain of Ω is finite. That is a global property, which `Plane` uses through finite ideals and filters (the guide sets Lo and Hi).
- So generation-depth closure is the wrong invariant for Ω × Ω × C₂ and Ω³. It behaves like a height-sensitive method:
  - depth 1 suffices for M_ω and M_ω × C_n (edge-height ≤ 3);
  - depth 2 suffices for M_ω² × C_n (edge-height 4 + n − 1);
  - M_ω³ (edge-height 6) needs depth ≥ 4 at the sizes tried.

**Whole-chain closure removes the climb.** In Ω every proper principal ideal and filter is finite, so the chain through a generated element is a finite set. It may be put into S whole.
- Then every element of an L₁ chain is a level-1 coordinate whose cells are closed. L₂ (the new elements at those cells) lies on other chains, and whole L₂ chains are coordinates of level 2.
- No gap symbols occur, so climbing has to leave the chain. This is sound for each fixed chain length. In the code it is a configuration whose chains carry a single level, with gaps off.
- Results:

| lattice | configurations (chain lengths) | result |
|---|---|---|
| Ω² | L₁ chain 1–3 × L₂ chain 1–3, plus two-chain variants (13 configurations) | all UNSAT |
| Ω² × C₂ | 8 configurations, chains ≤ 3 | all UNSAT |
| Ω² × C₂ | L₁ chain of length 4, one L₂ element | UNSAT (11 min) |

**The open point is uniformity in chain length.** Ω has chains of every length, and every element of an L₁ chain brings its own closed cells. So a proof needs one of three things:
- an argument uniform in the chain length;
- a reduction to bounded chain length;
- a locality property of the UNSAT cores, using only a bounded window around the generated elements and the chain ends.

The gap abstraction is the natural window, but it fails (it is SAT, see above). So a window has to keep enough chain elements as closed coordinates to stop the climb, and its size cannot be fixed in advance by depth alone. This is where the end-finite argument of `Plane` (finite guide sets Lo, Hi) would have to enter.

**Where DC2 is still informative.** On lattices of finite height that are not covered by `cl_height5`. The first candidate is **M_ω² × C₃**, of edge-height 6. No Lean product theorem covers it: M_ω² and M_ω × C₃ are not end-finite. Yet DC2 was UNSAT at every size tried (§34.3z⁶). A complete check there would be a new case beyond edge-height 5.

## 34.3z¹⁰ Twelfth outside brief: bounded-shape certificates do not exist here; the certificate is a chain induction

The twelfth brief (ChatGPT, against 37e8029) asks for a proof uniform in chain length. It proposes three strategies:
1. classify the small "sandwich" cores into finitely many templates and prove an extraction theorem;
2. an exact chain summary built from `Lo`/`Hi`;
3. a uniform obstruction theorem.

It asks to test the bounded-shape hypothesis before building on it. One correction first: the 6–10-fact sandwich cores of §34.3z⁷ are *block lemmas* of the M_ω lazy solver. They are local obstructions for one fresh atom, not certificates for a whole instance. A whole M_ω² × C₂ certificate used 10³ of them.

**Bounded windows fail** (`experiments/omega/omega_window.py`). This is a sound abstraction of whole-chain closure, uniform in chain length:
- keep each generated element and k chain neighbours as closed coordinates;
- send every other element of a represented chain to a gap symbol, which is allowed wherever the real elements are (level ≥ 1 for L₁ chains, frontier for L₂ chains).

On Ω² with one L₁ and one L₂ element:
- L₁ windows up to k = 1 are SAT in every shape (end, neighbour, gap on either side).
- With the L₁ chain trivial, L₂ windows are UNSAT exactly when the L₂ chain has no gap: B alone, mB, Bm, mBm.
- Any gap on the L₂ chain gives SAT, whatever the neighbourhood (tested up to two neighbours on each side).

So no bounded neighbourhood of the generated elements suffices. The obstruction needs the end of the L₂ chain.

**Certificates grow linearly, with a uniform step.** Minimal witness-clause cores for whole-chain configurations E | B m^{N−1} (L₂ chain of length N, B at either end):

| N | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| core cells | 51 | 75 | 99 | 125 | 152 |

- Every element of the L₂ chain occurs in the core.
- Grouped by the highest chain position they touch, the cells split into 27 that avoid the chain and 21–29 per position.
- The middle block (N = 5, position 2) has 25 cells. They are built only from ⊥, ⊤, E and two consecutive chain elements c₋₁ < c. Examples: (⊥, c₋₁) → (⊥, c), (E, c₋₁) → (E, c), (c₋₁, E) → (c, ⊤), (c, ⊥) → (⊤, ⊤).
- The certificate is therefore a **base plus one fixed step per chain element**. That is an induction along the chain, not a finite template. This is the brief's "if this fails" branch: each additional level contributes the same dependency.

**The step is the chain-local escape of Strategy 2.** The sets of possible values at the cells over a middle element, over all models of the climbing (unbounded-above) window, show the invariant at the anchor rows. For instance:
- at ((⊤, c), (⊤, E)) the second coordinate is in {⊤, E} ∪ {elements strictly above c};
- at ((c, ⊤), (⊤, c)) the first coordinate is in {⊤, E} ∪ {strictly above c}.

The step pushes a value strictly beyond c, and only the end of the chain stops it. The finite principal filter ↑c is exactly the measure that `Lo`/`Hi` exploit in `Plane`.

**Plan that this supports.**
1. *Inductive invariant.* Find P(c) on the cells over {⊥, ⊤, E, c}, D-aware, such that P(c₋₁) together with the step cells forces P(c). Find a base P(c₀), and show that P(top) is contradictory. Each of the three is a finite SAT check on a window {⊥, ⊤, E, c₋₁, c, gap}. Together they prove the configuration family for every chain length.
2. *Generalize the configuration.* Allow several generated elements, L₁ chains of any length (a second induction), and two layers. The counts of generated elements are bounded per pattern as in §34.3z⁸. Only chain lengths need induction.
3. *Lean.* The step and termination lemmas are statements about monotone maps on chains with no length bound. These are the "parametric sandwich lemmas" the brief asks for, but indexed by a chain position rather than a fixed shape.

The open mathematical point is unchanged: the invariant must be found and shown inductive. The experiments now say what kind of statement it is.

**Follow-up: what the chain induction actually needs** (`experiments/omega/{chain_pattern, thresholds}.py`). All of this is on the configuration E | B m^{N−1} on Ω², a toy where Cut is already a theorem.
- *Ends do not localize.* A window with both chain ends kept and a gap inside is SAT in every shape tried (B-m, Bm-m, B-mm, Bm-mm, m-B, m-mB, m-B-m).
- *The schema is base + step + top.* Take witness clauses only at the cell patterns of the N = 7 core:
  - cells near the bottom, anchored to the bottom;
  - cells near the top, anchored to the top;
  - middle cells as relative patterns over consecutive positions (29 base, 54 bottom, 29 middle, 54 top);
  - all other witness clauses dropped.

  This system is UNSAT for every N from 4 to 9. N = 2, 3 are SAT only because the bottom and top blocks overlap; the full instances are UNSAT there. So one translation-invariant middle block suffices, as far as tested.
- *But the step is not bandwidth-bounded.* Drop every cell that pairs chain elements more than s apart:

  | span s | UNSAT for | SAT for |
  |---|---|---|
  | 1 | N ≤ 3 | N = 4–9 |
  | 2 | N ≤ 5 | N = 6–9 |
  | 3 | N ≤ 7 | N = 8, 9 |

  So span s works up to N = 2s + 2, and no finite-state automaton along the chain exists.
- *The far cells are corners between D-thresholds.* Only 4 of the 204 core cells at N = 7 span more than one step. Examples: (⊥, c₂) → (⊤, c₅), (⊥, c₃) → (E, c₆), (c₃, ⊥) → (c₆, E). Along the chain, D meets each line {(⊥, c)}, {(E, c)}, {(⊤, c)} (and their coordinate-0 mirrors) in a prefix. The far cells pair a row on one line with a column on another, across these thresholds. They are the chain analogue of `Plane`'s corner cells F((⊥, z₂), (⊤, z′₂)).
- *Coordinate-1 thresholds alone do not localize.* Keep the chain ends, B and the elements straddling the thresholds of the (⊥, c) and (⊤, c) lines, with D fixed accordingly and gaps elsewhere. All 37 compressed shapes are SAT. The full test needs the thresholds of all six lines: windows of up to about 14 kept elements, thousands of arrangements, about 40k cell pairs each. That is out of reach of the present encoding.

**Conclusion for the brief's question.** The certificate is uniform in an important sense: one middle step block, repeated. But its essential long-range dependency is the corner between data-dependent D-thresholds along a chain, exactly the object `Plane`'s corner lemma handles via finite guide sets. So the uniform argument for Ω² × C₂ should come from a *threshold-indexed corner lemma*:
- corners F((⊥, z), (⊤, z′)) taken at the D-thresholds of each line, in each layer;
- with the local step lemmas between them.

That is a hand proof generalizing `Plane`, guided by this data, rather than a bounded-window SAT certificate.

**The restriction step is now formal** (`lean/Mordor/Exact.lean`; standard axioms, and `nw` and `flat_exact` use none). A map π is *exact* on kept coordinates S if it compares every element with every s ∈ S exactly as L does.
- `nw`: composing a witness-free F with an exact π and restricting to cells over S stays witness-free.
- `mono`: monotone π preserves monotonicity.
- `prod`: exactness is coordinatewise, so retractions of the factors of L₁ × L₂ × C combine.
- `id_exact`: the identity is exact (used for the layer coordinate).
- `flat_exact`: in flat bounded orders, any map that reflects ⊥ and ⊤ and identifies nothing with a kept coordinate is exact. This covers the M_ω fresh-atom retraction.
- `exact_of_fix`: reduces the Ω gap retraction to comparisons of non-kept elements with kept ones.

What remains informal behind §34.3z⁶–¹⁰ is only the SAT encoding of the finite instances.
