## 34.3z¹¹ Thirteenth outside brief: four lateral strategies, a skeleton theorem, and the k × ∞ cut

The thirteenth brief (ChatGPT) asks to turn four analogies into proof strategies for join-closure of R(F) (`Frontier.frontier`): exchange (stable matching), dependency of combined commitments (judgment aggregation), context across an interface (handoffs), and merge-preserved invariants (CRDTs). The analogies have no force by themselves, but each one points at a statement that can be made precise and tested. The verdicts: A and D are restatements, with one refuted local form. B collapses onto the meet mechanism of §34.3l. C gives a machine-checked normal form and a new intermediate conjecture.

**The reachability structure, made explicit.** Every closure condition of `Reach.Closed` has a single premise: y ≤ x ∈ D ⟹ y ∈ D; x ∈ D ⟹ a(x) ∈ D; b(y) ∈ D ⟹ y ∈ D.
- So the closed sets are exactly the down-sets of one preorder, and they form a complete ring of sets, closed under arbitrary ∩ and ∪.
- R(F) is reachability from ⊥ by finite paths of moves u → v with v ∈ S(u) = ↓u ∪ ↓a(u) ∪ B(u), where B(u) = b⁻¹(↓u). S is monotone in u.
- So the union of two closed sets is closed, and "merging" closed sets is free. Join-closure asks something else: that the closed set R(F) be an ideal, a statement about elements.

**A. Exchange: one-step joins suffice, and every type of them is delayed.**
- *Reduction (proved).* R(F) is join-closed iff for every w ∈ R(F) the generators G(w) = {w, a(w)} ∪ Max B(w) have pairwise joins in R(F).
  - Induct on the sum of the path lengths. If x ∈ S(u) and y ∈ S(u′), the induction hypothesis gives u ⊔ u′ ∈ R, and x, y ∈ S(u ⊔ u′) by monotonicity.
  - This is the strongest local form of an exchange principle. It is still equivalent to join-closure, not weaker.
- *Local quantitative forms (refuted).* Is some type of pair always joined one stage later? `experiments/dialgebra/sibling.py` hill-climbs the sibling delay s(g ⊔ h) − (s(w) + 1) by pair type.

  | lattices | w ⊔ a(w) | w ⊔ v | a(w) ⊔ v | v ⊔ v′ (v, v′ ∈ B(w)) | join delay |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | all 5-element | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
  | all 6-element | 2 | 2 | 2 | 3 | 3 |

  So every type carries delay, including w ⊔ a(w). The inequality w ≤ a(w) fails on R(F) in 51 of the 6-element runs.
- No exchange that uses only one step is local, and the delay of §34.3k sits in all four types. Strategy A collapses onto §34.3k(1).

**B. Dependencies between combined commitments.**
- The pair type that carries the largest delay is v ⊔ v′ with b(v), b(v′) ≤ w: two individually admissible "b-commitments" whose join is not.
- The only mechanism known to act on such a join is the meet of off-pin values (§31.2, §34.3l): F(x ⊓ x′, y) ≤ F(x, y) ⊓ F(x′, y).
- Localising to an interval is the box calculus of §34.3p, and its limits are recorded in §34.3t.
- No dependency lemma beyond these was found. Strategy B contributes the observation that the meet law is the one that matters, and it reappears, in isolated form, in C.

**C. Context across the boundary: the skeleton theorem** (`lean/Mordor/Skeleton.lean`, standard axioms only).
- `compress`: let ρ push rows up inside D (x ≤ ρ x ∈ D) and κ push columns down inside Dᶜ (κ y ≤ y, κ y ∉ D). Then witness-freeness transfers to F(ρ x, κ y).
- `compress_mono`: monotonicity transfers when ρ and κ are monotone.
- `rho`, `kappa`, `skeleton`: with ACC and DCC, take ρ x = ⋀{maximal p ∈ D : x ≤ p} and κ y = ⋁{minimal u ∉ D : u ≤ y}. These are monotone and satisfy the hypotheses.
- So a counterexample on a chain-finite lattice can be assumed to have rows among finite meets of Max D and columns among finite joins of Min Dᶜ.
  - When both sets are finite, this is the labelling of §34.3m(iii) and is closed by `cut_fingen`.
  - The open case is exactly an infinite labelling: index sets P = Max D and Q = Min Dᶜ, with one of them infinite.

*What context must be preserved.* The infinite labelling problem over finite subsets is satisfiable in the abstract:
- P ≅ ℤ, all cells D-valued, α(A, B) = {p < max A}. Then α is monotone in A, never contains A, and is never empty.
- Dually, Q ≅ ℤ with all cells Dᶜ-valued.

ACC kills the dual example.
- The joins c_N = ⋁{u_n : n < N} stabilise at some N₀.
- For max B = N ≥ N₀, the cell then satisfies ⋁B ≤ ⋁β(A, B) ≤ z(A, B), a witness.

So the context the analogy asks for is the finite-subjoin (finite-submeet) realization of the labels. It is precisely what a chain condition supplies and what the free labelling forgets.

**D. Merge-preserved invariants.**
- The closed sets are already merge-closed, as shown above. An invariant of the stages R_k that implies join-closure fails at finite stages, which are routinely non-principal (§34.3k).
- An invariant of R(F) alone that implies join-closure is principalization (§34.3k(3)).
- The genuine merge theorem in the repository is the product step `Merge.cut_prod_two_of_bot`. It is Cut on Q × C₂ from Cut on Q for non-thick cuts, and its open remainder is the thick cuts, which DC2 (§34.3z⁶⁻⁸) attacks.

Strategy D collapses onto those.

**Boundary normal form** (`Skeleton.bnf_nowit`, `bnf_mono`, Lean, standard axioms). This is stronger than the skeleton reduction.
- With ACC and DCC, a witness-free monotone F may be replaced by bnf F (x, y) = ⋀_{p ∈ Max D, x ≤ p} ⋁_{v ∈ Min Dᶜ, v ≤ y} F(p, v).
  - Witness-freeness: the column joins keep W1 because some minimal v ≤ y exists (DCC), and keep W2 because they sit below F.
  - The row meets keep W1 because they sit above the column joins, and keep W2 because some maximal p ≥ x exists (ACC).
- No down-set hypothesis is used.
- So a counterexample is determined by its **boundary table** Max D × Min Dᶜ, extended by meets over rows and joins over columns.
  - This is the labelling of §34.3m(iii) with F(⋀p_A, ⋁u_B) replaced by ⋀_A ⋁_B F(p, u). It is literally a table on generators even when Max D and Min Dᶜ are infinite.
  - In the k = 2 case, row r becomes exactly F(p, ·) ⊓ F(q, ·), and rows p and q become joins of their values at minimal columns.
  - Adding this shape to the z3 model (`kinf.py meetR,joinC`) does not shorten the k = 2 contradiction. With items 1–4, W2 at ⊤ or at depth 1 still leaves models at |U| = 6.

*Fourteenth brief (ChatGPT, against ff13fa2): orbit ranks.* Write f, g, h for rows p, q, r. The brief proposes two things:
- A1: the q-orbit of the last q-type column c_n of the r-orbit of ⊤ stays in T_r^q.
- B: a lexicographic rank in (α, β) that never resets at a type switch.

Its proposed test, a fully constrained finite model violating A1, cannot be run. The full reduced problem has no finite models (UNSAT for |U| ≤ 7), so every lemma holds vacuously there. The informative test is relative to near-models.
- In the near-models (items 1–4, W2 waived at minimal columns), the *premise* of A1 already fails. Whenever the r-orbit of ⊤ really reaches D, z3 finds no near-model at |U| = 3–7. This needs only item 4 among the lemmas (item 3 can be dropped).
  - Hence: the r-orbit of ⊤ terminates in D (true in every real instance), and items 4 and W2 then give UNSAT in all small cases.
  - W2 must still hold deeper than the first two levels below ⊤ (with W2 only at depth ≤ 1 or ≤ 2, models exist at |U| = 6).
- Two remarks from the brief are correct and now recorded.
  - The θ-descent raises β at every step it stays in U, since θ(y) ≤ g(y) ≤ b_{β(y)+1}. So it ends within l steps without appealing to DCC.
  - The same bound shows that every descent in items 2–4 is bounded by k or l. All uses of DCC reduce to the termination of the three orbits of ⊤.
- **Lemma X (proved informally).**
  - *Statement.* If g(s) ≤ s and h(s) ∈ U, then h(s) ∉ T_r^q. Symmetrically, if f(s) ≤ s and h(s) ∈ U, then h(s) ∉ T_r^p.
  - *Proof.* The q-orbit s_j = g^j(s) descends, with s_j ≤ b_j, and so reaches T_q within l steps. Inductively h(s_j) ≤ h(s) ∈ T_r^q and h(s_j) ≤ g(s_j) = s_{j+1}. So s_{j+1} lies above a column of T_r^q; it is not in T_r^p, and not in T_r^q by item 4, so it is in A_r. This contradicts the end of the orbit in T_q ⊆ T_r^p. ∎
  - *Corollary.* h maps Z = S_p ∩ S_q into A_r.
  - With Lemma X added, the single-violation near-models (r-orbit terminating, W2 waived at minimal columns, exactly one further W2 failure allowed) are UNSAT at |U| ≤ 4 (≤ 5 if W2 at ⊤ is kept).
    - At |U| = 5 and 6 the one violation sits on the q-orbit of ⊤: g(⊤) = ⊤, or g(b₁) = b₁.
    - Repairing it, in the |U| = 6 model, fails on monotonicity of row r at b₁. It would need an element between {h(a₁), h(b₂)} and {f(b₁), g(b₁)}, and the poset has none.
    - So the remaining contradiction runs through all three orbits together, and through interpolation constraints on row r; no single further invariant was found.
  - Lemma X is a proved, restricted form of lemma C: h, not g, preserves the absence of T_r^q from S_q-columns. It does not close the argument (near-models with Lemma X added still exist). The remaining case is the last step c_{n−1} → c_n of the r-orbit when c_{n−1} ∉ S_q.

*Captured tables after the normal form (k = 2).* `FinGenAll.captured` allows *any* elements u_j ∉ D as column generators, and for a finitely generated D every D-valued cell is captured automatically.
- In normal form, row r is σ(y) = F(p, y) ⊓ F(q, y). Take the Kleene chain c_0 = ⊤, c_{t+1} = σ(c_t). It descends, cannot stop at a U-fixed point (W2), and so by DCC ends at some c_n with σ(c_n) ∈ D.
- Use u = (c_0, …, c_n). Joins of a chain are its elements. Every U-valued cell at c_t with t < n lies above c_{t+1}, since F(p, c_t), F(q, c_t) ≥ σ(c_t) = c_{t+1}.
- So the table is captured **except at the two cells (p, c_n) and (q, c_n)**. At most one of these is D-valued, since both would give a witness in row r.
- The Sperner pivot of `captured` is therefore exactly one of those cells, and the k = 2 case reduces to capturing the end of the σ-chain.
- Adding the uncaptured value w = F(q, c_n) as a further generator creates the new columns c_t ⊔ w. Whether this refinement terminates is the B5 question of §34.3o, in its sharpest form so far: one uncaptured cell per round.
- One round can be pushed further by capturing w with u = w ⊓ c_n, which extends the chain whenever that meet lies in U.
  - The new bottom column u carries D-values in rows r and p. Row q is either D-valued, which gives a witness in row r, or U-valued and again uncaptured.
  - Iterating y ↦ y ⊓ F(q, y) is the θ-descent of item 4 and ends at a column y ∈ T_p with y ⊓ F(q, y) ∈ D, where the meet trick stops.
- *Assessment.* FG∞ at k = 2 does not isolate a finitary core. For k = 1 the σ-chain ends in a witness; at k = 2 the end of the chain leaves exactly one uncaptured cell. The open step is the same unbounded refinement that blocks the general case (§34.3o, B5), in its smallest form.
  - So a proof of k = 2 would probably contain the mechanism for the general case.
  - Conversely, a counterexample to the refinement at k = 2 would need an infinite sequence of meets falling into D, which is what DC2 and the M_ω² × C₃ run test from the other side.

### The k × ∞ cut (conjecture, partly proved)

> **Conjecture FG∞.** In a complete lattice with ACC and DCC, every cut D = ↓{p₁, …, p_k} with finitely many maximal elements has a witness, however many minimal elements Dᶜ has. Dually: finitely many minimal elements of Dᶜ.

- **Status.** (Superseded for k = 2 by §34.3z¹⁴: `FGTwo.cut_two` proves it in Lean, DCC only.)
  - k = 1 is `cut_principal_down` (DCC only).
  - Both sides finite is `cut_fingen`.
  - FG∞ for all k would reduce the open case to cuts with infinitely many maximal elements of D *and* infinitely many minimal elements of Dᶜ.
- **Reduction.** By `compress` with ρ = `rho`, the rows take the finitely many values m_A = ⋀_{i∈A} p_i. Nothing is lost on the column side.

**k = 2, proved pieces** (D = ↓{p, q}, r = p ⊓ q, rows r, p, q, columns in U = Dᶜ). Write T_p = {u : F(p, u) ∈ D} and S_p = {u : F(p, u) ≤ u}, and likewise for q.
1. T_p and T_q are down-sets of U, and they are disjoint.
   - A D-value of row p is ≤ q and not ≤ p; a D-value of row q is ≤ p and not ≤ q.
   - At a column in T_p ∩ T_q, F(r, u) ≤ F(p, u) ⊓ F(q, u) ≤ p ⊓ q = r, a witness.
2. S_p is closed under u ↦ F(p, u) whenever that value lies in U, and the iteration strictly decreases (no witness).
   - By DCC, every minimal element of S_p lies in T_p. Since ⊤ ∈ S_p, T_p ≠ ∅; likewise T_q ≠ ∅.
3. T_p ∩ S_q = ∅ and T_q ∩ S_p = ∅.
   - Inside T_p, row q is U-valued by 1.
   - A q-descent from u ∈ T_p ∩ S_q stays in T_p ∩ S_q (T_p is a down-set) and never ends.
4. Let T_r^p (resp. T_r^q) be the columns where F(r, u) is a D-value of p-type (resp. q-type). These are disjoint down-sets, with T_q ⊆ T_r^p, T_p ⊆ T_r^q, T_r^p ∩ S_p = ∅ and T_r^q ∩ S_q = ∅. A column above an element of T_p and an element of T_q (⊤, for instance) has all three rows U-valued.
5. In particular F(r, ⊤) ∈ U is a common lower bound in U of F(p, ⊤) and F(q, ⊤). So F(p, ⊤) ∈ T_p and F(q, ⊤) ∈ T_q cannot both hold.

**Missing step** (sharpened by the lemma mining in the table below: under items 1–4 and Z = {⊤}, the remaining contradiction never uses W2 at a minimal column, but does use it at depth ≥ 2 along the descents from ⊤). Show that the disjoint, nonempty down-sets T_p and T_q of item 1, together with items 2–5, are contradictory, by an argument that uses only DCC on U (a descent, not a count). The natural descent set S_p ∩ S_q ∋ ⊤ has a minimal element u whose three row values all lie in U below u. None of them is visibly in S_p ∩ S_q again.

**Further reductions for k = 2 (proved informally).** Put Z = S_p ∩ S_q, the columns where rows p and q both stay below the column; ⊤ ∈ Z.
6. *Sub-instances.* For t ∈ Z, every value at a column below t lies below t or in D, so ↓t ∩ U with top t is again an instance. By DCC we may assume **Z = {⊤}**.
7. *A minimal instance is generated by ⊤.* A column that is not the value of any cell can be deleted. So we may also assume every column other than ⊤ is a value.
8. *Joins (in L).* For y ∈ Z, H(y) = F(p, y) ⊔ F(q, y) lies in U and in Z, and H(y) ≤ y. Minimal elements of Z are therefore fixed points of H. Combined with 6: F(p, ⊤) ⊔ F(q, ⊤) = ⊤.
9. *ACC ascent.* With a = F(p, ⊤), the chain y ↦ y ⊔ F(q, y) started at a stays below ⊤ and ends in Z, so it ends at ⊤.
   - The weakened W2 ("no U-valued fixed point") already makes the finite form UNSAT up to |U| = 6. So the finite contradiction is a fixed-point argument.
   - What is missing is the right monotone map, one whose descending Kleene chain from ⊤ never leaves U.
10. *Not yet decisive.* The swap iteration (a, b) ↦ (F(p, b), F(q, a)) on pairs with F(p, b) ≤ a and F(q, a) ≤ b descends componentwise from (⊤, ⊤). It ends at a fixed pair or at a column of T_p or T_q; neither end is visibly contradictory.

**Falsification** (`experiments/fgcut/kinf.py`, `kinf_k.py`, z3). The reduced statement is posed on a finite poset {rows} ∪ E ∪ U: E holds extra D-elements, U has a top, and F is monotone and witness-free.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| k = 2, \|U\| ≤ 4, all U-side lattice laws on (joins in U, p ⊔ q, m ⊔ u, u ⊓ p), and each alone | UNSAT |
| k = 2, \|U\| ≤ 7, **no** lattice law on U at all | UNSAT |
| k = 2, without r = glb(p, q) | SAT at \|U\| = 3 |
| k = 2, without W2 on row r | UNSAT (\|U\| = 5) |
| k = 2, without W2 on row p, or without W1 on row r or p | SAT at \|U\| = 5 |
| k = 3 (rows: the free meet-semilattice on p₁, p₂, p₃), \|U\| ≤ 4 | UNSAT |
| k = 2, W2 weakened to "no U-fixed point", \|U\| ≤ 6 | UNSAT |
| k = 2, W2 waived at minimal columns, plus descent endpoints t ∈ T_m ∩ S_m required | SAT at \|U\| = 5–7 (each model stalls one descent on a waived column) |
| as above, plus items 1–4 as hard constraints | SAT at \|U\| = 5–8: the surviving models have a common fixed point of all three rows at a waived minimal column, i.e. a degenerate minimal element of Z |
| as above, plus Z = {⊤} (item 6) | **UNSAT** at \|U\| = 5, 6, 7: once items 1–4 and 6 hold, W2 is never needed at minimal columns |
| items 1–4, 6, endpoints, W2 only at ⊤ / only at ⊤ and the columns F(m, ⊤) | SAT at \|U\| = 5 / 6: W2 is needed deeper along the descents |
| items 1–4, W2 for rows p, q **only along their own Kleene orbits of ⊤** (no W2 on row r, no item 6) | **UNSAT** at \|U\| = 5, 6, 7 (with or without item 6) |
| the same with W2 only on the p-orbit (row q: none, or only at ⊤) | SAT at \|U\| = 5, 6 |
| W2 at depth ≤ 2 from ⊤ only | UNSAT at \|U\| ≤ 6, SAT at 7 (the model stalls the p-orbit at depth 3) |

So, on small instances, the contradiction needs exactly the following facts:
- items 1–4;
- W1;
- monotonicity;
- that the two Kleene chains ⊤ > F(p, ⊤) > F(p, F(p, ⊤)) > … and ⊤ > F(q, ⊤) > … reach D.

These are the endpoints a_k ∈ T_p and b_l ∈ T_q, and the chains themselves matter, not just the endpoints. One consequence is a cross-descent:
- From a_k, the iterates g_{t+1} = F(q, g_t) satisfy g_t ≤ b_t, so they reach T_q within l steps.
- Symmetrically, from b_l the iterates of F(p, ·) reach T_p within k steps.

What is missing is the reason this ping-pong between T_p and T_q cannot go on.

*A potential, and the lemma it needs.* Put α(u) = max{i : u ≤ a_i} and β(u) = max{j : u ≤ b_j}.
- By monotonicity, F(p, ·) raises α by at least one, F(q, ·) raises β by at least one, and F(r, ·) ≤ F(p, ·) ⊓ F(q, ·) raises both.
- α(u) = k forces u ∈ T_p ⊆ T_r^q, and β(u) = l forces u ∈ T_q ⊆ T_r^p.
- So the r-chain from ⊤ climbs both potentials. It stops at some c_n with n ≤ min(k, l) and F(r, c_n) ∈ D, say q-type, so c_n ∈ T_r^q. Then n < l, because F(q, c_n) ∈ U lies below F(q, b_n).
- The proof would close if **F(q, ·) mapped T_r^q into itself**. Its iterates from c_n would then raise β up to l while staying in T_r^q, which is disjoint from T_q ⊆ T_r^p.
- Near-models show that this lemma (lemma C) does not follow from the local constraints and items 1–4. Any proof of it has to use DCC again. The weaker statement "F(q, ·) never maps T_r^q into T_q" fails in near-models too.

*Near-models.* Waive W2 at the minimal columns of U, as a stand-in for a truncated infinite descent. Then models appear at |U| = 5, 6, and they are degenerate:
- row q takes a D-value at every column (T_q = U), so T_p = ∅;
- the p-descent ends at a waived column.

With DCC that escape is closed by item 2.

So the meet r = p ⊓ q, and W1/W2 on the generator rows, are the whole content. The U side needs nothing but its order and a top. This supports a descent proof using DCC alone. A finite check cannot replace one: the orbit of ⊤ under the row maps need not be finite.

**Honest scope.**
- The reachability preorder and the one-step-join reduction are proved informally; each is a few lines.
- `Skeleton.lean` is machine-checked.
- The sibling-delay table is a hill-climb on all lattices of 5 and 6 elements: a lower bound on the delays, not their maxima.
- FG∞ is conjectural beyond k = 1. Its k = 2 pieces 1–5 are proved informally. The z3 rows are exhaustive for the stated sizes only.
- None of this touches the M_ω² × C₃ run (§34.3z⁷⁻⁸ machinery), which continues.

## 34.3z¹² Fifteenth brief: recursive invariants across heights, Ramsey homogeneity, refinement as rule discovery

The brief (ChatGPT, against d26632f) asks three questions:
- whether the height certificates are explained by a recursive invariant (A);
- whether Ramsey homogeneity simplifies infinite boundaries (B);
- whether the refinement loop reveals a reusable rule (C).

**A. Do certificates nest?** The core terms are read from `CutHeight4.lean` (`tv4`, 17 terms) and `CutHeight5.lean` (`tv5`, 86 terms); the script is `experiments/sat/seeded_height.py`.
- All 17 H = 4 core terms occur in the H = 5 core. This is mostly an artifact of the deterministic search: they are among the first 26 terms the H = 5 run generates (positions 0–25), because the tightness loop starts identically at every height.
- **Relativized copy.** Under σ: ⊤ ↦ c₀ = F(⊥, ⊤) (so ⊥ ↦ ⊥, F ↦ F), all 17 H = 4 core terms land in the H = 5 core as well, at positions 28–54 of 128.
  - c₀ ∉ D (WLOG) and c₀ ≠ ⊤, so [⊥, c₀] is a poset of smaller edge-height.
  - The certificate thus contains the H = 4 block at the top and a second copy below c₀. Together they make 27 of the 86 core terms; the other 59 are new.
  - Among the new terms, the descent c_k = F(⊥, c_{k−1}) gains one step, c₄. The rest concentrate on rows c₁, c₂, c₃ and their iterates.
- **Seeding does not transfer the structure.**
  - H = 5 seeded with T₄ needs 121 terms, and with T₄ ∪ σT₄ needs 133, against 128 unseeded.
  - At H = 6, seeding with T₅, or with T₅ ∪ σT₅, was still SAT at 461 and 430 terms after 13–18 min.
  - The tightness loop still builds most of the certificate.
- **The interval induction is not what the certificates use.** The sound height-induction clauses (`lazy_ih`: an F-stable [x, ⊤] with x ∈ D, or [⊥, x] with x ∉ D, would be a smaller counterexample) never fire at H = 6 in 300 terms, and are not needed at H ≤ 5.
  - So the relativized block below c₀ is a copy of terms *without* F-stability: F-values of cells below c₀ leave [⊥, c₀].
  - A transition lemma "Cut at H − 1 on [⊥, c₀] ⟹ …" would need to control exactly those escapes.
- *Status.* The recursive copy is experimentally observed at 4 → 5 only. No transition lemma was found. The extraction property in its natural form (an F-stable sub-interval counterexample) is refuted by the IH clauses never firing.

**B. Ramsey homogeneity: the obstruction is precise.** In the boundary normal form (`Skeleton.bnf_nowit`), witness-freeness is a statement about each finite sub-table z(A, B) = ⋀_A ⋁_B φ.
- So the "no witness" colour is homogeneous by hypothesis. Ramsey can add information only through the *capture pattern*: whether a U-valued cell lies above some element of the tuple.
- If an infinite homogeneous family carries the captured pattern, `FinGenAll.captured` on any finite sub-table gives a witness. That is a contradiction.
- If it carries the uncaptured pattern, nothing follows locally. The canonical-Ramsey model of §34.3z⁵ is exactly this situation, and it is SAT even for the plane cut, where Cut is a theorem.
- What is lost is the F-dynamics: a proof must follow cell values to boundary elements *outside* the homogeneous family. Even the principal case k = 1 needs this, since its descent moves from column y to the column F(d, y).
- *Status: refuted* as a self-contained route. Any homogeneity argument must be combined with a descent along F-values.

**C. Refinement as rule discovery.** The refinement loop's obstruction has already been isolated in its smallest form (§34.3z¹¹, σ-chain tables): after the σ-chain, exactly one uncaptured cell per round remains.
- The rule to discover is therefore a *capture rule*: which element to add so that the next uncaptured cell is strictly smaller in some well-founded order.
- The θ-descent (meets with F(q, ·)) is the best candidate tested. It ends when the meet falls into D.
- A "discriminating" next-cell heuristic would change the search, not this structural fact. It is not pursued.

**Ranking.**
1. C, as the capture-rule question for k = 2: smallest, exactly posed, falsifiable in near-models.
2. A: there is a concrete observed structure (the relativized block), but no lemma yet.
3. B is refuted as stated.

The H = 6 ground refutation is still running externally (§34.3h). It is the one experiment whose answer is unconditionally interpretable.

## 34.3z¹³ Sixteenth brief: a route from the Cut Lemma to RH? A no-go for positivity bridges

The brief (ChatGPT) proposes three programs. **A** is a "Weil–Cut bridge": from explicit-formula data, build a monotone F_E on the subspace lattice Sub(E) of a finite test space E, such that a negative direction of the compressed Weil form Q_E produces a proper down-set D_E closed under a and b⁻¹. The Cut Lemma would then exclude it. **B** is the density/continuity transfer from finite compressions to the full form. **C** is a zero-exclusion strengthening of the rank–trace method. (Rank–trace refers to the 2026 result that more than 2/3 of the zeros are simple and on the line, arXiv 2608.13637, with an independent proof in 2609.02882.)

**The positivity–join lemma is correct and elementary.** Q ⪰ 0 iff 𝒫(Q ⊕ [1]) = {U : the form is PSD on U} is closed under spans. Since Sub(E) has ACC, a join-closed down-set is principal, so the lemma says no more than that a PSD subspace containing all others exists.

**Program A is vacuous as a use of the Cut Lemma** (`lean/Mordor/SubspaceCut.lean`, `Ranked.R_univ`, `Ranked.closed_eq_univ`; standard axioms plus the `bv_decide` trust of `cut_height5`).
- Take a complete lattice with a strictly monotone rank r ≤ 5. Every monotone F has R(F) = L, and *every* down-set containing ⊥ and closed under a F and (b F)⁻¹ is all of L.
- Sub(E) with dim E ≤ 5 is the instance r = finrank. That instance is not compiled here, because Mathlib's linear-algebra oleans are not built in this checkout.
- Under the standing conjecture (finite height ⊆ chain-finite) the same holds in every dimension; if the H = 6 run of §34.3h ends UNSAT, it holds in dimension 6 too.
- Hence condition 2 of the bridge (D_E closed) holds for *no* F when D_E is proper. The implication "negative direction ⟹ D_E closed" is then equivalent to "no negative direction". The Cut Lemma contributes no information about Q_E.
- At best it could serve as a proof by contradiction. That would mean turning a hypothetical negative direction into a witness-free monotone F. But the Cut Lemma's proofs (Sperner parity, ground certificates) use only the order. So all the arithmetic would have to sit in that construction, and the construction must fail for diag(1, −1). No such mechanism is in sight.

**Programs B and C do not involve Mordor.**
- B is standard functional analysis. Its only technical point is a topology in which the Weil distribution is continuous and the test spaces are dense.
- C is analytic number theory. An inertia bound n₋(G_N) < 1 is just positivity of G_N, restated.

*Verdict.* Not pursued. No mathematical link between the zeta function and the reachability dynamics was found, and the order-theoretic theorem cannot supply positivity.

## 34.3z¹⁴ New: FG∞ at k = 2 — the Cut Lemma for every two-generated cut (Lean)

**Theorem** (`lean/Mordor/FGTwo.lean`, `FGTwo.cut_two`, standard axioms only). Let α be a bounded lattice with DCC, F monotone, and D = ↓p ∪ ↓q with ⊤ ∉ D. Then some x ∈ D and y ∉ D have F(x, y) ≤ x or y ≤ F(x, y).
- No ACC and no completeness are assumed. The complement of D may have infinitely many minimal elements.
- By the order duality of §34.3h (dual lattice, cut Dᶜ, F(y, x)), the same holds under ACC when Dᶜ = ↑u ∪ ↑v. This dual is not yet stated in Lean.
- With k = 1 (`cut_principal_down`) this settles FG∞ for k ≤ 2 (§34.3z¹¹). It is the first case of the Cut Lemma for cuts with infinitely many boundary elements on one side and no finiteness assumption on the lattice.

**Proof.** Put r = p ⊓ q, h = F(r, ·), and for a generator a (the other one b) g = F(a, ·). Assume no witness.
1. *`orbit_end`.* The h-orbit of ⊤ descends strictly (W2 at row r) and reaches D by DCC. Its last value z lies below p or below q. It is never below both, since z ≤ r would be a witness at row r.
2. *`l4`.* Suppose h(u) is a D-value below a and g(u) ≤ u. A D-value of g lies below b (W1 at row a). It would put h(u) below a ⊓ b = r, so g(u) ∉ D. Then g(u) < u has the same two properties, which is an infinite descent.
3. *`sandwich`.* Let g(s) ≤ s, and suppose the h-orbit e₀ = s, e_{k+1} = h(e_k) ends at e_m with h(e_m) below a.
   - By `l4`, m ≥ 1.
   - Put w = g(s). Since r ≤ a, h(s) ≤ g(s) ≤ s, i.e. e₁ ≤ w ≤ e₀. By monotonicity e_{k+1} ≤ h^k(w) ≤ e_k for every k.
   - So the h-orbit of w stays outside D up to step m − 1. It then ends either at step m, below e_m with value below h(e_m) ≤ a, or at step m − 1, with a D-value above h(e_m). That value cannot be below b, because then h(e_m) would be below r.
   - In both cases the orbit of w ends below a, while w < s and g(w) ≤ w. DCC excludes the infinite descent.
4. Apply `sandwich` to s = ⊤ with a = q, or with a = p, according to where the orbit of ⊤ ends. ∎

**How it was found.** The k = 2 analysis of §34.3z¹¹ is item by item correct, but it looked for an invariant of row q's own dynamics (lemma C), which near-models refute. The decisive object is the *pair* of rows r ≤ a.
- Minimal unsat cores of the reduced z3 model (`experiments/fgcut/kinf.py`, flags `A1prem,startS,noP,track`; |U| = 2–5) use only rows q and r: L4q, W1 at row r, W2 at row q, and monotonicity.
- The two-row statement "for g(s) ≤ s the h-orbit of s cannot end below q" (K″) was UNSAT for |U| ≤ 6 even with no induction hypothesis (`stepK,noIH`). That pointed to a one-step descent, namely `sandwich`.
- The ChatGPT brief that prompted this round asked for exactly this capture lemma. It warned against assuming lemma C, which the proof indeed avoids.

**k ≥ 3.** The same argument breaks at step 3: a D-value above h(e_m) need only lie below *some* generator below which h(e_m) lies, not below a. For k = 2 the only candidate is a itself. FG∞ for k ≥ 3 remains open.

**k = 3: where the sandwich generalization stops** (seventeenth brief; informal). Put b = p₁ ⊓ p₂ ⊓ p₃, h = F(b, ·), and for A ⊆ {1, 2, 3} let m_A = ⋀_{i∈A} p_i, g_A = F(m_A, ·) and S_A = {s ∉ D : g_A(s) ≤ s}. For a D-value z, let Λ(z) = {i : z ≤ p_i}; W1 at row b gives ∅ ≠ Λ(z) ≠ {1, 2, 3}.
- *L4 generalizes, by downward induction on |A|.* Suppose u ∈ S_A and h(u) ∈ D with Λ ⊇ A. If g_A(u) ∈ D, then Λ(g_A u) ⊉ A (W1 at row m_A). So Λ(h u) ⊇ A ∪ Λ(g_A u) =: A′ ⊋ A, and u ∈ S_{A′}, because g_{A′} ≤ g_A. If g_A(u) ∈ U, descend. Hence no u ∈ S_A has an h-value in D with Λ ⊇ A. **Erratum (§34.3z²⁰):** A′ ⊋ A needs Λ(g_A u) ⊄ A, but W1 gives only Λ(g_A u) ⊉ A. The case ∅ ≠ Λ(g_A u) ⊊ A (k = 3: A = {1, 2}, Λ = {1}) is not covered, so this generalization is proved only for |A| = 1 with k = 2 (`FGTwo.l4`) and is open otherwise. It is the strict switch again.
- *The sandwich generalizes except in one case.* Take s ∈ S_A, w = g_A(s) < s. The h-orbit of w is squeezed as before and ends either below e_m, with labels ⊇ Λ(z), or at a D-value y with z ≤ y ≤ e_m and Λ(y) ⊆ Λ(z).
  - If Λ(y) ⊄ A, then s ∈ S_{A ∪ Λ(y)} handles it (a larger set).
  - **The open case is Λ(y) ⊊ A.** For k = 3 this is A = {1, 2} with Λ(y) = {1}. Continuing needs w ∈ S_{Λ(y)}, but w is only in S_A, and S_A ⊇ S_{Λ(y)}. So the larger label sets need the smaller ones and vice versa; no induction order serves both. This is precisely the "strict-switch" clause of the brief.
  - Using the join H = ⋁ g_i to stay in several S_i at once loses the strict descent: H-fixed points are not contradictory (§34.3z¹¹, item 8).
- *Status: open.* The missing ingredient is a way to pass from an S_A-state whose orbit ends with labels Λ ⊊ A to an S_Λ-state below it.
- *Eighteenth brief: the "transfer lemma".* Its statement asks for t < s with t ∉ D and g_B(t) ≤ t, where B = Λ(y). That is too weak: the induction also needs the h-orbit of t to end with labels ⊇ B.
  - *Corrected form (proved informally).* Every t with e_{j+1} ≤ t ≤ h^j(w) for some j < m has exactly this property. Its orbit is squeezed between the orbits of e_{j+1} and h^j(w), so h^{m−j}(t) ∈ [z, y] ⊆ D, with labels ⊇ Λ(y) = B. Moreover t ≤ w < s.
  - So with the combined order (t, B) ≺ (s, A) iff t < s, or t = s and A ⊊ B (well-founded: DCC times a finite label set), the strict switch is closed as soon as one of the intervals [e_{j+1}, h^j(w)] meets S_B.
  - But any such t contradicts the induction hypothesis directly. "Some interval meets S_B" is therefore equivalent to the strict-switch configuration being impossible: a restatement, not a reduction.
  - Two small facts. If |B| = 1, then g_B(v) ∈ U, since a D-value of g_B would need labels ⊊ B. And e_m ∉ S_B.
  - The obvious candidates fail without an extra inequality g_B(x) ≤ x: Kleene ascent of x ↦ x ⊔ g_B(x) from e_m, and θ-descent x ↦ x ⊓ g_B(x) from v.
  - *Nineteenth brief: clipped-map escape.* Put T(x) = (x ⊔ g_B(x)) ⊓ u_j on I_j = [e_{j+1}, h^j(w)].
    - **Lemma A1** (`lean/Mordor/ClipEscape.lean`, standard axioms). Under ACC, T has a fixed point t ∈ I_j (`exists_fixed`). If I_j ∩ S_B = ∅, then g_B(t) ≰ u_j (`escape_of_empty`). Also t ≰ g_B(t), by W2.
    - This is pure lattice theory and carries no information about F.
  - *Escape-to-progress: no measure exists along this route.* The escape value g_B(t) has only *lower* orbit bounds: g_B(t) ≥ h(t) ≥ e_{j+2}, so h^k(g_B t) ≥ e_{j+2+k}.
    - Every orbit-entry or label measure needs an *upper* bound. Only the upper sandwich bounds force the orbit into D and give the labels ⊇ B.
    - The only upper bound available is g_B(t) ≤ g_B(s), which is useful exactly when g_B(s) ≤ s, i.e. s ∈ S_B. In that case the sandwich could have been run with B from the start.
    - So escape-to-progress needs the same missing inequality as the transfer lemma. It is not an independent route.
  - No near-model was built for k = 3. The fully constrained reduced model is UNSAT at the sizes tried, and any relaxation would have to replace DCC by the induction hypothesis over all smaller states, which is the statement under test.

**Height induction in the ground search.** The interval reduction proposed for the H = 6 fallback already exists as `cut_ground.py`'s `ih` option: diagonal terms F(x, x) with "x ∈ D, x ≤ F(x, x) ⇒ x = ⊥" and the dual. It is sound given Cut at H − 1. The two-sided version [x, y] adds nothing, because x ≠ ⊥ or y ≠ ⊤ already triggers a one-sided clause. At H = 5 it makes the certificate larger (142 terms against 120, with `allmid`). FGTwo does not help the ground search either, because that search is for posets and `cut_two` needs the meet p ⊓ q.

**Other parts of the brief.**
- DC2 for M_ω² × C₃ is the running external computation (§34.3z⁶⁻⁸, `run_c3.sh`, `run_one.sh`). The edge-height 6 refutation (§34.3h, `run_height.py 6`) would settle it independently.
- RH: no Mordor-derived route. The no-go of §34.3z¹³ stands, and no zeta-specific monotone bifunction was found.

## 34.3z¹⁵ A second opinion (Le Chat): a uniform potential on the box? Box cells and the box Cut Lemma

The review proposes skipping per-height certificates: read the descent game off the small cores (`tv4`, `tv5`), and prove a strictly decreasing measure on the box (c_k, c_{k−1}) that works for every H at once. It describes the covered classes and the burned routes correctly (nesting, interval induction, Ramsey). Three points need qualifying.
- **The ground theory is the poset theory** (§34.3h). A uniform argument read off these certificates would hold in posets of every finite height, so it would prove Cut on every *finite poset*. That case is open (§34.3m). The only proof known for finite *lattices* is the Sperner parity argument (§34.3n), which is not a descent. A closed SAT model at H = 6 would be a finite poset counterexample, not a lattice counterexample.
- **Certificate growth does not argue against a uniform proof.** A Herbrand certificate is the union of the terms over all branches of a case analysis, so a uniform induction of depth O(H) can still need exponentially many terms. Growth only says that per-height computation will not scale.
- **The obvious potential is box height, and it fails exactly at the known escape.** See the H = 3 proof and the restriction tests below.

**H = 3 by hand, from the term-minimal certificate {c₁, c₂, c₃, F(c₂, ⊤), F(c₂, c₁)}** (c₁ = F(⊥, ⊤) ∉ D, c_{k+1} = F(⊥, c_k); edge-height 3 means at most four elements in a chain).
1. If c₂ ∉ D, then ⊥ < c₃ < c₂ < c₁ < ⊤ by no-witness at row ⊥, which is five elements. So c₂ ∈ D and ⊥ < c₂ < c₁ < ⊤.
2. c₁ = F(⊥, ⊤) ≤ F(c₂, ⊤) < ⊤ (W2), so height forces F(c₂, ⊤) = c₁ (the pin).
3. v = F(c₂, c₁) satisfies v ≥ F(⊥, c₁) = c₂ and v ≰ c₂, and v ≤ F(c₂, ⊤) = c₁ and c₁ ≰ v. So c₂ < v < c₁: a five-element chain. ∎
- Step 3 is the box potential: the value lands strictly inside the box (c₂, c₁), so the box shrinks.
- Step 2 is what keeps v inside: an upper bound for the row from the *global* height bound. At larger H the pin is unavailable and v can escape sideways. The H = 4 and H = 5 cores consist of exactly this escape handling: row iterates F(s, F(s, t)), and fresh column descents F(⊥, ·) restarted at escaped values (§34.3z¹² A).

**Term-minimal certificates** (`box_cut.py minimize`: deletion-minimal, subterm-closed, deepest terms tried first): 7, 17 and 71 terms for H = 3, 4, 5 (the drat-trim cores: 7, 17, 86). H = 4 has no redundant term at all.

**Which cells suffice?** These are runs of the tightness search, restricted to candidate cells F(x, y) of a given shape in the current model (`box_cut.py cells`):

| candidates F(x, y), x ∈ D, y ∉ D | H = 3 | H = 4 | H = 5 |
|---|---|---|---|
| all (§34.3h) | 7 terms | 19 | 128 terms, 17 s |
| **box: x < y** | 7 | 18 | **113 terms, 3 s** |
| covering box: nothing between x and y | still SAT at 200 terms | — | — |
| good box: x ≤ F(⊥, y), the shape (c_k, c_{k−1}) | 7 | no refutation in 100 s | — |

So the moves of a potential argument can be restricted to comparable cells. They cannot be restricted to the descent boxes (c_k, c_{k−1}), nor to adjacent pairs. At H = 6 the box restriction does not visibly change the growth rate: 328 terms in 8 min, still SAT.

**The box Cut Lemma (conjecture).** *Every bounded poset of finite edge-height, every monotone F and every down-set D with ⊥ ∈ D, ⊤ ∉ D have a witness F(x, y) ≤ x or y ≤ F(x, y) with x ∈ D, y ∉ D and x < y.*
- This is what a box-height potential would prove: every move stays on comparable pairs.
- It is formally stronger than Cut, and no reduction is known. From an incomparable witness, the obvious repairs y ↦ x ⊔ y and x ↦ x ⊓ y move W1 and W2 in the wrong direction.
- The theory is self-dual (x < y in α becomes y <ᵒᵈ x, and W1 and W2 swap), so WLOG F(⊥, ⊤) ∉ D stays sound.
- *Evidence (solver).*
  - The box theory, with no-witness asserted only for s < t (`box_cut.py cut`), is UNSAT for H = 2, 3, 4: 4, 7 and 33 terms (Cut needs 19 at H = 4).
  - It holds on every lattice of closure systems on 4 points, for every cut (`box_cut.py finite 4`): 932 pairs (L, D). Dropping W1 makes all 34 pairs at k = 3 fail, so the encoding is not vacuous.
  - H = 5: inconclusive. Still SAT at 453 terms after 9 min, while Cut itself needs 128 terms in 17 s. For this search, box Cut at H = 5 is about as hard as Cut at H = 6. `python3 box_cut.py cut 5 4000` is the external run that would decide it.
  - *Later (round 26, after §34.3z⁵⁹):* no finite box counterexample exists among bounded posets with ≤ 11 elements and edge-height ≤ 5 (`cut_finite.py 5 3 11 --box`, UNSAT for every n; 86 s at n = 11). Unlike Cut, box Cut has no finite-poset theorem behind it, so a closed SAT model of the box ground search would be a genuine finite counterexample. None is this small.
- *Status:* conjecture, with machine evidence through H = 4 (posets) and on small lattices. Not in Lean.

**Assessment.** The review's target, a measure on the box that bounds the iteration, is the right shape and the box-cell result is consistent with it. But the measure has to control escaped values. That is the same missing upper bound as in §34.3z¹² A and in the k = 3 strict switch (§34.3z¹⁴): an F-value with only lower bounds, so that no orbit-entry or label measure decreases. No candidate measure survives the restriction tests: covering boxes and descent boxes are both insufficient. Nothing here is proved.

## 34.3z¹⁶ Twenty-first brief (ChatGPT, Touhou v4 axioms as proof design): bouquet cuts, and why Route A cannot be tested finitely

The brief asks to turn the Danmaku-bialgebra axioms (instance tags, monotone fusion with a no-crossfire condition, seeded μ/ν, keeping non-compatible structures apart) into precise Mordor statements. It names three routes in priority order: A, joint commitment for v ⊔ v′ with v, v′ ∈ Max B(w) (§34.3z¹¹); B, FG∞ at k = 3; C, Ω × Ω × C₂.

**Audit.** The brief's statement of the frontier matches the source: `Frontier.frontier`, `cutMaps_iff_joinClosed`, the pairwise-generator reduction of §34.3z¹¹, and FG∞ for k ≤ 2 (§34.3z¹⁴).

**Route A cannot be decided by finite search.** The Cut Lemma holds on every finite lattice (§34.3m, `FinGenAll`), so R(F) = L there for every F.
- Every *qualitative* statement about R(F) is therefore true on all finite lattices: membership, join-closure, pairwise joins of generators, "v ⊔ v′ is reachable from w". Finite exhaustive tests cannot refute or support any of them.
- What finite search can test are *stage* statements, i.e. bounded delays. Those are refuted at every pair type (§34.3k, §34.3z¹¹).
- Likewise every down-set closed under a and b⁻¹ is all of L there, so "R relative to w" is no weaker. A joint-commitment lemma would need a proof that works on infinite chain-finite lattices directly, which is the Cut Lemma itself.

**The four principles, made precise.**
- *Instance tags (A1, B1–B2).* Tagged derivations of R(F) are paths of the move preorder u → v, v ∈ ↓u ∪ ↓a(u) ∪ b⁻¹(↓u) (§34.3z¹¹). The tags can always be forgotten: every closure rule has one premise, so the union of closed sets is closed. What the tags keep is stage information, and every stage-local join law is refuted.
- *Monotonicity and no crossfire (Br1–Br2).* For transformations of F, the two obligations are exactly `Skeleton.compress_mono` (monotone) and `Skeleton.compress` (witness-freeness transfers). The no-crossfire condition that is genuinely new is a condition on the *cut*. Two parts meet only below a common r: D = ↓a ∪ B with ↓a ∩ B ⊆ ↓r. This is what the k = 2 proof uses. See the theorem below.
- *Seeded μ/ν (C1–C3′).* R(F) is the μ-side seeded at ⊥, and the closed proper down-sets are the ν-side (`Reach`, `CutDuality`). The self-duality already in use is WLOG F(⊥, ⊤) ∉ D (`cut_of_wlog`). Nothing new.
- *Separate structures (D1′).* The brief's own example applies. ⋁R(F) = ⊤ does not give ⊤ ∈ R(F), and the gap between them is join-closure.

**Theorem (bouquet cuts; `FGTwo.cut_bouquet`, Lean, standard axioms only).** Let α be a bounded lattice with DCC and F monotone. Let (pᵢ)_{i ∈ ι} be any nonempty family, finite or infinite, with a common r such that r ≤ pᵢ and pᵢ ⊓ pⱼ ≤ r for i ≠ j. Let D = ⋃ᵢ ↓pᵢ with ⊤ ∉ D. Then some x ∈ D and y ∉ D have F(x, y) ≤ x or y ≤ F(x, y).
- *Proof.* The generic part of `FGTwo` (`down`, `l4`, `sandwich`) is restated with the second generator replaced by an arbitrary down-set InB, under the hypotheses D = ↓a ∪ InB and ↓a ∩ InB ⊆ ↓r. Its proof uses nothing else.
  - The h-orbit of ⊤, h = F(r, ·), ends at a D-value z (`orbit_end`). Then z ≤ p_a for some a, and z ≰ r by W1 at row r.
  - Put InB = ⋃_{j ≠ a} ↓p_j. Then ↓p_a ∩ InB ⊆ ↓r by the meet hypothesis, and `sandwich` with s = ⊤ gives the contradiction.
- `cut_two` is now the instance InB = ↓q, r = p ⊓ q, and k = 1 is the one-petal case.
- *Scope.* This is the first proved case with infinitely many maximal elements of D and no condition on the lattice beyond DCC: for example D = {⊥} ∪ (any set of atoms), or more generally any cut whose maximal elements pairwise meet in one point. It closes no named frontier case: whether the open cuts of Ω × Ω × C₂ or M_ω² × C₃ include bouquets has not been checked, and a general cut there has maximal elements with different pairwise meets. The ACC dual (Dᶜ a bouquet of principal filters joining pairwise in a common element) follows by the duality of §34.3h but is not stated in Lean.

**Route B, k = 3, restated as crossfire.** In the bouquet case an orbit value below two petals is below r, and W1 at row r excludes it.
- With three generators whose pairwise meets differ, a D-value can lie below p₁ and p₂ without lying below p₁ ⊓ p₂ ⊓ p₃. This is the label set Λ = {1, 2} of §34.3z¹⁴, and the strict switch is crossfire between petals.
- The next case beyond bouquets is a *laminar* family, e.g. p₁ ⊓ p₂ = c > b = p₁ ⊓ p₃ = p₂ ⊓ p₃. It is a bouquet over b with petals ↓p₃ and ↓p₁ ∪ ↓p₂, but the second petal is not principal, and `sandwich` needs a single row F(a, ·).
  - Running the two-generator argument inside that petal fails because the h-orbit of F(c, ·) may enter ↓p₃. That is a D-value outside the petal, where the petal's own witness-freeness says nothing.
  - *Status: open at the time; closed in §34.3z¹⁷ (`Laminar.cut_three_laminar`), by transporting the orbit end from b to c.*

**Route C.** Not pursued this round. The finite-window caveat of the brief is the content of §34.3z⁹.

## 34.3z¹⁷ New: laminar cuts — transport of the orbit end along r ≤ r′ (Lean)

§34.3z¹⁶ left the laminar family as the smallest open case beyond bouquets: p₁ ⊓ p₂ = c above b = p₁ ⊓ p₃ = p₂ ⊓ p₃. It closes, and so does every finite hierarchy of the same kind (`lean/Mordor/Laminar.lean`, standard axioms only).

**Transport lemma** (`Laminar.transport`). Let D = Q ∪ Q′ with Q, Q′ down-sets and Q ∩ Q′ ⊆ ↓r, and let r ≤ r′ both lie in D. Assume no witness. Take s ∉ D with F(r′, s) ≤ s. If the F(r, ·)-orbit of s ends in Q, then so does its F(r′, ·)-orbit.
- *Proof.* Induction on s (DCC). Put h = F(r, ·), w = F(r′, s); then h(s) ≤ w ≤ s since r ≤ r′.
- *Case w ∈ D.* The r′-orbit ends at once. Since h(s) ≤ w, the h-orbit of s also ends at once, at z = h(s) ∈ Q. If w ∈ Q′ then z ∈ Q ∩ Q′ ⊆ ↓r, which is W1 at row r.
- *Case w ∉ D.* Then w < s by W2 at r′. The h-orbit of w is squeezed, eₖ₊₁ ≤ hᵏ(w) ≤ eₖ, so it ends either below z or at a D-value y ≥ z. In the second case y ∉ Q′, else z ∈ Q′. Either way it ends in Q, and w satisfies F(r′, w) ≤ w, so the induction hypothesis applies.
- The only property of Q the proof uses is that Q is a down-set and *r-saturated*: a D-value above a Q-value not below r is again in Q. This is equivalent to a split D = Q ∪ Q′ over r.

**Laminar hierarchies** (`Laminar.Lam`, `cut_laminar`). Call a finite index set laminar if it is a single index, or a union S₁ ∪ S₂ of laminar sets whose cross meets pᵢ ⊓ pⱼ (i ∈ S₁, j ∈ S₂) lie below ⋀_{S₁∪S₂} p. If the whole index set is laminar and ⊤ ∉ D = ⋃ ↓pᵢ, every monotone F on a bounded DCC lattice has a witness.
- *Proof* (`lam_main`, induction on `Lam`). The state is (S, r): r lies below every pᵢ with i ∈ S, the cross meets out of S lie below r, and the F(r, ·)-orbit of ⊤ ends in ⋃_{i∈S} ↓pᵢ.
  - At a split, transport from r to r′ = ⋀_S p. The orbit end then lies in one part, which gives the state (Sₖ, r′). Its cross meets lie below r′: inside S by laminarity, outside S because they lie below r ≤ r′.
  - At a leaf {i}, `FGTwo.sandwich` with a = pᵢ and InB = ⋃_{j≠i} ↓pⱼ closes it.
  - The start is S = everything, r = ⋀ p, with the end supplied by `orbit_end`.
- **Three generators** (`cut_three_laminar`): if p₁ ⊓ p₃ ≤ p₂ and p₂ ⊓ p₃ ≤ p₁, the cut ↓p₁ ∪ ↓p₂ ∪ ↓p₃ has a witness. For three pairwise incomparable generators this is, up to renaming, exactly the laminar condition. A split with overlapping parts would force one generator below another.
- Finite bouquets are laminar (split off one petal at a time). `cut_bouquet` additionally allows infinitely many petals. An infinite laminar version, finite depth with infinite branching, would combine the two arguments; it is not formalized.

**Where the method stops: k = 3 with crossing meets.** Over b = p₁ ⊓ p₂ ⊓ p₃, a nontrivial b-saturated union of petals needs some generator p_k with pᵢ ⊓ p_k = pⱼ ⊓ p_k = b, which is the laminar condition. Over a larger r = pᵢ ⊓ pⱼ, the petal ↓pᵢ is r-saturated only if pᵢ ⊓ p_k ≤ pⱼ, the same condition again.
- So for crossing meets (no generator meets both others in b) the transport has nothing to move. This is the strict switch of §34.3z¹⁴: an orbit end with label set {1} can be squeezed to a value with labels {1, 3} or {1, 2}, and no saturated set separates them.
- A shape example: pᵢ = {1, 2, 3, 4} ∖ {i} for i = 1, 2, 3 in the Boolean lattice 2⁴. It is finite and so settled by `FinGenAll`, but in an infinite DCC lattice the same meet pattern is open.
- *Status.* FG∞ is proved for k ≤ 2, for every finite laminar family and for every bouquet. It is open for crossing meets, the smallest case being k = 3 with all three pairwise meets distinct from each other's cross meets.

## 34.3z¹⁸ Twenty-second brief: crossing meets — entry times, the column lemma, bags, and the reduced theory

The brief (ChatGPT, pinned to 670be43; the work below starts from that commit) targets the smallest open case of §34.3z¹⁷: D = ↓p₁ ∪ ↓p₂ ∪ ↓p₃ with crossing meets mᵢⱼ = pᵢ ⊓ pⱼ, r = p₁ ⊓ p₂ ⊓ p₃. It names three lateral strategies: orbit labels (lead), separator trees, uncrossing.

**Proved (`lean/Mordor/Crossing.lean`, standard axioms only).**
- *Lemmas A–C of the brief* (`endpoint_not_le`, `entry_mono`, `entry_eq_le`), for anchors a ≤ a′ in D.
  - The F(a, ·)-orbit of a column enters D at a value not below a.
  - It enters no later than the F(a′, ·)-orbit.
  - At equal entry times it enters below, so it carries every label of the other endpoint.
- **Column lemma** (`column_lemma`, any finite family). At every column y ∉ D, some generator row F(pᵢ, y) lies outside D.
  - *Proof.* Otherwise put S₀ = all indices and S_{n+1} = {j : ∃ i ∈ Sₙ, F(pᵢ, y) ≤ pⱼ}. This is a decreasing sequence of nonempty sets.
  - Where it stabilises, F(⋀_S p, y) ≤ pⱼ for every j ∈ S, i.e. F(m_S, y) ≤ m_S. That is W1 at row m_S.
  - This is the "compatibility at one argument" the brief asks for. It uses all meet rows at once.
- **Join-anchor sandwich** (`anchor_sandwich`). Put cᵢ = ⨆_{j≠i} pᵢ ⊓ pⱼ ≤ pᵢ. Then ↓pᵢ ∩ ⋃_{j≠i} ↓pⱼ ⊆ ↓cᵢ, so `FGTwo.sandwich` applies with anchor cᵢ.
  - For s ∉ D with F(pᵢ, s) ≤ s (for example ⊤), the F(cᵢ, ·)-orbit of s never enters D below pᵢ.
  - In the crossing case cᵢ is strictly above both mᵢⱼ. The splits {i} | {j, k} are saturated exactly over the anchors in [cᵢ, pᵢ], and over nothing lower.
- **Separator trees** (`Tree`, `Closes`, `tree_closes`, `cut_of_tree`). The laminar induction of §34.3z¹⁷ is restated with arbitrary bags at the leaves.
  - A bag S *closes* if for every anchor r below its generators, with cross meets to the outside below r, the F(r, ·)-orbit of ⊤ does not enter D inside ⋃_S ↓pᵢ.
  - If every bag closes, so does every node, by `Laminar.transport` at each split. Singletons close (`singleton_closes`), which recovers `cut_laminar`.
  - So FG∞ for all finite families reduces to *indecomposable* bags: sets with no split whose cross meets lie below the meet of the set.
  - For a crossing triple the bag property is the open statement itself. The tree theorem moves the problem to the bags; it does not solve one.

**Computational observations.**
- *Finite columns.* The reduced model of `kinf_k.py` has rows = the 7 meets of a free crossing triple, a finite column poset U with ⊤, and no lattice law on U. It is UNSAT for |U| ≤ 5 (|E| = 0, 2; |U| = 5 with |E| = 2 not finished).
  - Deletion-minimal cores (z3, tracked cells) are small. At |U| = 2 the core is 18 cells, all at one column: W1 at all 7 rows, W2 at the 3 generator rows, and monotonicity along the meet order. That is exactly the column lemma at a column below which U has nothing.
  - At |U| = 3 the core (59 cells) uses two incomparable columns u₀, u₁ whose generator rows point at each other, with ⊤ the only column above both. That is a finiteness artifact. In an infinite DCC lattice gᵢ(⊤) may lie above infinitely many such columns.
- *Bounded height* (`experiments/fgcut/crossground.py`). This is a ground refutation of the reduced theory: columns are terms over ⊤, rows are only the meets m_A, the meets are glbs (ground instances), D(x) ⟺ ∃i x ≤ pᵢ, and edge-height ≤ H. UNSAT means a proof that uses only the rows F(m_A, ·) exists at that height.

  | H | bouquet | laminar | crossing (meets may coincide) | strict crossing |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | 4 | 34 terms | 44 | 51 | 66 |
  | 5 | — | — | 133 terms, 18 s | 120 terms, 15 s |
  | 6 | — | — | **233 terms, 228 s** | **205 terms, 271 s** |
  | 7 | — | — | — | **362 terms, 3,196 s** (external run) |

  So any 3-generated cut has a witness at edge-height ≤ 6, and a strictly crossing one at edge-height ≤ 7 (DRAT-verified, §34.3z¹⁹; encoder correctness by inspection). The general H = 6 Cut Lemma is still open (§34.3h). By compactness, a counterexample to the reduced statement under DCC would need columns of unbounded height, the same island as the general frontier.
- *The endpoint table alone is consistent.* For the ten anchors r ≤ mᵢⱼ ≤ cᵢ ≤ pᵢ, Lemmas A–C and the anchor sandwich are satisfied by this assignment:
  - entry times 0, 1, 2, 3 by level;
  - endpoint labels {3} everywhere except J(c₃) = J(p₃) = {1}.
  - As the brief anticipated, finitely many labels give no descent. A transition lemma must use F-values at columns below the orbits.

**Uncrossing.** The exchange inequality the analogy needs, F(mᵢⱼ ⊔ mᵢₖ, y) ≤ F(mᵢⱼ, y) ⊔ F(mᵢₖ, y), fails for monotone F. Take F(x, y) = ⊤ if x ≥ cᵢ, else ⊥. Only ≥ holds. Under global no-witness every statement is vacuous on finite lattices (§34.3z¹⁶), so no exchange law follows from the hypotheses. Route dropped.

**The decisive step.** A column-indexed descent for an indecomposable bag. The column lemma says every column has an escaping generator row. The FG2 proof turns one escaping row into a descent by keeping s ∈ S_a (F(a, s) ≤ s) along the way. For a crossing triple the escaping row can change from column to column, so membership of S_a is lost exactly at the strict switch (§34.3z¹⁴). The obvious combined states do not survive one step:
- 𝒮 = ⋂ᵢ Sᵢ: it is meet-closed and contains ⊤, but gᵢ(s) need not lie in Sⱼ.
- The meet iteration y ↦ y ⊓ ⋀ gᵢ(y): it ends in D, by W2 at a fixed point, but not at a value in any Sᵢ.

**What the bounded-height certificates look like** (`experiments/fgcut/crossmin.py`: deletion-minimal, subterm-closed term sets; a word p₁·p₁·m₁₂₃·⊤ means F(p₁, F(p₁, F(m₁₂₃, ⊤)))).
- Strict crossing, H = 4: 57 → **32** terms. H = 5: 111 → **52**.
- They consist mostly of:
  - the generator orbits gᵢⁿ(⊤) as deep as the height allows (p₁⁵·⊤ at H = 5);
  - the meet rows m_A with i ∈ A at columns of the gᵢ-orbit (m₁₂·p₁³·⊤, m₁₂₃·p₂²·⊤, …), which are the rows that are also contracting there;
  - a few meet-row orbits (m₁₂·m₁₂·⊤, m₂₃·m₁₂·m₁₂·⊤) and one cross term g₁·g₂·⊤.
  - The r-orbit of ⊤, which drives the FG2 and laminar proofs, has length ≤ 2 in them.
- *Fixed schemas.* "Generator orbits to depth H, plus one layer of meet rows" does not refute (SAT at H = 4, 5). Two layers refute H = 4, with 653 terms against the 32 needed. So the certificates do not instantiate this schema.
- *Forward reading.* Adding the 32 terms layer by layer, the only literals forced before the final layer are gᵢ(⊤) ≰ pᵢ. The refutation is a global case analysis, not a chain of local consequences. This is the same situation as the general height certificates (§34.3z¹²).
- H = 7 (strict) was still SAT at 210 terms after 3 min here. The external run ended **UNSAT with 362 terms after 3,196 s**. Strict-crossing certificate sizes are 29, 66, 120, 205, 362 for H = 3…7. The ratio is about 1.75 per height and does not increase, unlike the general Cut certificates (7, 17, 86 for H = 3, 4, 5).

**Next experiment.** Near-models of unbounded height for the reduced theory, with columns shaped like Ω: chains of every length below the gᵢ(⊤). Either they become UNSAT at every truncation, which would give a candidate descent, or they exhibit an infinite reduced countermodel. The latter would show that crossing triples need rows outside the meets or lattice structure on the columns.

## 34.3z¹⁹ Twenty-third brief: switching, multiset reset, abstraction refinement — what the reduction keeps, and certificates

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint e076901, which was `main` when this round started) asks for a switching-composition lemma for escaping rows (lead), a multiset reset in the style of `IdealFinite`, and abstraction refinement with unbounded-height models. It also asks that the height-7 crossing result be verified or quarantined.

**Audit.** No source differences between e076901 and `main` at the start. The README said "edge-height ≤ 6" for the crossing triple while the log already had the external height-7 run. Both are now stated with their validation status (below).

**Certificates** (`experiments/sat/drat_certify.py`: Glucose 4 with DRAT logging, then drat-trim; `crossground.py … out.cnf` writes the clause set on UNSAT). All DRAT-verified here:

| run | clauses / variables | Glucose | DRAT core |
|---|---|---|---|
| strict, H = 4 | 74,819 / 4,752 | 0.1 s | 1,960 clauses, 263 lemmas |
| strict, H = 5 | 398,631 / 15,240 | 1.9 s | 13,746 clauses, 5,139 lemmas |
| crossing, H = 5 | 416,305 / 18,620 | 1.3 s | 12,098 clauses, 2,916 lemmas |
| **crossing, H = 6** | 2,007,206 / 56,153 | 25.9 s | 34,649 clauses, 22,288 lemmas |
| **strict, H = 6** | 1,891,727 / 43,665 | 55.4 s | 49,276 clauses, 38,295 lemmas |

- **Strict, H = 7: DRAT-verified.** The external run wrote `x7.cnf`: 7,495,784 clauses, 134,302 variables, sha256 `ef0623fb…5559f`.
  - Glucose 4 needs 1,370 s on it from scratch. drat-trim verifies the proof: core 148,262 clauses, 374,273 of 628,404 lemmas, 1.0·10⁸ resolution steps.
  - The clause set was regenerated here independently (`crossground.py 3 7 6000 strict`, CaDiCaL 1.5.3, pysat 1.9.dev16 against the external dev17). The file is byte-identical: same sha256, 362 terms. So the verified CNF is exactly the encoder's output.
- *Where the certificates spend their clauses.* In the H = 4 strict core (1,960 clauses): 966 are monotonicity, 354 transitivity, **338 height-bound (rank)**, 108 down-set, 120 D-definition and glb, and 74 no-witness.
  - The no-witness cells at deep columns are the generator row and *one* meet row on each generator orbit: p₁, m₁₂ at p₁·p₁·⊤; p₂, m₁₂ at p₂·p₂·⊤; p₃, m₁₃ at p₃·p₃·⊤.
  - So the bounded-height refutations lean on the height bound to cut the generator orbits short. A uniform proof has to replace exactly that use, the length of the gᵢ-descents, by DCC.
- *What a verified certificate proves.* The generated clause set is unsatisfiable. Each clause is an instance of an axiom true in every bounded lattice: monotonicity, D a down-set, D(x) ⟺ ∃i x ≤ pᵢ, meets as glbs, generator incomparability (and, for strict, incomparable pairwise meets), the height bound, and no-witness on meet rows. That correspondence is checked by inspection of the encoder, not in Lean, since `cut_lean.py` translates only the clause kinds of `cut_ground`.

**What the meet-row reduction loses** (`Crossing.meetRow`, `rows_to_meets`, Lean, standard axioms).
- Replacing every row x by ⋀{pᵢ : x ≤ pᵢ} keeps F monotone and witness-free. This needs no chain condition and is `Skeleton.compress` with κ = id and a finite meet for ρ.
- So for a finitely generated cut, a counterexample may be assumed to read only the 2ᵏ − 1 meet rows. The reduced theory forgets *nothing about rows*.
- It does forget the *lattice structure among columns*: joins and meets of columns, and the positions of D-values relative to them.
- This answers the brief's question 4.1 exactly. Of its two possible losses, (1) "values of F outside the meet rows" is not a loss, and (2) "column structure" is the only one. A refinement must add column meets and joins, or nothing.

**Lead strategy: composition of escapes.**
- *`no_closed_rows`* (Lean) is the closed-set form of the column lemma. At a column y ∉ D, no nonempty set S of generator rows has all its values below generators in S. For three generators, two rows that stop at y never point at each other.
- *`escape_below`* (Lean). If row i contracts at y (F(pᵢ, y) ≤ y), every column x ≤ y outside D has F(pᵢ, x) < y. So an escape path from y that uses only rows contracting at y stays strictly below y.
  - This is the composition law that monotonicity gives, and it is sharp.
  - At a switch, from row i which stops at z to an escaping row j, the new column F(pⱼ, z) is comparable with z only when j contracts at z. No combination of monotonicity, the lattice laws and (NW) *at the cells of the path* says more, because all of them are local and the next value may be placed anywhere outside ↑z.
  - A switching lemma stronger than `escape_below` therefore needs global information: membership of z in Sⱼ = {y : F(pⱼ, y) ≤ y}, which is the strict-switch statement of §34.3z¹⁴.
- *Falsification.* Under global (NW) every intermediate statement is vacuous on finite lattices, where Cut holds. With local hypotheses (NW on the path's cells only), any "switch forces a comparison" claim fails trivially: assign F(pⱼ, z) to a fresh column incomparable with z. That is consistent with every local constraint, including the meet rows at z, which only need values below F(pⱼ, z) and F(pᵢ, z).
- *Status.* The switching route reduces to the same missing global inequality. Proved: `no_closed_rows`, `escape_below`. Refuted as local statements: every stronger switching law.

**Multiset reset (`IdealFinite`).**
- The reset there needs two finite sets: the anchor rows, and the values of each pinned row (`↓F(h, ⊤)` finite).
- With `rows_to_meets` the first is free, since there are 2ᵏ − 1 meet rows. The second asks for each meet row to take finitely many values on the columns. That holds only below finite principal ideals, which is the hypothesis of `cut_of_ideal_finite` itself. A crossing triple in an infinite-width lattice has no such bound.
- So the reset gives no new route unless the row images can be compressed, and column compression (`Skeleton.kappa`) keeps infinitely many columns.
- *Status.* The obstruction is the infinite row image, not the anchors.

**Unbounded-height near-models.** Any reduced model of edge-height ≤ H (the whole poset, D-part included) is refuted by the bounded-height runs: verified to H = 6, solver-only at H = 7. A truncation of an Ω-shaped column poset is not a model, since values point into the removed parts. The only first-order approximations are the height-bounded theories themselves, which are refuted. A reduced countermodel would need columns of unbounded height, *and* by `rows_to_meets` it would then be a genuine counterexample once the column lattice laws are added. So refinement means adding column joins and meets to `crossground.py`. That makes the theory stronger, which can only make refutation easier; it cannot produce a countermodel the meet-row search missed.

**Next action.** Proof-theoretic rather than computational. The verified certificates at H = 4…6 have DRAT cores of 2·10³ to 5·10⁴ clauses. Extracting from them which *columns* carry the contradiction (as was done for k = 2 with `kinf.py`, where the cores showed the two-row sandwich) is the most direct way to find the S-state the strict switch needs.

## 34.3z²⁰ Twenty-fourth brief: join saturation, Skolemized DCC, and an erratum

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint 5aa9623 = `main` at the start of the round) asks to extract a height-independent argument from the verified crossing certificates (A), to try join saturation with minimal fixed points (B), and to restore the column lattice structure (C).

**Erratum to §34.3z¹⁴.** The informal "L4 generalizes by downward induction on |A|" has a gap. If g_A(u) ∈ D, W1 at row m_A gives Λ(g_A u) ⊉ A, not Λ(g_A u) ⊄ A. With ∅ ≠ Λ(g_A u) ⊊ A the label set does not grow. This is exactly the strict-switch configuration (A = {1, 2}, Λ = {1}). So that lemma is proved only in the two-generator form `FGTwo.l4`. Nothing in Lean depended on it.

**B. Join saturation** (`lean/Mordor/Saturation.lean`, standard axioms). Put J(y) = y ⊔ ⨆ᵢ gᵢ(y) and 𝒮 = {y ∉ D : ∀i gᵢ(y) ≤ y}, the common contracting columns. ⊤ ∈ 𝒮.
- `sat_le_iff`: J(y) ≤ y iff y ∈ 𝒮 (for y ∉ D), so the fixed points of J outside D are exactly 𝒮.
- `corridor` (B2): if gᵢ(c) ≤ c, row i contracts strictly at every column of [gᵢ(c), c] outside D.
- `amalgam` (C2): joining y with escaping row values keeps those rows escaping.
- **`min_common_join`** (DCC only, no ACC). A minimal c ∈ 𝒮 exists (`exists_min_common`), and c = g₁(c) ⊔ g₂(c) ⊔ g₃(c).
  - The join is outside D by the column lemma, and every row contracts on it because it lies below c. Minimality forces equality.
  - This makes the brief's B3 ("the saturation ascent from gᵢ(c) returns to c") hold without ACC. Any element of 𝒮 between gᵢ(c) and c is c.
- *Where it stops, checked on k = 2.* For D = ↓p ∪ ↓q at a minimal c ∈ 𝒮, if one row stays in D, `FGTwo.l4` gives a contradiction at once (F(r, c) ≤ g_q(c) ≤ p and g_p(c) ≤ c).
  - If both rows escape, c = d_p ⊔ d_q with dᵢ = gᵢ(c) ∈ U, and minimality says nothing further. Closing this case needs the orbit sandwich again.
  - For k = 3 even the first case is the erratum's gap. If g₃(c) ∈ D with Λ ⊆ {1, 2}, the two-generator l4 does not apply.
  - So saturation organises the descent around one column, c = ⨆ gᵢ(c), but needs the same orbit information as before. The missing implication: from a minimal c ∈ 𝒮 with c = ⨆ gᵢ(c), produce c′ < c in 𝒮, or a witness.

**Join anchors carry no information after compression.** Under `rows_to_meets` the row cᵢ = ⨆_{j≠i} mᵢⱼ becomes ⋀{p_k : cᵢ ≤ p_k} = pᵢ. So for a compressed counterexample `anchor_sandwich` is W1 at row pᵢ. Since compression is lossless, any argument may assume the counterexample is compressed. The cᵢ add nothing beyond W1.

**A. Height-independent extraction: Skolemized DCC** (`experiments/fgcut/dccground.py`).
- The reduced theory is run with no height bound. DCC is supplied instead as constants m_R, each minimal in X_R = {y ∉ D : F(m_A, y) ≤ y for all A ∈ R}, for every antichain R of meet rows, with ground minimality instances for every term. These hold in every DCC lattice, so an UNSAT here would be a height-independent proof.
- *Calibration fails.* Even the bouquet triple, a theorem (`cut_bouquet`), is still SAT at 400 terms; so is the laminar triple. The DCC proofs that exist (`orbit_end`, `l4`, `sandwich`, `transport`) induct over sets defined by *row values below a generator* and *orbit endpoints*, not by contraction alone.
  - The orbit-endpoint condition ("the h-orbit of s ends below pᵢ", some n) is not a finite formula.
  - That is why every bounded-height certificate spends a large share of its clauses on rank constraints (§34.3z¹⁹): the height bound substitutes for these inductions.
- A faithful Skolemization would need minimal constants for orbit-defined sets, with existential orbit lengths. That turns the ground search into a search over induction schemas. It is not attempted here.

**C. Column joins.** `amalgam` is the valid join law. Adding column joins and meets to the encoding strengthens a theory that is already refuted at every bounded height. It could only shorten certificates, not remove their dependence on the height bound, which is the induction issue above. Not run.

**Proved DCC lemmas as clause families: nothing to add for the strict triple.** The DCC lemmas in Lean are `orbit_end`, `FGTwo.sandwich`/`l4`, `Laminar.transport` and `anchor_sandwich`. Their instances need a split D = ↓a ∪ InB, or D = Q ∪ Q′, that is saturated over a meet anchor. For a strictly crossing triple no such split exists over any meet, by `rows_to_meets` and the saturation remark of §34.3z¹⁷. The only remaining instances are the join-anchor ones, which are W1 after compression. So adding these families to the strict-crossing search adds no clauses. (They would remove the height dependence for the bouquet and laminar shapes, where they *are* the proofs.)

**No finite countermodel to the meet-row theory** (`experiments/fgcut/finred.py`; complete SAT search, no height bound). The elements are ⊥, the seven meets of a strictly crossing triple (as glbs), E extra D-elements and N columns including ⊤. The order is free apart from that; rows are the meets, F is monotone and witness-free. Columns are numbered along a linear extension to break symmetry.

| N columns | E = 0 | E = 1 | E = 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| ≤ 6 | UNSAT | — | UNSAT (N = 6: 174 s) |
| 7 | UNSAT (12 s) | UNSAT (51 s) | — |
| 8 | UNSAT (414 s) | — | — |

- These finite models may have any height, so this complements the bounded-height refutations, which cover every model, finite or infinite, of edge-height ≤ 7.
- A finite model would have shown that the poset version is strictly weaker than the lattice problem: Cut holds on finite lattices, and `min_common_join` uses column joins. None appears.
- So the meet-row theory over posets shows no sign of being too weak. The missing step looks like an induction principle, not missing lattice structure.

**Status.** Proved: the saturation lemmas above. Open, and now precisely located: an induction principle over orbit-defined sets for the crossing triple. It amounts to a replacement for `FGTwo.sandwich` whose state survives the strict switch. Next step: generate the bounded-height certificates with the *proved* DCC lemmas (`orbit_end`, the laminar `transport` where its hypothesis holds, the singleton l4) added as clause families. Then measure which share of the rank clauses each one replaces. A family that removes the height dependence at H = 4…6 points at the missing induction.

## 34.3z²¹ Twenty-fifth brief: orbit-aware DCC — minimal states, a faithful Skolemization, and a DCC-lemma tester

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint 10151f3 = `main` at the start) asks for DCC as minimality on orbit-defined sets, with the strict switch built into the set, and for the transition that a minimal element would need.

**Minimal states and their normal form** (informal derivation; the minimality step is in Lean).
- Let h = g₁₂₃ and 𝓢 = ⋃_A X(A), where X(A) = {s ∉ D : g_A(s) ≤ s and the h-orbit of s enters D at a value z with Λ(z) ⊇ A}.
- ⊤ ∈ 𝓢: ⊤ ∈ S_A for every A; take A = Λ(z_⊤). So DCC gives a minimal s*. Let m be the length of its h-orbit (e₀ = s*, …, e_m ∉ D, z = h(e_m) ∈ D), L = Λ(z), and 𝒜 = {A ⊆ L : s* ∈ S_A}. 𝒜 is nonempty and upward closed in the subsets of L, so L ∈ 𝒜. Put w_A = g_A(s*).
- *m ≥ 1.* Then w_A ∈ U and h(s*) ≤ w_A ≤ s*, so the h-orbit of w_A is squeezed between consecutive eₖ.
  - Either it ends below z: then w_A ∈ X(A) below s*, contradicting minimality.
  - Or it ends at y_A ∈ [z, e_m] with Λ(y_A) ⊆ L, and minimality forces A ⊄ Λ(y_A).
  - With A = L: ∅ ≠ Λ(y_L) ⊊ L, so |L| = 2 (|L| = 3 is W1 at m₁₂₃), say L = {1, 2}, Λ(y₁₂) = {1}.
  - Then {1} ∉ 𝒜: y₁₂ ≤ y₁ forces Λ(y₁) = {1} ⊇ {1}.
- *m = 0.* Every w_A (A ∈ 𝒜) lies in D, since otherwise h(w_A) ≤ z makes w_A a smaller member of X(A). Its labels form a nonempty proper subset of L.
  - So again L = {1, 2}, Λ(w₁₂) = {1} and s* ∉ S₁. Moreover g₁(s*) ∈ U, since a D-value above w₁₂ would have labels ⊆ {1} not containing 1.
  - **`MinSwitch.interval_in_D`** (Lean, standard axioms): every column of [w₁₂, s*) lies in D, hence below p₁ only. A column x outside D there would satisfy g₁₂(x) ≤ w₁₂ ≤ x and h(x) ≤ z, a smaller member of X({1, 2}).
  - In particular s* ⊓ g₁(s*) ∈ D ∩ ↓p₁. This uses a column meet.
- **Normal form.** The orbit end has two labels {1, 2}. s* ∈ S₁₂ ∖ S₁. The switch value, w₁₂ for m = 0 or y₁₂ for m ≥ 1, has labels exactly {1}.
- This is the brief's SwitchBad configuration, now derived from minimality rather than assumed. The missing transition is a smaller member of 𝓢. Such a member would follow from g₁(w₁₂) ≤ w₁₂, i.e. w₁₂ ∈ S₁, and nothing above gives that.

**A faithful Skolemization of orbit-defined DCC** (`experiments/fgcut/orbmin.py`).
- For a family of triples (A, B, C) with A ⊆ B, put X(A, B, C) = {x ∉ D : g_A(x) ≤ x, and the g_B-orbit of x enters D at a value with labels ⊇ C}.
- T_m is the reduced theory with *no height bound*, plus:
  - a constant s in the union of the family, with its g_B₀-orbit of length exactly m;
  - the instances "x < s ⇒ x ∉ X(A, B, C)" for every term x, triple and orbit length.
- For x < s the g_B-orbit of x is bounded by that of s, so these instances are exactly the minimality of s. Every clause is valid in a DCC lattice. An UNSAT T_m is a height-independent proof that the minimal element cannot have orbit length m.
- If the family contains (B, B, C), then g_B₀(s) ∈ X(B₀, B₀, C₀) whenever m ≥ 1, so only m = 0 remains. This is the "shift" closure.
- *Calibration* (all with no height bound):

  | shape | family | m = 0 | m = 1 | m = 2 | m = 3 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | bouquet | X(A, ⋀, A), all A | 23 terms | 31 | 39 | — |
  | laminar | X({3}, h, {3}) (sandwich at b) | 15 | 25 | 31 | 37 |
  | laminar | X({1}, m₁₂, {1}) (sandwich at c) | 14 | 23 | 28 | 33 |
  | laminar | X({1}, h, {1}) (no saturated split) | SAT at 500 | | | |
  | strict | X({1}, h, {1}), X({1}, m₁₂, {1}), X({1,2}, h, {1,2}) | SAT at 500 | | | |

  - So the tester finds the sandwich inductions, with certificates growing linearly in m. It separates valid sandwiches from invalid ones, and it confirms that no single-orbit family closes for the strict triple.
  - The laminar *theorem* is not one family. It is three: the two sandwiches plus transport, whose state carries two orbit conditions ("the h-orbit ends in P, the m₁₂-orbit ends outside P"). That is why the union families fail on laminar.

**Status.** Proved: `interval_in_D`. Derived informally: the normal form. Machine-checked by the tester: the single-orbit families for the strict triple do not close at the sizes tried. Open: a family with several orbit conditions, transport-like, that closes for the strict triple.

**Next experiment.** Enumerate two-orbit families. These are X(A; B₁ : 𝓛₁; B₂ : 𝓛₂), where 𝓛ᵢ is a set of allowed end-label sets for the g_Bᵢ-orbit. Run each through the tester, check transport's family on laminar first, and then the finite covering condition at ⊤. Each refuted family is a DCC lemma. A cover of ⊤'s profiles by refuted families would prove the strict triple.

### 34.3z²¹ (continued): two-orbit families

`experiments/fgcut/orbfam.py` extends the tester to several orbit conditions.
- A family is X(A; B₁ : 𝓛₁; …) = {x ∉ D : g_A(x) ≤ x, and the g_{Bⱼ}-orbit of x enters D at a value whose exact label set lies in 𝓛ⱼ}, with Bⱼ ⊇ A.
- The state s gets explicit orbit lengths (m₁, …). Minimality instances cover every term and every combination of orbit lengths. There is no height bound.

**Calibration: transport.** The laminar transport state X(m₁₂; h : P-labels; m₁₂ : {3}) is refuted for (m₁, m₂) = (0,0), (0,1), (1,0), (1,1), (2,1), in 10–15 terms. An invalid sandwich stays SAT. So the tester finds all three laminar inductions.

**Strict triple, contraction row m₁₂, orbit rows h and m₁₂** (all exact label pairs, orbit lengths ≤ 1, 300-term budget; 64 runs).
- The refutations at (1, 0) are Lemma B (the h-orbit cannot enter later). Those at equal times with L_h ⊉ L₁₂ are Lemma C. Both are entry-time consequences already in Lean.
- At (0, 1), where the h-orbit enters one step earlier, every profile whose h-end has *two* labels is refuted, in 19 terms. Most single-label h-ends are not.
- The profile that the normal form of the minimal strict-switch state lands in is (h : {1, 2}, m₁₂ : {1}) at equal times. It is not refuted at (0, 0) or (1, 1), even with a 3,000-term budget for 9 minutes.
- The laminar control with the same profile is not refuted either. In laminar this profile is closed only at ⊤, by composing the sandwich and transport, never by its own induction.

**Strict triple, contraction row p₁** (orbit rows h and m₁₂; 52 runs).
- The critical profile X(p₁; h : {1, 2}; m₁₂ : {1}) is **refuted at (0, 0) in 20 terms**. Here is the argument, read off by hand. g₁(s) lies above g₁₂(s), which has labels {1}, so g₁(s) ∉ D. Hence x = g₁(s) < s is in S₁. h(x) ≤ h(s) keeps the h-end labels {1, 2}. g₁₂(x) ≤ g₁₂(s) gives labels {1} or {1, 3}, and {1, 3} would put h(x) ≤ g₁₂(x) below all three generators. So x has the same profile, an infinite descent.
- At (1, 1) it is **not** refuted (SAT at 300 terms; a 4,000-term run for 9 minutes did not finish). Repeating the argument, the h-orbit of x = g₁(s) is squeezed and may end above z with labels {1} or {2}. That is the strict switch once more.
- Of the 52 runs, the other refutations are (0, 1) profiles and Lemma C cases.

**Reading.** The tester decides, per orbit length, which candidate DCC lemmas are true. For the strict triple every lemma found so far closes at orbit length 0, and fails or is undecided at length ≥ 1 exactly where the h-orbit of the stepped column can change its label set. The missing ingredient is therefore unchanged, but it is now isolated in machine-checkable form. One would need a family that also constrains the h-orbit ends of the columns gᵢ(s), i.e. a third orbit condition one step down, together with a proof that the resulting family is closed.

## 34.3z²² Twenty-sixth brief: nested orbits — the length-one transition, a descent graph, and the terminal obstruction

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint c7fbd68 = `main` at the start) asks to follow the orbits of the stepped column gᵢ(s), derive the transition of the critical profile, and either cover all profiles or locate the surviving case.

**The critical profile and its transition** (hand derivation; the order facts used are `MinSwitch.squeeze`, `entry_after_step`, `escapes_of_low_labels` and `Crossing.entry_mono`/`entry_eq_le`, all in Lean).
- *P:* s ∉ D with g₁(s) ≤ s. Its h-orbit enters D at step 2 with labels {1, 2}, and its g₁₂-orbit enters at step 2 with labels {1}.
- *The step.* Put x = g₁(s). Then x ≥ g₁₂(s) ∉ D, so x ∉ D, x < s (W2), and g₁(x) ≤ x.
  - Both orbits of x are squeezed (`squeeze`, rows h ≤ g₁ and g₁₂ ≤ g₁). Each enters one step earlier at a value above the old endpoint, or at the same step at a value below it (`entry_after_step`).
  - Entry-time monotonicity: the h-orbit is not later than the g₁₂-orbit, and at equal times its labels contain the g₁₂ labels.
- *The outcomes for x:*

  | h-end of x | g₁₂-end of x | profile | status |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | step 2, {1, 2} | step 2, {1} | P | smaller member: descent |
  | step 1, {1, 2} | step 1, {1} | P₀ | refuted (§34.3z²¹: g₁ escapes, same profile, infinite descent) |
  | step 1, {1, 2} | step 2, {1} or {1, 3} | R | **refuted** (below) |
  | step 1, {1} | step 1, {1} | Q₁ | not refuted at 300 terms |
  | step 1, {1} | step 2, {1} or {1, 3} | Q₂, Q₃ | not refuted at 300 terms |
  | step 1, {2} | step 2, {1} | Q₄ | not refuted at 300 terms |
  | step 1, {2} | step 2, {1, 3} | Q₅ | refuted by the tester (28 terms) |

- *R is refuted.* For x in R, the column x′ = g₁(x) lies above g₁₂(x) ∉ D. Squeezing again sends x′ to R or to P₀. In R and P₀, g₁ always escapes: its value lies above a g₁₂-value that is outside D or has only label 1 (`escapes_of_low_labels`). So these states never stop descending, which contradicts DCC.

**The profile system is a descent graph, so cycles are harmless.** Every edge is a g₁-step, a strict descent in L (the brief's soundness case 1). Any path is finite, and the only states that need a separate argument are the *terminal* ones, where g₁(x) ∈ D.
- At a terminal state, J = Λ(g₁(x)) ⊆ {2, 3} is nonempty, and every row g_A with 1 ∈ A has a D-value at x with labels ⊇ J (it lies below g₁(x)).
- A g₁₂-end at step 1 with labels ⊆ {1} rules terminality out (`escapes_of_low_labels`).
- If the sandwich invariant "1 is in the h-end" survives the descent, the terminal state is **T: g₁(x) ≤ p_j only, and h(x) has labels {1, j}**.
- For k = 2 this is the end of `FGTwo.l4`, since there h(x) would lie below p₁ ⊓ p_j = r. For three generators, m₁ⱼ ≠ r and nothing follows.
- *Tester* (`orbfam.py`, minimal-element families, no height bound): X(p₁; h : {1, 2}; g₁ : {2}), X(p₁; h : {2}; g₁ : {2}), X(p₁; h : {2, 3}; g₁ : {2, 3}) and the union form X(p₁; h ∋ 1; g₁ ∌ 1) are **not refuted** at 400 terms. The one refuted variant was a Lemma C triviality.
