**Diagnosis.** The nested profile language cannot close the strict triple. Its graph is well founded, with every edge a descent, but it has a genuine unresolved *terminal* node, T.
- At T the contracting row p₁ is exhausted, and the descent can continue only on row p_j. That needs x ∈ S_j, which no profile of orbits seeded at s or g₁(s) records or forces.
- This is the strict switch in its terminal form. The obstruction is not a cycle but an exit from the single-row descent.
- A closing argument must either show that T forces x ∈ S_j for the escaping row j (a statement about rows other than the contracting one, so outside the profile language), or bring in a second descent that starts at T.

**Addendum: at T the escaping row is p_j, but its contraction is the open point.** At T (j = 2), g₂(x) lies above h(x), whose labels are {1, 2}. A D-value of g₂ would have labels ⊆ {1, 2} without 2, i.e. {1}, and rows 1 and 2 would form a closed set (`no_closed_rows`). So v = g₂(x) ∉ D.
- If v ≤ x, the descent continues on row 2. It may alternate between rows 1 and 2, but every step is strict.
- At a minimal T-type state with v ≰ x, the meet x ⊓ v is a smaller candidate. It contracts on row 1 iff g₁(x ⊓ v) ≤ v, and on row 2 iff g₂(x ⊓ v) ≤ x, and neither follows.
- So the obstruction is exactly the inequality v ≤ x.
- The whole project builds (`lake build`, 1,062 jobs), with no `sorry` and no added axioms.

**Status.** Proved (Lean): `squeeze`, `entry_after_step`, `escapes_of_low_labels`. Proved by hand from them: the transition table, the refutations of P₀ and R, and the descent-graph structure. Machine observation: Q₅ is refuted; Q₁–Q₄ and the terminal T are not refuted at 300–400 terms. Open: T.

## 34.3z²³ Twenty-seventh brief: the terminal cross-row transfer — proved consequences and a local countermodel

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint 4b48f78 = `main` at the start) states the candidate lemma `terminal_cross_row_transfer`. At the terminal state T of §34.3z²², with v = g₂(x) and, in case II (v ≰ x), w = x ⊓ v, it asks for one of: a witness, v ≤ x, or the cross-row transfer (CT) g₁(w) ≤ v or g₂(w) ≤ x.

**Proved** (`lean/Mordor/MinSwitch.lean`: `Terminal`, `terminal_v_notin`, `terminal_meet`, `terminal_transfer`; no axioms at all).
- *v ∉ D.* A D-value of g₂ at x would lie below p₂ (W1 at p₂), below p₃, or below p₁.
  - Below p₃ is excluded because h(x) ≤ g₂(x) is not below p₃.
  - Below p₁ is excluded because then g₁₂(x) lies below p₁ (via g₂) and below p₂ (via g₁), so g₁₂(x) ≤ m₁₂, which is W1 at row m₁₂.
- *The meet, in case II.* w < x (from x ≰ v, W2 at row p₂) and w < v.
  - h(x) ≤ w and g₁₂(x) ≤ w.
  - g₁(w) ≤ x, and g₂(w) ≤ v. The latter comes from w ≤ x, not from w ≤ v as the brief has it.
  - If w ∈ D, then w lies below p₂ only: w ≤ p₁ would put g₁₂(x) ≤ m₁₂, and w ≤ p₃ would put h(x) ≤ p₃.
- *(CT) is exactly contraction at w.* g₁(w) ≤ v gives g₁(w) ≤ w, and g₂(w) ≤ x gives g₂(w) ≤ w (`terminal_transfer`). For w ∉ D, W2 at row p₂ already says g₂(w) ≱ w.

**Local countermodel to (CT)** (`experiments/fgcut/ctlocal.py`).
- *The encoding.* The elements are ⊥, the seven meets of a strictly crossing triple (as glbs), E extra D-elements, and columns x, v, w = x ⊓ v (as glb), ⊤ and N extra columns.
  - F is monotone on all rows and all columns. No-witness is imposed only at the cells of the listed columns.
  - It adds the terminal hypotheses at x, v = g₂(x), case II, and the negation of (CT).

  | no-witness at | N, E | w ∉ D | w ∈ D |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | x, w | 0, ≤ 2 / ≤ 3, 0 | UNSAT | SAT (0, 1) |
  | x, w | 1, 2 | **SAT** | — |
  | x, v, w, ⊤ | 1, 2 · 2, 2 · 1, 3 | **SAT** | SAT (1, 2) |
  | every column | 1, 2 | UNSAT (vacuous: no finite model at all, §34.3z²⁰) | — |

- *A minimal countermodel* (no-witness at x, v, w, ⊤; one extra column c₀; extras e₀, e₁):
  - g₁ = p₂ at x, v, w (labels {2}), so g₁(w) = p₂ ≰ v.
  - g₂(x) = v and g₂(w) = g₂(v) = c₀ < v with c₀ ≰ x.
  - h = m₁₂ at x, v, w (labels {1, 2}).
  - The cells at c₀ = g₂(w) carry no no-witness constraint.
- So (CT) is *not* a consequence of the constraints at x, v, w, ⊤. Any proof must use no-witness at the next column, g₂(w): the induction has to continue below w.

**Incidence and enriched state (the brief's second and third programs).** In the countermodel the incidence pattern at w is the one at x: rows 1 and 3 go to petal 2, row 2 escapes, and h has labels {1, 2}. But g₁(w) ≰ w, so w carries no contracting row.
- So the incidence relation does not separate x from w. Moving to the meet regenerates T's labels and loses contraction.
- An enriched descent with a meet transition therefore has the terminal node again, now without a contracting row. Incidence alone is insufficient, as the brief anticipated.

**Status.** Proved: the three lemmas above. Refuted: (CT) as a local lemma, by a minimal countermodel. The open implication is unchanged in substance: at T, some row contracts at a column below x, and the proof must look at least one column beyond w.

## 34.3z²⁴ The Go axiom framework (v3/v4) read for proof strategies

The document axiomatizes Go in μ (inductive), ν (coinductive) and dialgebraic layers, with superko, Benson certificates, seki as a νμ alternation, and ladders. Read against the open step, the terminal row switch of §34.3z²²–²³:

| Go notion | Mordor counterpart | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| μ-layer: capture, reading as well-founded trees | DCC descents; ground refutations and DRAT certificates | already present |
| ν-layer: life as a gfp; Benson's two-eye certificate | the gfp/closed sets of `Reach`, `CutDuality`; a certificate of "no witness" would be vacuous, since Cut holds on finite lattices | no new content |
| ladder, a forced line | an escape path: `column_lemma` forces an escaping row at every column | already present |
| seki as νμ alternation; parity progress measures | descents within a row (μ) alternating with row switches (ν). A progress measure for that game *is* the missing induction | restatement |
| **superko, history-first states**: legality is a property of (position, history), never of the position | sideways escapes have no measure on columns alone; the *meet of the history* does | **one new lemma** |

**History meet** (`lean/Mordor/Saturation.lean`).
- **`theta_contract`** (no axioms): if row a contracts at y, the meet row a ⊓ b contracts at y ⊓ F(b, y).
- **`theta_terminal`** (DCC). Start at ⊤ and repeatedly replace y by y ⊓ gⱼ(y) while that stays outside D, recording the meet of the rows used. Each step is strict (W2), so the walk stops.
  - It stops at a state y ∉ D with a contracting row a ∈ D such that y ⊓ gⱼ(y) ∈ D for *every* generator j.
  - This is the θ-descent of §34.3z¹⁴ with the missing ingredient supplied: the contracting row is carried along as the meet of the rows used.
- *The terminal state is locally consistent.* In the near-model encoding of §34.3z²³ (`experiments/fgcut/thetaloc.py`) it is SAT, with the triple-meet row contracting at y and every common lower bound of y and gⱼ(y) in D, for no-witness at y alone, at y and ⊤, or at four columns with one or two extra columns.
  - So the descent ends in a configuration that only the global hypotheses can exclude. It is not a proof; it is a third normal form for the same obstruction, alongside the minimal 𝓢-state (§34.3z²¹) and the terminal state T (§34.3z²²).

**Verdict.** Of the framework's ideas only history-first states give a lattice statement that is new and true. The rest are relabellings of existing machinery (well-founded readings, gfp certificates, forced lines) or of the open step (νμ alternation). None supplies the cross-row inequality.

## 34.3z²⁵ The Bombadil bialgebra (v4) read for proof strategies

The document is a partial bialgebra (μ merge, ν scatter) with a Loday dialgebra layer, bounds, a gfp/lfp section and an explicit structure-constant model. Its organizing principle, the **Bent Law**, says the classical laws hold on a calm sector and fail exactly at a listed set of cells. Read against the open terminal row switch (§34.3z²²–²⁴):

| Bombadil notion | Mordor counterpart | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| B5 eldest bound: a well-ordered age with an attained minimum | DCC minimality, used for the minimal 𝓢-state (§34.3z²¹) and in `theta_terminal` | already present |
| Hopf subalgebra "invertible until the Forest's edge" | the laminar subfamily, where sandwich and transport close; the antipode does not extend over the crossing | restatement |
| Two-Hands Lemma: absorption and expulsion are one event seen from the two sides | the W1/W2 pair, and the per-F self-duality Φ ⊣ c∘Ψ∘c (§33.3) | already present. Under DCC alone the up-set form is not available as a descent. |
| Column (dol) view: Kleene iteration of d ↦ F(d, c) from ⊥ | a chain in D = ⋃↓pᵢ has a cofinal part under one pᵢ, so its join stays in D. The iteration therefore cannot stay in D without reaching a W1 fixed point, and it exits D at a successor step. | `column_lemma` |
| T3 no-go: no word of the prop 𝒫 completes the quest | local countermodels: a configuration closed under the proof moves considered (§34.3z²³–²⁴) | already the method |
| gfp/lfp, terminal coalgebra vs initial algebra | the subject of the whole program | — |
| **Bent Law: the laws fail exactly at a computed set of cells** | where must no-witness fail in a finite near-model of the terminal state? | **tested below: not localized** |

**The bent-cell experiment** (`experiments/fgcut/bentcells.py`).
- *Setup.* This is the §34.3z²³ encoding, with no-witness imposed at every column, which has no finite model. Each W1/W2 clause at each cell is made soft. MaxSAT (RC2) then gives the minimum number of clauses that must be dropped, and the enumeration lists every minimum set.
- If the bending were forced into one structural position, an induction would have its target. Two cases would decide it:
  - Always at a column strictly below x: the defect is pushed down, and DCC can close.
  - Possibly at a column above x or beside it: the defect can escape, so the configuration is compatible with an infinite counterexample.
- *Results* (N = 1 extra column, E = 2 extra D-elements, w ∉ D; minimum = 1 bent clause in every mode):

  | hypotheses | single bent cells | columns that carry them |
  |---|---|---|
  | none (base theory only) | 29 | x, v, w, ⊤, c₀ (c₀ below x, beside x, or beside everything) |
  | T + case II | 17 | v, w, c₀. **Never x or ⊤.** |
  | T + case II + ¬CT | 11 | v, w, c₀ |

- *Reading.* The terminal hypotheses do one thing. They make x and ⊤ calm: a single-cell defect at x or at ⊤ alone is impossible at this size. Otherwise the defect may sit at v = g₂(x), which is beside x, as well as at w < x or at a column below x.
  - Example: W2 at (p₂, v), i.e. v ≤ g₂(v). This is the contraction-or-fixation of the escaping row at the escaped column, the step §34.3z²² called "the descent continues on row 2".
  - So finite near-models do not force the bending downward. The defect can sit beside x, which is the sideways escape that DCC does not control.
  - Like CT (§34.3z²³), the bending is spread across the T-neighbourhood rather than confined to a cell. There is no Bent Law for the strict triple at this size.

**Verdict.** Every structural idea of the framework is already present in Mordor or restates it. The one diagnostic it suggests, localizing the bent cells, gives a negative answer at the smallest size: under T the defect avoids x and ⊤ but may lie at v, beside x. A proof must therefore handle the escaped column v itself, not only columns below x. This agrees with §34.3z²²: the open inequality is v ≤ x.

## 34.3z²⁶ Twenty-eighth brief: minimal contracting rows, the map φ_y, and a label fixed point

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint c9342d2 = `main` at the start) asks to minimize the contracting row at the history-meet terminal column y, to iterate φ_y(z) = y ⊓ F(z, y) on D, and to test the resulting normal forms with targeted encodings.

**Source audit.**
- *Formal* (`Saturation.lean`): `theta_contract` and `theta_terminal`.
- *By hand* (§34.3z²²–²³): the descent graph and T. `MinSwitch.lean` checks its order lemmas and the T consequences.
- *Experimental*: `thetaloc.py` (θ-terminal locally SAT), `ctlocal.py` (CT refuted locally) and `bentcells.py` (defects not localized).

**Strategy 1 degenerates.** C_y = {a ∈ D : F(a, y) ≤ y} is a down-set, so its minimal element is ⊥ (`HistoryFix.contract_bot`, `min_contract_eq_bot`; no axioms).
- The projection lemma (P), a ≤ zᵢ, holds trivially.
- (B) reads ⊥ < F(⊥, y) ≤ y ⊓ h(y). This is W1 at row ⊥ together with monotonicity.
- So minimizing the row carries no information. Minimizing the *column* does. With the contracting row fixed to any r below every generator (⊥, or m₁₂₃ after `rows_to_meets`), the history-meet terminal is a minimal element:
- **`HistoryFix.min_terminal`** (DCC). Some y ∉ D satisfies:
  - F(r, y) ≤ y;
  - y ⊓ gⱼ(y) ∈ D for every j (HT);
  - minimality: no y′ < y outside D has F(r, y′) ≤ y′.
  - The minimality is the new part relative to `theta_terminal`. Proof: if y ⊓ gⱼ(y) ∉ D, then F(r, y ⊓ gⱼ y) ≤ F(r, y) ⊓ F(pⱼ, y) puts it in the set, strictly below y by W2.

**Strategy 2: φ_y, in reduced form a label fixed point.**
- *`HistoryFix.phi_mem`.* Under HT, φ_y maps D into D: z ≤ pᵢ gives φ_y(z) ≤ y ⊓ gᵢ(y).
- *The fixed point needs no ACC.* By `rows_to_meets` we may take F(z, ·) = g_{Λ(z)}. Then φ_y acts on label sets, K ↦ Λ(z_K) with z_K = y ⊓ g_K(y), and the iteration from {1, 2, 3} only shrinks labels.
- **`HistoryFix.label_fixed`** (no chain condition). Under HT there is a nonempty K with Λ(z_K) = K exactly.
- For a general F the brief's fixed point q ∈ D also exists without ACC: D is closed under joins of chains, because a chain in a finite union of principal ideals has a cofinal part in one of them. It is not needed here.
- **`HistoryFix.fixed_sideways`, `label_fixed_proper`.** At such K, the row m_K does not contract at y. Otherwise z_K = g_K(y) ≤ m_K, which is W1 at m_K. If h contracts at y, then K ≠ {1, 2, 3}, so |K| ∈ {1, 2}.
- *Label analysis at the fixed point*, with c = g_K(y):
  - c ≰ y.
  - c ⊓ y has labels exactly K.
  - If c ∈ D, then Λ(c) ⊆ K, and W1 at m_K makes the inclusion strict, so |K| = 2 and Λ(c) = {i}.
  - If c ∉ D, then c is a sideways column whose meet with y lies under exactly the generators in K.
- *This is T again.* K = {j} with gⱼ(y) ∉ D is the case w ∈ D of §34.3z²³: "w = x ⊓ g₂(x) lies below p₂ only". The minimal column y now carries the contracting row h itself.
- So the minimal history-meet column and the terminal row switch are the same obstruction: an escaping row j with y ⊓ gⱼ(y) ≤ pⱼ only.

**Falsification** (`experiments/fgcut/fixloc.py`).
- *The encoding.* The reduced meet-row theory, with explicit glbs z_A = y ⊓ g_A(y) taken among the elements. It imposes:
  - HT;
  - h(y) ≤ y;
  - minimality of y over the listed columns below it.
- With these, the label fixed point is forced and printed.
- No-witness is imposed at chosen columns:

  | no-witness at | N, E | result |
  |---|---|---|
  | y, ⊤ | 0, 2 | UNSAT (no room for an escaping row: every value would be y, ⊤ or in D) |
  | y, ⊤ | 1, 2 | SAT |
  | y, ⊤, every column that is a value g_A(y) or g_A(⊤) | 2, 2 · 2, 3 · 3, 3 | **SAT**: K = {1} or {3}. The fixed row escapes to a sideways column (c ∉ D, c ≰ y), and the other generator rows take D-values. |
  | every column reachable from y, ⊤ by row values | 1–4, 2–3 | UNSAT (N = 5: undecided in 25 min) |
  | the same, **without** HT, h(y) ≤ y and minimality (control) | 1–3, 2–3 | UNSAT, so the row above is vacuous at these sizes (N = 4, 5: undecided in 25 min) |

- So the label-fixed state is locally consistent with no-witness at y, ⊤ and the escaped values.
- The reach-closed UNSAT does not come from the terminal hypotheses. It also holds without them: a finite set of columns closed under the meet rows cannot carry no-witness in these encodings. That is the finite obstruction again, as the brief warned.
- Reading: in a counterexample, the row-value closure of y must be infinite. Every finite truncation needs a defect, and the defect can sit at a sideways column.

**Next step.** The smallest unresolved implication is unchanged in substance, now in its sharpest form:
- At a minimal y ∉ D with h(y) ≤ y and HT, the label fixed point is K = {j} or K = {i, j}.
- Show that the escaping column c = g_K(y) yields a smaller contracted column. This needs no-witness at c and at the columns it generates, an infinite set in any counterexample.
- The most promising experiment is a *lasso* encoding. The row-value closure of y is finite modulo a declared order-preserving shift, i.e. a periodic infinite column set. Ask whether HT + minimality + no-witness on the closure is satisfiable.
  - SAT gives a candidate infinite counterexample pattern, to be checked for DCC.
  - UNSAT gives a genuine terminal theorem, since the control (no terminal hypotheses) must then be SAT for the test to mean anything.

## 34.3z²⁷ Lasso models: eventually periodic column sets

§34.3z²⁶ ended with this proposal: test whether the infinite row-value closure of a counterexample can be eventually periodic. `experiments/fgcut/lasso.py` encodes such *infinite* models exactly, as one finite SAT instance.

**The encoding.**
- *The D-part:* ⊥, the seven meets of a strictly crossing triple (as glbs), and E extra D-elements.
- *Periodic columns:* c_{t,n} for types t < T and n ∈ ℕ.
  - The order relation c_{t,n} ≤ c_{s,m} holds only if m ≥ n, and depends only on (t, s, min(m − n, W)). So it is shift-invariant and eventually constant, and a type's tail may be an ascending chain.
  - Since every relation raises the index, descending chains end: DCC holds.
- *Other columns:* U further columns L_u (each above a whole tail or above none of it), and ⊤.
- *Values:*
  - F(r, c_{t,n}) is a D-element, ⊤, some L_u, or c_{s,n+δ} with 0 ≤ δ ≤ Δ, shift-invariantly.
  - F(r, L_u) and F(r, ⊤) are a D-element, an L_v, ⊤, or a fixed column c_{s,m} with m ≤ W.
- *Constraints:* F is monotone, and no-witness holds at every column.
- *Why one finite instance suffices:* by shift invariance, checking the poset axioms on index offsets 0..2W, monotonicity on a base column against offsets 0..W, and no-witness on base columns covers every n.
- The models are posets, not lattices. UNSAT therefore excludes lattice counterexamples of this shape a fortiori.

**A vacuous first version.** If the values at the L_u and at ⊤ are confined to D ∪ L ∪ {⊤}, then {L_u} ∪ {⊤} is a finite row-closed set of columns. Such a set is UNSAT even with no periodic types (T = 0), which is the finite obstruction once more. In the final encoding these values may also be periodic columns.

**Results** (final encoding):

| T | W | Δ | U | E | result |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 2 | UNSAT |
| 2 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 2 | UNSAT |
| 2 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 2 | UNSAT |
| 3 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 3 | UNSAT |
| 3 | 2 | 2 | 3 | 3 | UNSAT |
| 3 | 3 | 2 | 2 | 3 | UNSAT |
| 4, 5 | 2 | 2 | 3 | 3 | undecided in 28 min |

**Controls.** The same instances, with no-witness weakened, are all SAT. This shows the UNSAT is not an artefact of the shape.

| no-witness at | T, W, Δ, U, E | result |
|---|---|---|
| nowhere | 2, 2, 1, 2, 2 | SAT |
| the periodic columns only | 2, 2, 1, 2, 2 · 3, 2, 2, 2, 3 | SAT: every row sends c_{t,n} up to an incomparable column, an escape to infinity |
| the periodic columns and ⊤ (not at the L_u) | 1, 1, 1, 1, 2 · 2, 2, 1, 2, 2 · 3, 2, 2, 2, 3 | SAT |
| the periodic columns and the L_u (not at ⊤) | 2, 2, 1, 2, 2 | SAT |

- *An example from the third row* (T = U = 1):
  - c_n is an ascending chain above all three generators, and its join is ⊤.
  - g₁(c_n) = L₀, a column beside the chain; g₂ and g₃ take D-values.
  - At L₀ and ⊤, rows 2 and 3 return to the chain at c_{n+1}.
  - The defect is W1 at (p₁, L₀): g₁(L₀) = ⊥.

**Reading.**
- Under no-witness, an infinite periodic family of columns can exist *only* by escaping to infinity, and this is consistent locally. The contradiction comes from the finitely many columns at the top: the extra columns and ⊤ dominate the escapes by monotonicity, and no-witness must hold there too.
- **Conjecture (finite top).** No counterexample has its columns made of finitely many eventually periodic ω-families together with finitely many further columns. It is verified at the sizes above.
- A counterexample would therefore need infinitely many columns that are not on a periodic family, for example infinitely many limit columns. At the next level these would have to be infinitely many in turn, since a two-level lasso has a finite top again.
- *Strategy suggested.* Induct on a Cantor–Bendixson-type rank of the escape closure: rank 0 finite (the finite obstruction), rank 1 finitely many limits (the conjecture above), and so on. DCC bounds the descent within each layer. What is missing is a human proof of the rank-1 step. The solver needs no-witness at both the extra columns and ⊤, so the proof must combine both.

## 34.3z²⁸ What the lasso contradiction uses

Minimal unsatisfiable sets of constraint groups (`lasso.py … mus`). The groups are W1 and W2 per row and column, and monotonicity per row pair and column pair; the poset axioms are hard.

| instance (T, W, Δ, U, E) | MUS size | W2 at a periodic column | monotonicity between different shifts |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1, 1, 1, 1, 2 | 60 | none | none |
| 2, 2, 1, 2, 2 | 141 | none | none |

**What is used, and what is not.**
- *Used.* The periodic columns enter only in two ways:
  - through W1 at all seven rows, i.e. `no_closed_rows`: some generator escapes there;
  - through monotonicity into the top layer {L_u, ⊤}: a shifted value is never below a fixed column.
- *Not used.* Their own W2, and the order between shifts, play no part. W2 is used only at the generator rows at the L_u and at ⊤. W1 at ⊤ is not used.
- So the contradiction is a finite case analysis on the top layer. The infinite family contributes one fact: its escape values are unbounded under every fixed column.

**The one-type case by hand** (T = U = 1).
- *An escape at the family goes to L, and L is beside the family.*
  - Let generator i escape at c_n. Its value is not ⊤, since g_i(⊤) ≠ ⊤ (W2 at ⊤).
  - If the value is a shifted column, g_i(⊤) must dominate the whole tail, so g_i(⊤) = L with the tail below L. Then g_i(L) ≥ the tail as well, so g_i(L) = L, which is W2 at (p_i, L).
  - So g_i(c_n) = L, and then g_i(⊤) = L. If the family were below L, g_i(L) ≥ L would again break W2. So L is beside the family, and g_i(L) ∈ D.
- *The two escape sets are disjoint.* Let E_c and E_L be the sets of generators escaping at the family and at L.
  - At L an escape goes to a fixed column c_m: it cannot be ⊤ or L, and it is not in D.
  - For j ∈ E_L, g_j(⊤) is then a fixed column, so g_j(c_n) ∈ D for all n: a shifted value is never below a fixed one.
  - Hence E_c ∩ E_L = ∅, and both sets are nonempty by the column lemma.
- *Mixed meet rows are trapped in D.* For i ∈ E_c and j ∈ E_L, g_{ij}(⊤) lies below L and below a fixed column, so it lies in D. Then g_{ij} takes D-values at every column.
- What remains is the finite label analysis at the family and at L under `no_closed_rows`, with the rows m_{ij} confined to D. The solver closes it; a hand proof is still open.

**Reading.**
- The finite-top conjecture of §34.3z²⁷ is a finite statement about the top layer. The periodic family is used only as one column whose escapes must be dominated from above.
- This also explains why the vacuous version and the real version agree: both are the finite obstruction, and periodicity adds only the domination constraint.
- *Strategy.* A Cantor–Bendixson induction would need, at each rank, the same two facts:
  - every escape from a lower layer is dominated by the next layer up;
  - each layer, with W2 only at its own columns, is finitely contradictory.
- The second fact is the finite obstruction, i.e. the reduced theory has no finite model (§34.3z²⁰, verified to 8 columns), which is itself unproved. So the lasso line reduces the infinite problem to the finite poset problem plus a domination lemma. It does not bypass the finite problem.

## 34.3z²⁹ The finite obstruction is a theorem: a fixed point on simplex × cone

§34.3z²⁶–²⁸ reduced both the history-meet line and the lasso line to the *finite obstruction*: the reduced meet-row theory has no finite model (§34.3z²⁰, there verified only to 8 columns). This section proves it for every k, by a fixed-point argument that needs no joins among columns.

**Chain labelling lemma (CLL).**
- *Data.* Let C be a finite poset with a top ⊤, and k ≥ 1. A *cell* is a pair (A, σ), with ∅ ≠ A ⊆ [k] and σ a nonempty chain of C. Cells are ordered by (A, σ) ≤ (A′, σ′) iff A ⊇ A′ and σ ⊆ σ′.
- *Labelling.* Each cell c gets a value z(c): either a nonempty label set α(c) ⊆ [k] (a *label cell*), or an element of C (a *column cell*).
- *Monotonicity.* If c ≤ c′, then:
  - a column cell c has a column cell c′ above it, with z(c) ≤ z(c′);
  - two label cells satisfy α(c′) ⊆ α(c).
- *Conditions.*
  - W1: A ⊄ α at every label cell.
  - W2: max σ ≰ z at every column cell.
- **Lemma.** No such labelling exists.

*Soundness for Cut.* Take a finite bounded poset L and a down-set D ∌ ⊤ whose maximal elements p₁, …, p_k have all meets m_A = ⋀_{i∈A} pᵢ. A witness-free F gives a CLL labelling with C = L ∖ D: put z(A, σ) = F(m_A, max σ) if it is a column, and its label set Λ(z) if it lies in D. Then W1 and W2 are exactly no-witness at the cell (m_A, max σ). The labelling may depend on the whole chain, not only on max σ, so CLL is stronger than what Cut needs.

**Proof.**
1. *The space.* The order complex of the cell poset triangulates X = Δ^{k−1} × |Δ(C)|. A flag c₁ < … < c_r with weights λᵢ > 0 is sent to the point (Σλᵢ b(Aᵢ), Σλᵢ b(σᵢ)), where b is the barycentre. This is the staircase triangulation of a product of two subdivided complexes.
   - The point's P-support is A₁, since A decreases along the flag.
   - Its Q-support is σ_r, since σ increases.
2. *The space has the fixed-point property.* C has a top, so Δ(C) is a cone with apex ⊤. Hence X is a contractible compact polyhedron, and Lefschetz gives every continuous self-map a fixed point.
3. *The map.* Define Ψ : X → X on each closed simplex of the triangulation by Ψ(x) = Σᵢ λᵢ·Ψ(cᵢ; x). Write s(x) for the P-coordinate of x.
   - A label cell c goes to (b(α(c)), ⊤).
   - A column cell c goes to (s(x), z(c)).

   Ψ lands in X:
   - The column values in a flag form a chain, by monotonicity. Together with ⊤ they span a simplex of Δ(C).
   - The P-part lies in the simplex.

   On a common face, the formulas for two simplices agree, so Ψ is continuous.
4. *The fixed point.* Let x = Ψ(x) lie in the open simplex of the flag c₁ < … < c_r. A cell above a column cell is a column cell, so the label cells form an initial segment c₁ … c_m.
   - *Only column cells.* Then σ_r is the Q-support of Ψ(x), which is {z(cᵢ)}. So max σ_r = z(c_j) ≤ z(c_r), contradicting W2 at c_r.
   - *Some label cell.* The P-coordinate gives s = Σ_{i≤m} λᵢ b(αᵢ) + Λ_col·s. Since Λ_lab = 1 − Λ_col > 0, supp s = ⋃_{i≤m} αᵢ = α(c₁), because α is antitone. But supp s = A₁, so A₁ ⊆ α(c₁), contradicting W1 at c₁. ∎

**Machine check** (`experiments/fgcut/labpos.py`): every column poset with a top up to n elements, chain cells, no-witness as above.

| k | n | posets | SAT |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 6 | 63 | 0 |
| 2 | 6 | 63 | 0 |
| 3 | 1–6 | 1, 1, 2, 5, 16, 63 | 0 |
| 4 | 4 | 5 | 0 |

Controls on the same posets: dropping W1 or W2 makes every instance SAT. With *singleton* cells only, many instances are SAT (11 of 16 at k = 3, n = 5), because a singleton keeps no order between columns. Letting B range over all sets with a least upper bound gives UNSAT as well. That variant is the natural one, but chains suffice, and chains are what the proof uses.

**Consequences.**
- **Theorem (Cut on finite posets with generator meets).** Let L be a finite bounded poset and D a proper down-set. If the maximal elements of D have all meets, every monotone F has a witness.
  - Dually, it suffices that the minimal elements of L ∖ D have all joins (self-duality, §33.3).
  - Proof: `rows_to_meets` (its proof uses only the order), then CLL.
- *Finite lattices.* This gives a second proof of the Cut part of `cl_finite`. On the column side it needs only the top, not joins of minimal elements.
- *The finite obstruction of §34.3z²⁰ and ²⁶–²⁸ holds for every k and every finite column poset.*
  - The reach-closed UNSAT of `fixloc.py` and the finite top layer of `lasso.py` are instances.
  - So the finite-top conjecture (§34.3z²⁷) reduces to its domination step.
- *Finite posets in general* (open since §34.3m) are now open only when neither side has the meets or joins: some meets of maximal elements of D fail to exist, and some joins of minimal elements of the complement fail to exist.

**What it says about the infinite case.**
- A counterexample to FG∞ for the crossing triple is now exactly a fixed-point-free map of the form Ψ on Δ² × |Δ(C)|, with C an infinite DCC poset with top.
- The cone is still contractible, so the obstruction is *only* compactness: the map must push mass off to infinity in Δ(C). Ψ's Q-image of a flag is supported on its column values together with ⊤.
- Any finite C₀ ∋ ⊤ whose chains' column values stay in C₀ would give a contradiction. So the row-value closure of ⊤ is infinite in every counterexample, now a theorem rather than SAT evidence.
- DCC rules out escape downward. What remains is escape upward or sideways, which is the picture every experiment since §34.3z²² has shown.
- *For lattices this is not new.* `FinGen.captured` (§34.3o, Lean) already says that any finitely many columns u_j capturing every column value F(m_A, ⋁u_B) give a contradiction. That is stronger than the closure statement above: it needs neither ⊤ nor values staying in the set, only values *above* some u_j.
  - The projection π(z) = ⋁(C₀ ∩ ↓z) turns any finite join-closed C₀ ∋ ⊤ whose values all dominate an element of C₀ into such a family.
  - So "compactness" in a lattice is exactly the finite-support problem of §34.3o–t.
- What CLL adds is the poset case. There the joins ⋁u_B used by `captured` need not exist, and chains are the substitute.
- *No reduction of CLL to `captured` is apparent.* `captured` needs values on all subsets B, and no monotone B ↦ chain with max in B exists once B contains two incomparable elements. Restricting to one maximal chain M, the Lean Sperner lemma yields only a cell whose column value lies above no element of M. Combining those across maximal chains is where a parity proof on the cone would be needed.

**Status.**
- Paper proof (Lefschetz): CLL and the poset theorem.
- Machine-checked: CLL to the sizes in the table.
- Not in Lean: Mathlib has neither Brouwer nor Lefschetz, and the Lean product Sperner lemma (§34.3n) covers simplex × simplex only. A Sperner-type parity proof on simplex × cone is the missing piece for formalization.

## 34.3z³⁰ Finite posets beyond CLL: searches and the double cone

CLL (§34.3z²⁹) proves Cut on finite posets when D's maximal elements have all meets, or dually when the complement's minimal elements have all joins. Open: finite posets where *both* fail.

**Direct search** (`experiments/fgcut/poscx.py`).
- *The shape:*
  - D = {⊥} ∪ {a₁…a_m} ∪ X ∪ {p₁…p_k}, where the a's are pairwise incomparable maximal common lower bounds of the p's. So for m ≥ 2, ⋀p fails to exist.
  - C = {u₁…u_r} ∪ {s₁…s_n} ∪ Y ∪ {⊤}, where the s's are pairwise incomparable minimal common upper bounds of the u's. So for n ≥ 2, ⋁u fails to exist.
  - X and Y are extra free elements.
- *The variables:* every other order relation, including all relations between D and C, and a monotone F on D × C with values anywhere.

| k, m, r, n, |X|, |Y| | elements | result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2, 2, 2, 2, 0, 0 | 10 | UNSAT |
| 2, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1 | 12 | UNSAT |
| 2, 3, 2, 3, 0, 0 | 12 | UNSAT |
| 2, 2, 2, 2, 2, 2 | 14 | UNSAT |
| 3, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1 | 13 | UNSAT |
| 2, 3, 2, 3, 2, 2 | 16 | UNSAT |

Controls: dropping W1 or W2 makes the 10-element instance SAT. This extends the exhaustive ≤ 11-element search of §34.3h to 16 elements for the shapes that CLL does not cover.

**Double-cone chain labelling** (`experiments/fgcut/dcll.py`).
- *Cells:* pairs (ρ, σ), with ρ a chain of D (which has ⊥) and σ a chain of C (which has ⊤).
- *Conditions:* values are monotone, with W1 "z ∈ D ⇒ z ≰ min ρ" and W2 "z ∈ C ⇒ max σ ≰ z".
- *Why this one instance suffices:* the most permissive cross order is the ordinal sum D ⊕ C, so solving it covers every finite poset with that D and C. Since values may depend on the whole chain pair, the statement is stronger than Cut.
- *Results:* UNSAT on all pairs of posets up to 5 × 4 (4, 25 and 80 pairs at sizes 3 × 3, 4 × 4 and 5 × 4). The 5 × 5 run did not finish in 25 minutes.
- So the double-cone lemma is a candidate theorem implying **Cut on every finite poset**.

**Where the fixed-point proof stops.**
- X = |Δ(D)| × |Δ(C)| is again a product of two cones, hence contractible.
- *The natural map:*
  - a label cell goes to (vertex z, ⊤);
  - a column cell goes to (⊥, vertex z).
- Its fixed points in pure flags (only label cells, or only column cells) violate W1 or W2, exactly as in §34.3z²⁹.
- *In a mixed flag the fixed point only forces this:*
  - min ρ₁ = ⊥ and max σ_r = ⊤;
  - every label cell's chain ρ_j contains an element below z_j, and every column cell's chain σ_j contains an element above z_j;
  - two weight equations: Σ_{i: ⊥∈ρᵢ} λᵢ/|ρᵢ| = Λ_col and Σ_{i: ⊤∈σᵢ} λᵢ/|σᵢ| = Λ_lab.
  - None of this is contradictory by itself.
- CLL avoided the problem by keeping the P-coordinate fixed at column cells. That is legal only because every vertex set of a simplex is a face. In Δ(D), the set ρ₁ ∪ {z_label} need not be a chain.
- *What would close it:* a retraction of Δ(D) onto the cone Δ(↑z) that is natural in z. That would mean x ↦ x ∨ z, i.e. joins in D, which is the structure that is missing.

**Status.** Finite posets lacking both meets and joins: no counterexample to 16 elements in the family above. The double-cone lemma is UNSAT to 5 × 4. It has no proof. Its pure-flag cases are proved; the mixed case is open.

## 34.3z³¹ Thirty-first brief: mixed flags, completion transfer, and why the double cone is hard

The brief (ChatGPT, checkpoint ca90f77) asks for a proof or a counterexample for the double-cone chain labelling lemma (DCL, §34.3z³⁰), a completion-transfer theorem, and a Lean CLL.

**Source audit.**
- *On paper* (Lefschetz): CLL, and with it Cut on finite posets whose D-maxima have all meets, or dually whose C-minima have all joins (§34.3z²⁹).
- *Experimental:*
  - DCL is UNSAT on all pairs of posets up to 5 × 4.
  - `poscx.py` finds no counterexample up to 16 elements.
  - New this round: the decisive 5 × 5 instance, bowtie × bowtie, is *undecided*. It is 289 cells, 23,800 clauses. CaDiCaL ran out of time at 20 minutes, and CP-SAT (8 workers, table constraints, `dcll_cp.py`) at 20 minutes.
- *Lean:* nothing for CLL.
- *Frontier:* `Frontier.lean` reduces the standing conjecture to join-closure of R(F). The finite-poset case is not on that path.

### 1. Completion transfer is impossible

**Proposition.** There is a finite bounded poset P, a down-set D and a monotone F, witness-free at every original column below ⊤, such that **every** monotone extension of F to the Dedekind–MacNeille completion has a witness at a new element.

*Example* (`experiments/fgcut/swapcheck.py`):
- The poset: D = {⊥ < d < p}. The columns are b₁, b₂ above p and incomparable, then s₁, s₂ above both and incomparable, then ⊤.
- The completion adds w = b₁ ⊔ b₂ = s₁ ⊓ s₂.
- The map: F(x, b₁) = b₂, F(x, b₂) = b₁, F(x, s₁) = s₂, F(x, s₂) = s₁ and F(x, ⊤) = ⊤, for every x ∈ D.

*Proof.* Any monotone extension F̂ has F̂(x, w) ≥ F(x, b₁) ⊔ F(x, b₂) = b₂ ⊔ b₁ = w, which is W2 at the new column w. The script confirms monotonicity, the absence of original witnesses below ⊤, and the failure for both the lower and the upper extension. ∎

- The cause is a *swap* between incomparable columns whose join is missing. Swaps are exactly the sideways escapes.
- Completion creates the missing join, and monotonicity then forces a fixed point there, but that witness does not pull back.
- So any proof for posets must work in the poset itself. The ⊤-cell's witness, which CLL guarantees for this poset since D is a chain, lies elsewhere.

### 2. Mixed flags: a sharper map, and the exact residue

Take the map of CLL on the C-side, and on the D-side split the label cells by whether their chain reaches above the value.
- A *non-free* label cell (z ∈ D, T = ρ ∩ ↑z ≠ ∅) goes to (b(T), ⊤).
- A *free* label cell (z ∈ D, ρ ∩ ↑z = ∅) goes to (s(x), ⊤).
- A column cell goes to (s(x), vertex z).

*Well defined.* The P-image is supported in ρ₁, because T ⊆ ρ_i ⊆ ρ₁. The Q-image is supported on {z_col} ∪ {⊤}, a chain. So Ψ maps X = |Δ(D)| × |Δ(C)| into itself and is continuous.

*Fixed points.* Free label cells are upward closed among label cells, so a flag reads: non-free label cells, then free label cells, then column cells.
- *No label cells:* W2 fails at c_r, as in CLL.
- *Some non-free label cells:* the P-equation gives Λ_NL·s = Σ_NL λᵢ b(Tᵢ), so ρ₁ = ⋃Tᵢ. Then min ρ₁ ∈ Tᵢ ⊆ ↑zᵢ for some i. Since ρᵢ ⊆ ρ₁ and min ρ₁ ∈ ρᵢ, min ρᵢ ≥ zᵢ, which violates W1.
- *Otherwise:* every label cell in the flag is free.

**Residue.**
- A fixed point survives only in a flag whose label cells are all *free*: z ∈ D with no element of ρ above z.
- Free cells satisfy W1 automatically, so they carry no information.
- A configuration of free label cells alone is excluded by CLL with k = 1 on D^op: label cells ordered by ⊇ on ρ form a k = 1 instance on the cone Δ(D^op) with apex ⊥. So the residue is genuinely *mixed*: free label cells below column cells.
- The symmetric map (non-free *column* cells with T′ = σ ∩ ↓z) leaves the dual residue: flags whose column cells are all free.
- A proof of DCL therefore needs one map whose fixed points avoid *both* residues, or an argument that a flag of free label cells followed by column cells, with σ_r = {z_col} ∪ {⊤}, violates something.
- **Exact missing step:** at a free label cell, find a D-side target, i.e. a point of the cone Δ(↑z) compatible with the current chain ρ₁. In CLL this is b(α), always available because every subset of the generators is a face. In a poset it would be supplied by z ∨ (ρ₁), which need not exist. This is the same missing join as in §1, now in the fixed-point language.

### 3. Hardness as evidence

- The instance resisted CDCL and CP-SAT for 20 minutes at 289 cells. Resolution is exponentially weak on parity and Sperner principles, so this is the expected signature if DCL holds for a topological or parity reason.
- A satisfying labelling of this size would normally be found quickly by CP-SAT's portfolio, though that is not proof.
- So bowtie × bowtie is the smallest open case, and it is undecided.
- *Next experiment:* a solver with XOR reasoning (CryptoMiniSat with Gauss–Jordan) on a parity-augmented encoding. The alternative is symmetry breaking under the group of the bowtie pair, which has order 16: independent swaps a↔b, p↔q, u↔v, s↔t (corrected in §34.3z³⁴).

### 4. Lean CLL: the obstacle

- `Sperner.odd_goods` counts good maximal chains of N_P × N_Q by pairing doors across codimension-one faces. That needs every interior facet to lie in exactly two maximal simplices.
- In sd(Δ(C)) × sd(Δ^{k−1}), a facet that drops the top face M of a flag lies in one maximal simplex per maximal chain of C extending M ∖ {v}. That number can be any count ≥ 1, so the complex is not a pseudomanifold.
- With the Sperner label λ₂ = max σ, bijectivity forces the flag to add the elements of M in increasing order, which is unique per M. The doors that drop M are then the only branching ones.
- A cone-Sperner lemma would have to show that labelled doors at branching facets cancel mod 2. This is the formalization target; it is not attempted here.

**Status.**
- Proved: completion transfer fails, for every monotone extension; the mixed-flag residue reduces to flags whose label cells are all free.
- Open: DCL; Cut on finite posets lacking both meets and joins; Lean CLL.

## 34.3z³² Dismantling: the finite-poset problem reduces to cores

**Lemma (Lean, `lean/Mordor/Dismantle.lean`; standard axioms).** Let α be a poset, D a down-set and F a witness-free monotone map (`Cx D F`).
- **`dismantle_D`.** Suppose x ∈ D has a least strict upper bound x⁺ ∈ D, i.e. a unique upper cover lying in D. Then `up x x⁺ ∘ F` is a witness-free monotone map on {y ≠ x}.
  - Pushing a value up keeps W1: r(z) ≤ m ⇒ z ≤ m.
  - W2 is untouched, except when F(a, b) = x. Then b ≤ x⁺ ∈ D would put the column b in D.
- **`dismantle_C`.** Dually, a column y ∉ D with a greatest strict lower bound y⁻ ∉ D can be deleted, with values pushed down.

The same argument transfers the double-cone lemma (DCL): chains of the smaller poset are chains of the larger, min ρ and max σ are unchanged, and the retraction only moves values.

More generally, any monotone retraction r onto a subposet S with z ≤ r(z) when r(z) ∈ D, and r(z) ≤ z when r(z) ∉ D, transfers counterexamples to S. That much is paper only; Lean has the two one-point cases.

**Cores.**
- Repeating the two steps reduces the Cut Lemma on finite posets to *cores*:
  - no D-element has a unique upper cover that lies in D;
  - no column has a unique lower cover that is a column.
- Deleting a dismantlable element never destroys a meet of two or more maxima of D. Such a meet x = p ⊓ q is covered by p and q directly, or by a common lower bound above x, which contradicts x being the meet.
- **Corollary (paper).** Cut holds on every finite bounded poset whose core has all meets of its D-maxima, or all joins of its C-minima (by CLL, §34.3z²⁹).
- So the open finite case is exactly: cores failing both conditions.

**The smallest open case is minimal.**
- A D-core whose maxima lack a meet needs at least two maximal common lower bounds of two maxima, each covered twice, together with ⊥. The bowtie ⊥ < a, b < p, q is the smallest.
- Dually on the C-side, so bowtie × bowtie (10 elements, already covered by `poscx.py` for Cut) is the smallest open instance of DCL.
- Its automorphism group has order 16: independent swaps a↔b, p↔q, u↔v and s↔t.
- With lex-leader symmetry breaking for all 15 non-identity elements (`experiments/fgcut/dcll_sym.py`, 53,437 clauses), CaDiCaL is still undecided after 10 minutes.
- The hardness is intrinsic and not symmetric duplication. This is consistent with a parity principle.
- *Connection.* The 4-crown {a, b < p, q} is the classical minimal poset without the fixed-point property: a↔b, p↔q is a fixed-point-free automorphism. Cores in the sense above are posets with no dismantlable elements, as in Rival's theory. The swap obstruction of §34.3z³¹ is this automorphism acting on the columns.

**Addendum: routes to a Lean CLL that do not work.** Mathlib has neither Brouwer nor Lefschetz, so CLL needs a combinatorial proof. Each reduction to the Lean product Sperner (`captured`) tried here fails.
- *Values on non-chain subsets.* `captured` needs values at every subset B of the Q-vertices. No monotone B ↦ chain has max in B once B contains two incomparable elements (§34.3z²⁹).
- *Columns as singletons in the face lattice of Δ(C).* The map z ↦ {z} is not monotone.
- *Maximal chains as Q-vertices, with σ(B) = ⋂B.* This is antitone in B. W2 also gives only max σ ≰ z, never z ∉ σ.
- *Induction by deleting a maximal or minimal element of a core.* Replacing the deleted value needs a choice of lower cover that is constant on comparable cells, and a core does not provide it.
- *By SAT*, a variable-order search over all bounded posets with |D| = |C| = 5 is UNSAT, also when restricted to posets uncovered by CLL. This is subsumed by §34.3h's ≤ 11. At 6 + 6 it is undecided in 10 minutes.
- A Lean CLL therefore needs a genuine cone-Sperner lemma: parity of good flags on simplex × cone, with cancellation at branching facets.

## 34.3z³³ The cone Sperner lemma, and CLL in Lean

CLL (§34.3z²⁹) had a paper proof through Lefschetz, which Mathlib does not have. This section replaces the topology by a combinatorial lemma, now proved in Lean together with CLL and its Cut corollary. Standard axioms only, no `sorry`.

**Cone Sperner lemma (`lean/Mordor/ConeSperner.lean`, `exists_full`, `trace_G`).**
- *Setting.* K is a finite simplicial complex that is a cone: some apex v₀ can be added to every face. φ sends faces to vertices, and the labels of every complete flag of every face form a face.
- *Claim.* The number of pairs (face τ, complete flag of τ) whose labels are exactly τ is **odd**. In particular, one exists.
- *Proof: the mod-2 Hopf trace formula, by matrix algebra over ZMod 2 on the faces.*
  - `G σ τ` counts the flags of τ labelled exactly by σ, so trace G is the count above.
  - **`chain`: G·∂ = ∂·G.** This is a door count.
    - Deleting one label of a flag of τ leaves σ an odd number of times iff the labels are σ plus one new vertex (`Sperner.removal`).
    - The inner doors cancel under the adjacent swap (`inner_doors`).
    - The last door is a complete flag of a facet (`last_door`).
  - **`cone`: ∂H + H∂ = 1 + Π**, for the cone homotopy H(τ) = τ ∪ {v₀} and Π the projection to the apex. The entries are checked by a case analysis (`cone_entry`).
  - Hence trace G = trace(G(∂H + H∂ + Π)) = 2·trace(GH∂) + trace(GΠ) = trace(GΠ) = 1. The middle step uses cyclicity of the trace and the chain identity.
- This is the combinatorial content of Lefschetz for simplicial maps sd(K) → K on an acyclic K.
- *Why the existing Sperner lemma could not do it.* Its door pairing needs a pseudomanifold. A cone over an arbitrary complex is not one, and the trace identity sidesteps that.

**CLL from it (`lean/Mordor/CLL.lean`, `cll`).**
- *The complex.* Vertices are `Fin k ⊕ C`. A face is a nonempty set whose generator part is proper and whose column part is a chain. It is a cone with apex inr ⊤ (`cone_data`).
- *The labels.*
  - A face with no column (pure) gets a generator outside it.
  - A label cell gets a generator in A ∖ α (W1).
  - A column cell gets its column.
- *Labels of a flag form a face (`lab_face`).*
  - Column labels are comparable, by monotonicity.
  - Generator labels avoid a common generator. If the first letter is a generator i₀, every label avoids i₀. Otherwise every label avoids α of the last label cell, since α is antitone along the flag.
- *No fully labelled flag.*
  - A generator label at the top face lies outside it.
  - A column label c at the top makes every column of the face a label, hence ≤ c. That violates W2.

**Corollary (`cut_of_meets`).** The Cut Lemma holds on every finite bounded poset whose D-generators have all meets.
- No structure is needed among the columns beyond the top.
- The labelling reads F(m_A, max σ) on chains σ of the column subtype.
- This is the theorem of §34.3z²⁹, now machine-checked. It reproves the Cut part of `cl_finite` for finite lattices with a different combinatorial core, a trace rather than door pairing on a product of simplices.

**Status.**
- Lean: `ConeSperner.exists_full`, `CLL.cll` and `CLL.cut_of_meets` (541 + 311 lines).
- The double-cone lemma (both sides cones, §34.3z³⁰) would follow from the same cone lemma if a flag-simplicial labelling of the product complex could be found. The obstruction of §34.3z³¹ (free label cells) is exactly the failure of the construction above when the generator side is a cone instead of a simplex.

## 34.3z³⁴ Thirty-second brief: product-cone parity, symmetry, and where the DCL constraints live

The brief (ChatGPT, after a0dca86) asks for a relative mod-2 trace on the product of cones, an acyclic matching on the mixed residue, a core-to-cycle reduction and equivariant methods for the double-cone lemma (DCL).

**Exact frontier.**
- *Lean:*
  - `ConeSperner.exists_full` and `trace_G` (cone Sperner);
  - `CLL.cll` and `CLL.cut_of_meets` (Cut on finite posets with generator meets);
  - `Dismantle.dismantle_D` and `dismantle_C` (reduction to cores).
- *Open:* DCL, and Cut on finite posets whose core lacks both meets of D-maxima and joins of C-minima. The smallest open DCL instance is bowtie × bowtie.
- *Group order.* `dcll_sym.py` breaks 15 non-identity symmetries, so the group has order 16: (C₂)⁴, generated by a↔b, p↔q, u↔v and s↔t. The "order 8" in §34.3z³¹ was wrong; §34.3z³² has 16.

**1. The product contraction (verified by hand, mod 2).**
- Take H = H_D ⊗ 1 + Π_D ⊗ H_C on the tensor product of the two chain complexes, with ∂(x ⊗ y) = ∂x ⊗ y + x ⊗ ∂y. Then:
  - (∂H + H∂)(x ⊗ y) = (1 + Π_D)x ⊗ y + (∂Π_D + Π_D∂)x ⊗ H_C y + Π_D x ⊗ (1 + Π_C)y;
  - the middle term vanishes, since Π_D lives in degree 0, ∂ is 0 on vertices, and Π_D∂(edge) = 2v₀ = 0;
  - hence ∂H + H∂ = 1 + Π_D ⊗ Π_C, as the brief proposed.
- *But the contraction was never the obstruction.* X is contractible, so any self-map has a fixed point, and any cone gives a trace 1. What is missing is a G built from the labelling that is a chain map, i.e. a flag-simplicial labelling, whose fully labelled flags all violate W1 or W2. The free label cells of §34.3z³¹ are exactly where every construction tried so far fails to be flag-simplicial.

**2. Where the constraints live: the boundary is the join of the proper parts.**
- W1 at a cell with ⊥ ∈ ρ says only z ≠ ⊥, and W2 at a cell with ⊤ ∈ σ says only z ≠ ⊤.
- All other constraints sit on cells with ρ ⊆ D̄ = D ∖ {⊥} and σ ⊆ C̄ = C ∖ {⊤}, i.e. on chains of the *proper part* P̄ of P = D ⊕ C. Topologically this is ∂X, since ∂(cone A × cone B) ≅ A * B, so
  ∂X ≅ Δ(D̄) * Δ(C̄) = Δ(P̄).
- For bowtie × bowtie, D̄ and C̄ are 4-crowns with Δ ≅ S¹, so ∂X ≅ S¹ * S¹ = S³: **the order complex of the proper part is a 3-sphere.**
- This is the classical setting of fixed-point-free maps of posets (Baclawski–Björner). The crown's automorphism a↔b, p↔q is fixed-point-free, and the swap obstruction of §34.3z³¹ is that automorphism acting on columns.
- CLL has the same shape: its generator side ∂Δ^{k−1} is a sphere, joined to a cone. There the sphere is the boundary of a simplex, and its cells carry meet-rows.
- So the relative question of the brief has a precise form. A parity argument for DCL would have to be a mod-2 *degree* statement on Δ(P̄), relative to the cone points, not a trace on the contractible X.

**3. Symmetric counterexamples do not exist** (`experiments/fgcut/dcl_eq.py`).
- Imposing equivariance z(g·c) = g·z(c) for each of the 15 cyclic subgroups of (C₂)⁴, and for several larger subgroups, gives UNSAT on bowtie × bowtie in at most 0.3 s each.
- Reason: g-fixed cells must take g-fixed values. The cell ({⊥}, {⊤}) is fixed by everything, and under the full group its only fixed values are ⊥ and ⊤, both excluded by W1/W2. Propagation does the rest.
- So any DCL counterexample is asymmetric. The equivariant approach of the brief gives no constraint beyond this.

**4. How much chain-dependence is hard** (`dcl_dep.py`, `halfdcl.py`).

| statement on the cells | bowtie × bowtie | crown₆ × crown₆ | K₃,₃ × K₃,₃ | bowtie² × bowtie² |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cut: z depends on (min ρ, max σ) | UNSAT 0.0 s | — | — | — |
| z depends on (ρ, max σ) (half-DCL) | UNSAT 0.4–0.6 s | UNSAT 12.7 s | undecided, 15 min | undecided, 15 min |
| z depends on (min ρ, σ) | UNSAT 0.8 s | — | — | — |
| full DCL | undecided (§34.3z³¹–³²) | — | — | — |

- Half-DCL already suffices for Cut on finite posets, since Cut labellings are a special case.
- But half-DCL is as solver-hard as DCL once the cores grow. So the hardness belongs to chain-dependent statements in general, which is consistent with a parity or degree principle, and is not a two-sided phenomenon.

**5. Acyclic matching and core-to-cycle.** Not attempted at proof level this round. The structural point of §2 is relevant to both:
- Any matching argument must produce critical cells on Δ(P̄), which is S³ for the bowtie, not on X.
- Any transfer to a bowtie subposet must preserve the spherical proper part, which dismantling does. The retractions remove only dismantlable elements, and those do not change the homotopy type of the order complex.
- *Conjecture (degree form).* For a finite core P whose proper part has order complex homotopy equivalent to a sphere, the W1/W2 labellings of cells induce a map of degree 1 mod 2 on Δ(P̄). That would make the DCL constraints impossible to satisfy. Neither the statement nor the induced map is defined yet.

**Status.**
- Proved: the product contraction identity (hand computation).
- Machine evidence: no equivariant DCL labelling on bowtie × bowtie.
- Observation: the DCL constraints live on the order complex of the proper part, a 3-sphere for bowtie × bowtie.
- Open: DCL, half-DCL, and Cut on doubly uncovered cores.

## 34.3z³⁵ Cut on spherical cores, and the join construction

**Cut holds on every spherical core tested** (`experiments/fgcut/cutsph.py`).
- *Setup.* P = D ⊕ C with D = ⊥ ⊕ D̄ and C = C̄ ⊕ ⊤.
- *Why the ordinal sum suffices.* It is the most permissive cross order: a witness-free F on any poset with these D and C stays monotone and witness-free on D ⊕ C, because W1 and W2 never compare a column below a row. So each instance covers every poset with that D and C.
- *Variables.* F on all rows × all columns.

| D̄, C̄ | proper part of P | elements | result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4-crown, 4-crown | S¹ * S¹ = S³ | 10 | UNSAT 0.0 s |
| 6-crowns | S³ | 14 | UNSAT 0.0 s |
| 8-crowns | S³ | 18 | UNSAT 0.5 s |
| 12-crowns | S³ | 26 | UNSAT 1.8 s |
| levels 2,2 | S¹ * S¹ = S³ | 10 | UNSAT 0.0 s |
| levels 2,2,2 | S² * S² = S⁵ | 14 | UNSAT 0.0 s |
| levels 2,2,2,2 | S³ * S³ = S⁷ | 18 | UNSAT 0.5 s |
| levels 3,3 | (∨ 4 S¹) * (∨ 4 S¹) | 14 | UNSAT 0.5 s |
| levels 3,3,3 | wedge of 3-spheres, joined | 20 | UNSAT 35 s |
| levels 4,4,4 | wedge of 3-spheres, joined | 26 | UNSAT 285 s |
| levels 2,2,2 and 6-crown | S² * S¹ | 14 | UNSAT 0.0 s |

A level poset is an ordinal sum of antichains; with antichains of size 2 its order complex is an iterated join of S⁰, i.e. a sphere. All these posets are cores, and all fail both CLL conditions.
- Cut is solver-easy on all of them. The double-cone and half-chain strengthenings (§34.3z³⁴) are the hard ones.
- This is evidence that Cut holds on finite posets in general, and that DCL may be the wrong intermediate.

**The join construction for Cut, and where it breaks.**
- *Freedom.* For Cut, no-witness holds at every row, so the row of a chain can be max ρ, not only min ρ. Then a chain τ = ρ ∪ σ of P gets the label F(max ρ, max σ), and labels increase along inclusion flags. This is the order complex Δ(P), a cone, instead of the product of §34.3z²⁹–³⁴.
- *Every fully labelled flag violates W1 or W2*, in all cases but one:
  - a top label in D lies in ρ (W1);
  - a top label c in σ is below max σ (W2), so max σ must be labelled by an earlier face with a smaller F-value, which is impossible;
  - **except when max σ = ⊤ and ⊤ is the label of a pure-D face.**
- *Why pure-D faces are the problem.* A chain with no column needs a column below every later column, and C has no bottom. Labelling such faces by ⊤ creates exactly the exception; labelling them by ⊥ creates one trivially fully labelled vertex, and the parity is lost.
- *The mirror choice* (rows and columns both decreasing, labels F(min ρ, min σ)) needs a top in D instead.
- So the join construction proves Cut whenever C has a bottom or D has a top. Those cases are trivial anyway, by the column or row lemma.
- The asymmetry is the same one the product construction meets as "free label cells": on one side the cone apex is missing.

**Status.** The join construction is analysed and fails at one exception. Cut holds experimentally on spherical cores up to 26 elements. Open: Cut on doubly uncovered finite cores. A proof must label the pure-D faces, or the free label cells, using something other than the missing apex.

**Addendum: the apex dichotomy (why no join construction can work).**
- *Apex ⊥.* Take the cone Δ(P ∖ {⊤}), label column-free chains ⊥, and label the others F(max ρ, max σ) (row ⊥ when ρ = ∅).
  - The map is flag-simplicial.
  - *No* mixed flag is fully labelled: max σ would be the label of an earlier face, so it equals the top label, which violates W2.
  - The only fully labelled flag is the vertex {⊥}. It supplies the odd count of the cone lemma by itself, so there is no contradiction, and none is possible.
- *Apex ⊤* (labels ⊤ on column-free chains). The odd count is carried by the exceptional flags described above.
- In both cases the parity is absorbed by the apex, and the W1/W2 constraints never enter.
- *CLL avoids this* because its generator side is the sphere ∂Δ^{k−1}, not a cone:
  - pure faces are labelled by a generator outside the face;
  - flag-simpliciality is automatic, since any proper set of generators is a face.
- *The chain analogue* would label a column-free chain ρ ⊆ D̄ by an element of D̄ ∖ ρ forming a chain with the D-valued F-labels of later faces. That needs an upper bound of ρ comparable to F-values, i.e. joins in D.
- **So the missing structure is the same in every formulation tried:** the column apex (§34.3z³¹, free label cells), the column bottom (§34.3z³⁵, join construction), or joins in D (sphere construction).

## 34.3z³⁶ The Cut Lemma on every finite bounded poset (relative Lefschetz on the proper part)

The brief (ChatGPT, after f458922) proposes a relative homology obstruction on the proper-part complex. Carried out in the form below, it proves the double-cone lemma (DCL, §34.3z³⁰) outright, and with it the Cut Lemma on all finite bounded posets. The parity now comes from the contractible *interior*; no cone apex enters, which is why the apex dichotomy of §34.3z³⁵ does not apply.

**Setting.**
- D is a finite poset with bottom ⊥, C a finite poset with top ⊤, P = D ⊕ C, and P̄ = P ∖ {⊥, ⊤}.
- Cells c = (ρ, σ), ρ ⊆ D and σ ⊆ C nonempty chains, ordered by ρ ⊇ ρ′, σ ⊆ σ′.
- A labelling z is monotone with W1 (z ∈ D ⇒ z ≰ min ρ) and W2 (z ∈ C ⇒ max σ ≰ z).

**Theorem (DCL).** No such labelling exists.

**Corollary.** The Cut Lemma holds on every finite bounded poset.
- WLOG the cross order is D ⊕ C: it is the most permissive, and W1/W2 never compare a column below a row.
- A witness-free F gives the DCL labelling z(ρ, σ) = F(min ρ, max σ).

**Proof.**
1. *The spaces.* X = |Δ(D)| × |Δ(C)|, the product of two cones, is triangulated by sd X, the order complex of the cell poset. Each flag c₁ < … < c_r maps to Σλᵢ(b(ρᵢ), b(σᵢ)).
   - Its *outer boundary* is ∂ₒX = |Δ(D̄)| × |Δ(C)| ∪ |Δ(D)| × |Δ(C̄)| ≅ Δ(D̄) * Δ(C̄) = K := Δ(P̄).
   - It is triangulated by the subcomplex L of flags with ⊥ ∉ ρ₁ or ⊤ ∉ σ_r (ρ₁ the largest ρ, σ_r the largest σ).
   - The carrier of a cell is car(c) = (ρ ∖ ⊥) ∪ (σ ∖ ⊤), and that of a flag is the union, (ρ₁ ∖ ⊥) ∪ (σ_r ∖ ⊤).
2. *The labelling is a simplicial map Φ : sd X → K.* W1 gives z ≠ ⊥ and W2 gives z ≠ ⊤, so z(c) ∈ P̄. Along a flag the labels increase, so they form a chain. Write ψ = Φ|L.
3. *Subdivision.* sd : C(K) → C(L) sends τ to the sum of the flags s ∈ L with car(s) = τ and |s| = |τ|.
   - It is a chain map (mod 2), and it sends each vertex v to the single cell ({v}, {⊤}) or ({⊥}, {v}).
   - This is the statement that L subdivides K.
4. *g = ψ_# ∘ sd is zero on reduced homology.* It factors through C(sd X), and X is contractible. On H₀ it is the identity: K = Δ(D̄) * Δ(C̄) is connected when D̄ and C̄ are nonempty, and g preserves the augmentation.
5. *Hopf trace mod 2.* Σ_d tr(g on C_d(K)) = Σ_d tr(g on H_d(K)) = 1. The trace on C_d counts the flags s ∈ L with |s| = |car(s)| whose labels are exactly car(s), the *fully labelled boundary flags*. So their number is **odd**.
6. *A fully labelled boundary flag violates W1 or W2.*
   - If ⊤ ∉ σ_r: max σ_r is a label z(c_j) ≤ z(c_r), so max σ_r ≤ z(c_r), contradicting W2 at c_r.
   - If ⊥ ∉ ρ₁: min ρ₁ is a label z(c_j) ≥ z(c₁), so z(c₁) ≤ min ρ₁, contradicting W1 at c₁.
   - One of the two holds on L. ∎
- The degenerate cases D̄ = ∅ or C̄ = ∅ are the column and row lemmas (Kleene).

**Verification** (`experiments/fgcut/relcheck.py`). The script builds the cells, L and K, checks that sd is a chain map, and counts the fully labelled boundary flags for monotone labellings into P̄ sampled by SAT under random assumptions. W1/W2 are not imposed: the parity statement holds for every monotone labelling.

| instance | cells | flags of sd X | L | K | sd chain map | samples: odd / even |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| chain₂ × chain₂ | 9 | 33 | 9 | 3 | yes | 300 / 0 |
| V × Λ | 25 | 113 | 32 | 8 | yes | 251 / 0 |
| bowtie × chain₃ | 119 | 6,825 | 1,429 | 35 | yes | 300 / 0 |
| **bowtie × bowtie** | 289 | 24,353 | 5,024 | 80 | yes | 296 / 0 |

**Consequences.**
- *Finite posets are settled.* The Cut Lemma holds on every finite bounded poset, open since §34.3m. This is a paper proof; the ingredients are standard: the Hopf trace formula over 𝔽₂, contractibility of a product of cones, and the outer boundary subdividing the join (step 3, checked by machine on the instances above).
- *The meet and join conditions are unnecessary.* CLL (§34.3z²⁹, §34.3z³³) and the dismantling reduction (§34.3z³²) become special cases.
- *Why the solvers struggled.* DCL is a parity principle, and parity principles are exponentially hard for resolution. That explains why bowtie × bowtie (289 cells) resisted CDCL and CP-SAT (§34.3z³¹–³²), while Cut, which has far fewer cells, was easy.
- *Scope.* This says nothing about the infinite chain-finite case. The argument needs compactness of X, and `Frontier.lean` still reduces the standing conjecture to join-closure of R(F).

**Formalization plan.** Lean already has the cone version of the trace argument (`ConeSperner`). This proof additionally needs:
- (a) the subdivision chain map for the outer boundary, a door-counting lemma like `chain`;
- (b) a chain contraction of C(sd X), for example from the poset homotopy id ≥ (ρ ↦ ρ ∪ {⊥}) ≤ const{⊥} on the D-factor, via a prism operator;
- (c) the trace argument tr(Φ_# M) = tr(M Φ_#) with M = i_# ∘ sd, as in `trace_G`.

## 34.3z³⁷ The double-cone lemma in Lean: `RelativeCut.lean`

The §34.3z³⁶ argument is now machine-checked. It also has a simpler combinatorial form, which needs no product triangulation and no separate proof that L subdivides K.

**Theorems** (`lean/Mordor/RelativeCut.lean`, 801 lines, no `sorry`, axioms `propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`):
- `dcl (L : Labelling D C) : False`, for finite D with ⊥ and C with ⊤. A labelling is
  - a map z on pairs of finite sets,
  - monotone on nonempty chains (ρ ⊇ ρ′, σ ⊆ σ′ ⇒ z ρ σ ≤ z ρ′ σ′ in the ordinal sum D ⊕ C),
  - with W1 (z = d ∈ D ⇒ ¬ ∀ x ∈ ρ, d ≤ x) and W2 (z = c ∈ C ⇒ ¬ ∀ y ∈ σ, y ≤ c).
- `cut_finite`: **the Cut Lemma on every finite bounded poset.** For a down-set D with ⊥ ∈ D ∌ ⊤ and monotone F, some x ∈ D, y ∉ D has F(x, y) ≤ x or y ≤ F(x, y). This supersedes `cut_of_meets` and the dismantling reduction.

**The combinatorial form.**
- *Complex.* K = chains of D ⊕ (C ∖ ⊤), a cone with apex ⊥. A cone face τ ∋ ⊥ plays the role of the interior simplex (τ ∖ ⊥) * apex of §34.3z³⁶.
- *Words.* A face τ is subdivided by *words*: injective words over τ ∪ {⊤}, and, when ⊥ ∉ τ, also over τ ∪ {⊥}. A word must start with a C-letter and end with a D-letter.
  - Cut t of a word is the cell (D-letters after t, C-letters up to t). A word of length N has N − 1 cuts, increasing in the cell order, so its labels form a chain.
  - These are exactly the maximal flags of §34.3z³⁶: each step of a word either drops a D-letter from ρ or adds a C-letter to σ.
- *Door lemma (`door`).* Over 𝔽₂, deleting one label of a word over an alphabet A amounts to deleting a letter:
  - deleting an inner label is cancelled by the adjacent swap;
  - deleting the first label is cancelled by a swap unless the second letter is in D, and then it is deleting that letter;
  - deleting the last label is cancelled by a swap unless the second-to-last letter is in C, and then it is deleting that letter.
  - So Σ_{doors} = Σ_{x ∈ A} S(A ∖ x), where S(B) counts words over B with given labels.
- *Chain map (`chain`).* G σ τ = S_σ(τ ∪ ⊤) + [⊥ ∉ τ]·S_σ(τ ∪ ⊥) satisfies G·∂ = ∂·G. With the door lemma this is bookkeeping:
  - the letters ⊤ and ⊥ of the two alphabets both contribute S(τ), the *corner words* over τ itself;
  - on a base face (⊥ ∉ τ) the two copies cancel;
  - on a cone face they equal the term of the facet τ ∖ ⊥.
- *Trace.* `ConeSperner.trace_chain` gives trace G = Σ_{vertices v} G(v, {⊥}) = 1, since {⊥} has the single word ⊤⊥. This lemma is new and abstract: the mod-2 Hopf trace formula for any chain map on a cone, refactored out of `trace_G`.
- *Contradiction.* trace G = 0, because a word labelled exactly by its face is impossible (`not_full`):
  - over τ ∪ {⊤}, all labels lie above the first label, and these include every D-letter of the first cut, contradicting W1;
  - over τ ∪ {⊥}, the last label lies above every C-letter of the last cut, contradicting W2.

**Numerics** (`experiments/fgcut/wordcheck.py`). The word matrix G is a chain map and has trace 1 for random monotone labellings on chain₂ × chain₂, V × Λ and bowtie × chain₃, 20 samples each.

**Status.** The Cut Lemma for finite bounded posets is proved in Lean, together with every finite-poset corollary of §34.3m–n. The infinite chain-finite frontier (`Frontier.lean`: join-closure of R(F)) is untouched: the argument is a finite trace computation.

## 34.3z³⁸ Stabilized reachability, the ideal orbit, and finite cross-capture

Response to the ChatGPT brief "Phase II" (stabilized reachability, finite capture, derivation amalgamation). Everything below is in `lean/Mordor/Stable.lean` with the standard axioms, except §4 (experiments).

### 1. Finite stages and finite support (Lean)

| theorem | statement | hypotheses |
|---|---|---|
| `R_eq_iUnion_Rk` | R(F) = ⋃ₖ Rₖ | none; the rules are unary, so ω stages suffice |
| `sSup_finite_support`, `sInf_finite_support` | ⋁S = ⋁E (⋀S = ⋀E) for a finite E ⊆ S | ACC (DCC); from Mathlib `WellFoundedGT.isSupFiniteCompact` |
| `sSup_Rk_stable` | ∃N, ∀n ≥ N, ⋁Rₙ = ⋁R(F) | ACC |
| `stable_support` | ⋁R(F) = x₁ ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ xₘ with all xᵢ in one stage R_N | ACC |

### 2. The ideal orbit always reaches ⊤, so the gap s < r* is the whole problem (Lean)

- `eq_top_of_kstep`: if κ⁺r ≤ r then r = ⊤ (DCC). Reason: ↓r is then closed, and principal cuts have witnesses (`cut_principal_down`).
- `rk_eventually_top`: under ACC and DCC, r_M = ⊤ for some M. So the stabilized ideal orbit is r* = ⊤ for **every** F.
  - The brief asked whether the stable gap ⋁R_N < r* could be exploited. That gap is just "⋁R(F) < ⊤ or ⋁R(F) ∉ R(F)", the conjecture itself. `orbit_lax` carries no information at the limit.
- `sSup_R_mem_iff`: ⋁R(F) ∈ R(F) iff R(F) = L (DCC only). So the brief's "stable-stage capture lemma" is *equivalent* to the conjecture for each F, not an intermediate step. Stabilization always happens, and its consequences a(R) ⊆ ↓s and b⁻¹(R) ⊆ ↓s hold for any s ⊇ R.
- What the orbit does give is a localization:
  - `cutMaps_iff_rk_mem`: Cut for F iff every rₖ ∈ R(F).
  - `cutMaps_iff_kstep`: Cut for F iff R(F) is closed under κ⁺r = r ⊔ a r ⊔ ⋁{y | b y ≤ r}.
  - `orbit_escape`: a counterexample has an r ∈ R(F) with κ⁺r = r ⊔ a r ⊔ y₁ ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ yₘ ∉ R(F), where a r and every yᵢ (b yᵢ ≤ r) are in R(F).
  - **The decisive missing implication is therefore one structured join:** for r ∈ R(F), r ⊔ a(r) ⊔ ⋁B(r) ∈ R(F), where B(r) = {y | b y ≤ r}. This is a three-term join with B(r) ⊆ R(F), rather than arbitrary pairs.

### 3. Finite cross-capture: the poset theorem removes ⊓ and ⊔ (Lean)

- `RelativeCut.cut_cross`: in **any bounded poset**, a finite S ∋ ⊥, ⊤ with F(S ∩ D, S ∖ D) ⊆ S forces a witness for the cut D. The proof labels the double-cone cells of the finite posets S ∩ D and S ∖ D by F(min ρ, max σ) and applies `dcl`. `cut_finite` is now the case S = univ.
- `cutCells_of_finite_Fclosure`: a finite **F-closure** of {⊥, ⊤} forces Cut, on any complete lattice and with no chain condition.
  - This strictly strengthens `FinGen.cut_of_finite_closure` (§34.3o), which needed a finite (⊓, ⊔, F)-closure.
  - Finite capture no longer needs the boundary skeleton's meets of D-maxima or joins of complement-minima.
- `R_univ_of_cross`: if a finite S ∋ ⊥, ⊤ is closed under F on (S ∩ R(F)) × (S ∖ R(F)), then R(F) = L.
- `crossClosure_infinite`: in a counterexample, the R(F)-relative cross-closure S_ω of {⊥, ⊤} is **infinite**. This answers §5 of the brief: the boundary-expansion process never terminates in a counterexample.
  - The aggregate anchors ⋀P₀ and ⋁U₀ change only finitely often (chain-finiteness).
  - The residual is an infinite, countable, chain-finite **bounded subposet** S_ω, generated from ⊥, ⊤ by F on cross pairs, on which (F, R(F) ∩ S_ω) is witness-free.
- **Reduction.** The standing conjecture follows from the *poset* conjecture: Cut holds on every countable chain-finite bounded poset. The lattice structure is used nowhere after restriction to S_ω.
  - The poset conjecture holds for finite posets (§34.3z³⁷) and for posets with no chain of seven elements (§34.3f–h, infinite ones included).
  - Completion transfer (§34.3z³¹.1) shows that a poset counterexample need not lift to a lattice one. So the poset conjecture may be strictly stronger.

### 4. Critical pairs (§4 of the brief): experiments

Last rules of two derivations (the critical-pair table):
- *Downward* with anything reduces to the other derivation.
- *a/a* reduces to the bases, since a(z) ≥ a(x) ⊔ a(y) for any reachable z ≥ x, y.
- The irreducible pairs involve b⁻¹. `orbit_escape` concentrates all of them into the join r ⊔ a r ⊔ ⋁B(r).

`experiments/stable/critpair.py` and `delay.py`: random monotone F on finite lattices (where R(F) = L by `cut_finite`, so only stage-sensitive statements are non-vacuous).

| test | statement | result |
|---|---|---|
| E1 | B(r) is directed, i.e. b(⋁B(r)) ≤ r | **false generically**: violated in 947–2922 of 1500–3600 (F, r) pairs, on each of M₃, N₅, 2×3, M₄, B₃, 3×3, M₃×C₂, 3×4 (300 random F each) |
| E2 | delay of κ⁺ inside R: least k with κ⁺r ∈ Θ⁺ᵏ(↓r) | at most 2 in random samples. Hill-climbing (1500 mutations × 4 restarts) finds **3 on N₅** and 2 on M₃ and B₃; only 1 found on the larger lattices tried (search, not exhaustive) |
| E3 | first stage containing rₖ, minus k | at most 1 |

- **E1:** the b⁻¹/b⁻¹ critical pair is genuine. ⋁B(r) is usually not itself rescued by r, so it enters R(F) only through further a- and b⁻¹-steps. No single-step exchange rule exists.
- **E2:** the delay is not uniformly 1. Whether it is bounded is open: the brief warns against uniform depth bounds, and the searches are small.
- Since r ∈ R(F) gives Θ⁺ᵏ(↓r) ⊆ R(F) for all k, **any finite delay at r proves κ⁺r ∈ R(F)**. On chain-finite lattices the conjecture is exactly "the delay is finite along the orbit". The state-dependent measure the brief asks for is this delay.

### 5. Status and next step

- **Proved (Lean):** §1–§3.
- **Open:**
  - the single implication "r ∈ R(F) ⟹ κ⁺r ∈ R(F)" on chain-finite lattices;
  - equivalently, the countable-poset Cut for the cross-closure S_ω.
- **Next experiment:** search for an infinite chain-finite *poset* counterexample (edge-height ≥ 6), translation-periodic in an index ℤ. Its F must fail to descend to every finite quotient, since finite posets satisfy Cut. A periodic counterexample would show that the poset reduction loses the lattice structure. A proof that none exists would point to a compactness argument on S_ω.

## 34.3z³⁹ Periodic counterexamples are impossible; the minimal counterexample is rigid

§34.3z³⁸ reduced the conjecture to Cut on countable chain-finite bounded posets and recommended searching for a translation-periodic infinite poset counterexample at edge-height ≥ 6. The search was built and run (`experiments/periodic/`). It is UNSAT everywhere, for a structural reason that turns into a theorem.

**Search** (`periodic.py`, independent checker `verify.py`).
- *Elements:* constants K (⊥, ⊤ and a few more, each on a fixed side of the cut) and indexed elements (i, t) with i ∈ ℤ and t in a finite set of types.
- *Order:* translation invariant. Between indexed elements it depends on the types and on the class of the index difference, clamped to [−W−1, W+1]. Same-type elements at different indices are incomparable.
- *Height:* a rank on kinds bounds the height by H.
- *F on cross pairs:* a constant, or an indexed element at the index of x or of y plus an offset e with |e| ≤ E.
- *Axioms:* transitivity, monotonicity in each argument and no-witness are checked on index windows that cover every class pattern.
- A model would be a genuine infinite bounded poset of edge-height ≤ H with a witness-free cut. F extends by ⊥ on D × D and ⊤ elsewhere.

| H | D/C types | D/C constants | W, E | result |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2, 3, 4, 5 | ≤ 3/3 | 2/2 | 1, 1 | UNSAT (control: H ≤ 5 is `Ground.cut_height5`) |
| 3 | 2/2 | 2/2 | 1, 1 | **SAT** without no-witness; SAT without W1 (control). `verify.py` confirms the model on the window [−6, 6] except W1 |
| 6 | 3/3, 4/4 | 2/2, 3/3 | (1,1), (2,1) | UNSAT |
| 7, 8, 10 | 4/4, 5/5 | 2/2, 3/3 | (1,1), (1,2) | UNSAT |
| 6 | 3/3 | 2/2 | 1, 1 | UNSAT **even without monotonicity** |

The last row explains the rest. F on a pair of constants must be a constant, so the constants form a finite set S ∋ ⊥, ⊤ with F(S ∩ D, S ∖ D) ⊆ S, and `RelativeCut.cut_cross` then produces a witness inside S.

**Theorem (`Stable.cutCells_of_equivariant`, Lean, standard axioms).** Let σ be an order automorphism of a complete lattice that commutes with F (F(σx, σy) = σF(x, y)) and has only finitely many fixed points. Then the Cut Lemma holds for F.
- Proof: Fix σ contains ⊥ and ⊤ and is closed under F, so `cutCells_of_finite_Fclosure` applies.
- Example: every shift-equivariant F on lattices whose only shift-invariant elements are ⊥ and ⊤, such as M_ℤ, or ℤ-indexed versions of D_ω and E_ω. This explains, uniformly, the empty translation-invariant searches of §31.4–§31.6.

**Rigidity.** Every automorphism of a counterexample (P, F, D) fixes the F-generated part ⟨⊥, ⊤⟩_F pointwise. The fixed set of any group of automorphisms is again a counterexample, closed under F, so it is infinite (`cut_cross`). In particular the minimal counterexamples (the F-closure, or the R(F)-relative cross-closure of §34.3z³⁸) are **rigid, term-generated and infinite**.
- Symmetry ansätze (periodic, indiscernible, EM-type) cannot produce a counterexample unless their invariant part already is one.
- The right search space is term models. That is exactly the ground refutation of §34.3g–h.
- Since `cut_finite`, that search can stop in only two ways: **UNSAT** (Cut at that height, infinite posets included) or never. A SAT model closed under the term rule is now impossible.

**H = 6 term search, rerun with the new interpretation.** Two runs:
- `run_height.py 6 4000 1 forget=10⁶`: still SAT at 478 terms after about 45 min.
- `run_height.py 6 4000 2 allmid,forget=10⁶,cd195`: still SAT at 701 terms after about 40 min, 1.8 GB.

Both stopped; this is inconclusive, as in §34.3h. Because of `cut_finite`, a run that never ends would now mean an infinite height-6 poset counterexample. Such a counterexample is necessarily rigid and term-generated.

**Next step.** The remaining options are proof-theoretic:
- a uniform ground refutation at height 6, with a longer run on a larger machine;
- or an argument for infinite term models, e.g. compactness along the cross-closure stages S_n, where every finite S_n has an escaping cross value (§34.3z³⁸).

## 34.3z⁴⁰ ℕ-indexed models, and the shape of the height-6 term models

**ℕ-indexed ansatz** (`experiments/periodic/periodicN.py`). This ansatz escapes `cutCells_of_equivariant`.
- *Structure:* generic indices i ≥ 0, a boundary at 0, and F allowed to output an absolute index. So F(⊥, ⊤) may be a generic element near 0, and the shift is only an embedding, not commuting with F at the boundary.
- *Search:* SAT with axiom instances on a window [0, M], then counterexample-guided refinement. Each model is checked on [0, 12–14], and its violated transitivity, monotonicity and no-witness instances are added.

Results:
- With a fixed window (M = 4) even the **H = 5 control is SAT**. `verify.py` shows the model failing monotonicity at index 5, outside the window: windowed models are artifacts. UNSAT on any window is sound; SAT is not.
- With refinement, H = 5 (window 4, check on [0, 14]) ran 15 rounds in 15 min. Violations did not decrease monotonically (220, 660, 220), and the run timed out. Runs at M = 6 (H = 5 and 6, 2.2–6.7M clauses) did not finish their first rounds and were stopped.
- **Inconclusive.** The ansatz is too expensive in this form. Since minimal counterexamples are rigid and term-generated (§34.3z³⁹), the term search below is the canonical one anyway.

**Shape of the term models** (`experiments/sat/model_shape.py`).
- At H = 6 with the standing tightness selection (`room='fit'`), the SAT model after 250 terms has only **24 distinct elements**: 6 in D, 18 in C, ranks 0–6.
- **Pathology:** a candidate F(x, y) whose pair of model classes already carries an F-term has exactly one place, so tightness ranks it first. Most added terms are forced duplicates.
- **Frontier selection** (`room='front'`, `run_height.py … front`) restricts candidates to class pairs with no F-term, one shallowest representative each. The frontier is never empty: a model whose quotient is closed on cross pairs would be a finite counterexample (`cut_finite`).

| H | fit (§34.3h) | front |
|---|---|---|
| 4 | 19 terms, 0.1 s | 17 terms, 0.0 s |
| 5 | 128 terms, 17 s | **122 terms, 2.6–5.5 s** |
| 6 | SAT at 478 terms / 45 min | SAT at 553 terms / 31 min (1 per round), 593 terms / 23 min (3 per round); stopped |

- **The H = 6 models collapse to a chain.** With frontier selection, the model after 200 terms has 7 distinct elements, ⊥ < d₁ < d₂ < d₃ < c₁ < c₂ < ⊤ (the longest chain the height bound allows). All terms sit on it.
- Each new frontier term is absorbed by moving terms between classes.
- A finite chain is never a model (`cut_finite`), so these are partial models: one class pair has no term. They survive because the ground clauses constrain only existing terms.
- Refuting H = 6 therefore means exhausting the ways to place a growing term set on short chains and small posets with one hole. The SAT solver does this by re-placement, not by any structural argument. This is consistent with §34.3h: the runs say nothing about an infinite model.

## 34.3z⁴¹ Structured-join closure: sequential insertion, delays, the bottom anchor, term trees

Response to the ChatGPT brief "Prove structured-join closure". New Lean in `Stable.lean` and `RelativeCut.lean` (standard axioms, full build); experiments in `experiments/stable/`.

### A. Audit
- The audit of §34.3z³⁸–⁴⁰ against `Stable.lean`, `Orbit.lean` and `Frontier.lean` found nothing to correct.
- The gap is (SJ): r ∈ R(F) ⟹ κ⁺r = r ⊔ a r ⊔ ⋁B(r) ∈ R(F), where B(r) = {y | b y ≤ r} (`cutMaps_iff_kstep`).
- Already refuted or shown equivalent: directedness of B(r), stable-stage capture, a delay bound of 1, periodic and EM-type ansätze.

### B. Sequential insertion (Lean)
- `kstep_of_base_insert` (ACC): Base (u ∈ R ⟹ u ⊔ a u ∈ R) and Insert (u ∈ R, b y ≤ u ⟹ u ⊔ a u ⊔ y ∈ R) give (SJ).
  - Proof: finite support of ⋁B(r), then Finset induction.
  - Each generator is inserted at the current anchor u, with b y ≤ r ≤ u. The empty support is the Base case.
- `cutMaps_iff_base_insert` (ACC, DCC): **Base ∧ Insert ⟺ Cut for F.** Quantified over R(F), the proposed local lemmas are exactly as strong as the conjecture.
