  pair is separated by ⊥, and the run ends stuck at (q_m, ⊤) after **2k − 1** non-goal stages.

The simulator confirms monotonicity, the cut property, height 3 and length 2k − 1 for every k = 3, …, 40 (|L| up to 82).

**Consequences.**
- **RB(3) is false.** The response-run length is unbounded at edge-height 3, where Cut is a theorem. So
  `cut_of_resp_bound` (§34.3z¹³¹), which is still valid, cannot prove Cut at any height ≥ 3: its finite hypothesis
  already fails.
- The response rule is too weak because it never jumps to high separators. On the same family, the
  maximal-separator process ends after **1** stage for k = 3–8: the first maximal separator is a coatom (a line or Q).
  Descending runs exist with maximal and with general separators.
- **The transfer obstacle for existential runs is the stuck test.** For a run in the finite L′ = S (with
  F′ = cl_S ∘ F):
  - badness and legality of general separators correspond to those in L (`bad_iff`);
  - "stuck" in L′ (y ≤ cl_S F(x, y)) implies stuck in L only when F(x, y) ∈ S;
  - a run chosen *after* S is fixed may use any pair of S, so S would have to be closed under F on cross pairs, which
    is infinite in a counterexample (`crossClosure_infinite`).

  The response transfer works because the run is determined first and S is built around it. For existential runs this
  order cannot be reproduced.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite. The
existential conjectures (∃-SB, shortest-run descent) survive. The canonical response route is closed.

## 34.3z¹³³ Follow-up (no brief): term rules — every tested absolute rule is width-dependent at height 3

**Motivation.** On the near-pencil family of §34.3z¹³² the maximal-separator process wins at stage 0 by taking a
line above p, i.e. a *high* separator. The response rule never jumps. A high separator can still be absolute if it is
a *term* over the run: the response transfer argument (§34.3z¹³¹) only uses that each choice is determined by finitely
many elements, which S can then contain.

**Term rules** (`experiments/separation/term_rule.py`). Fix a stage T with bad pairs B and responses
R = {F(x, y) : (x, y) ∈ B}.
- C₀ = T ∪ R and C_{j+1} = C_j ∪ {F(a, b), a ∨ b, and optionally a ∧ b : a, b ∈ C_j}; C = C_d at depth d.
- Rule *term-d-all*: for every bad pair (x, y), add each separator s ∈ C (F(x, y) ≤ s, y ≰ s) that is maximal among
  the separators in C. Rule *term-d-one*: add one of greatest height.
- *Transfer class.* Bounded d keeps C finite and term-determined. The full closure (d = ω) does not: in a chain-finite
  lattice the {∧, ∨}-closure of a finite set can be infinite. Classically, four points in general position in PG(2, ℚ),
  a lattice of height 3, generate the whole plane over the prime field ℚ. So only bounded-depth rules are candidates
  for a transfer theorem. Such a theorem is not formalized; the ω rule is tested only as a limit case.

**First tests (term-1, no meets).**
- Near-pencil family of §34.3z¹³²: **1** stage for k = 3..12, against 2k − 1 for the response rule.
- The 8 stage records and all 17 stored counterexample JSONs of earlier rounds descend (maximum gap).
- Mutation hunt (`hunt`, 4 × 500 s, seeds 141–144): 186,402 distinct instances with existential minimum ≥ 3.
  Maximum-gap descent fails for term1_all in 2,146 (1.15 %), for term1_one in 2,151 and for the response rule in
  6,731 (3.61 %). The longest term-1 run is 6 stages, at H = 6 and 7.

Mutations of hard instances therefore look harmless, as the response rule's did in §34.3z¹³¹. The exact maxima decide
otherwise.

**Exact maxima** (`term_sat.py`). This is the SAT encoding of `response_sat.py` with the stage update replaced by the
layered closure C₀ ⊆ … ⊆ C_d, the candidate and maximality literals, and N[m, s] = "s is added". k increases until
UNSAT, and every model is decoded and re-simulated with `term_run`. Depth |L| − 2 saturates the closure, so its
maxima are the exact ω-rule maxima.

| Rule (all-variant) | Near-pencils on k points (\|L\| = 2k + 2) | Height-3 linear spaces (seed 1) |
|---|---|---|
| response (§34.3z¹³²) | k = 4..7: 7, 9, 11, 13 = 2k − 1 | 6–10 |
| term-1, F/∨ | k = 4..7: **5, 6, 7, 8 = k + 1** | 5 pts: 5, 6; 6 pts: 6, 5; 7 pts: 6, 7 |
| term-1, F/∨/∧ | k = 4..7: 5, 6, 7, 8 = k + 1 | same as above |
| term-2, F/∨/∧ | k = 4..7: 4, 5, 6, 7 = k | 6 pts: 6, 5 |
| term-3, F/∨/∧ | k = 4..6: 4, 5, 6 = k | — |
| term-ω, F/∨/∧ | k = 3..6: **2, 3, 4, 5 = k − 1** | 5 pts: 4, 3 |

Chains give H − 1 (no choice exists). Output `out_term_rule.txt`.

**An explicit family** (`term_family.py`).
- *Lattice:* near-pencil on k = m + 1 points, with p = 0, lines lᵢ = {0, i} and the long line Λ = {1, …, m}.
- *Cut:* D = {⊥} ∪ atoms ∪ {lⱼ : j odd}.
- *Bifunction:* F is the least monotone map above the generators
  - g(⊥, ⊤) = {0}, g(⊤, ⊥) = l₁;
  - for odd j < m: g(lⱼ, ⊤) = l_{j+1}, g(lⱼ, l_{j+1}) = {j+1}, and g({j+1}, ⊤) = l_{j+2} when j + 2 ≤ m;
  - g(l_m, ⊤) = ⊤ for odd m.
- *Design:* each new line is the *only* separator of its pair, because lines are coatoms. Λ enters the depth-1 closure
  only once two of its atoms are present.

The simulator confirms monotonicity and the cut property, and gives these run lengths:

| Rule | Run length on the family |
|---|---|
| term1_all, term1_one | **k + 1** for k = 5..12; checked to k = 14 in an earlier run of the same script |
| F/∨ closure, depth 2 | k |
| F/∨ closure, depths 3 and 4 | k − 3 |
| with meets, depth 1 | k + 1 |
| with meets, depth 2 | k |
| with meets, ω | 1–2 |
| response rule | 1 |
| maximal-separator process (existential minimum) | 1 |

Shortest maximal runs descend.

**The ω witness** (`term_omega_witness_k5.json`, near-pencil k = 5, D = {⊥, {0}, {1}, {4}}).
1. At stage 0 the closure C is already all of L, so term-ω-all coincides with the all-maximal-separator process.
2. The bad pair (⊥, ⊤) with F = {4} has maximal separators {0, 4} and Λ; both are added.
3. Along the run every later bad pair (an atom against Λ) has a unique maximal separator in L, the line through 0 and
   the response atom. The run is forced and has 4 stages.
4. Taking {0, 4} alone ends in 1 stage.

The cost comes from the "add every maximal separator" policy, already refuted for descent in §34.3z¹²⁷, not from
the depth of C.

**Reading.**
- On near-pencils every tested deterministic rule has run length growing linearly with width at height 3, where Cut
  is a theorem. This covers the response rule, term-d for d ≤ 3 with and without meets, and term-ω. These are
  SAT-exact maxima for k ≤ 7, plus explicit families for all k: response 2k − 1, term-1 k + 1.
- No bounded-length transfer theorem in the style of `cut_of_resp_bound` can therefore be built on any of these
  rules.
- What wins in every instance is a *selective* choice among maximal separators: choose {0, 4} and not Λ; choose an
  even line, outside D, on the term family. The existential minimum stays at 1 throughout.
- *Conjecture-level observation, not tested here:* a parameter-free rule is invariant under automorphisms of
  (L, ≤, F, D), so it must add whole orbits of maximal separators. Selective choices can therefore be canonical only
  when F breaks the symmetry.
- **Remaining routes:**
  1. existential runs (∃-SB, shortest-run descent), which still need a transfer mechanism past the stuck test of
     §34.3z¹³²;
  2. a rule with *lookahead*: choose a separator that creates no new bad pair. It is term-determined only if the
     lookahead is bounded, and one-step lookahead was refuted for descent (§34.3z¹²⁶), not for length.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite. Solver
maxima are evidence about finite lattices, not proofs; no Lean changes this round.

## 34.3z¹³⁴ Follow-up (no brief): lookahead does not help; the deficit is in the term closure, not in the selection

**The lookahead rule** (`experiments/separation/look_rule.py`). Use the term closure C of §34.3z¹³³, with meets. For
each bad pair, add those C-maximal separators s whose addition alone creates no new bad pair: every bad pair of
meetClose(T ∪ {s}) is already bad at T. If no such s exists, add all of them. The rule is still finitely determined
at bounded depth: it uses only F-values on T ∪ {s}.

**Heuristic tests.** Run lengths at depth 1 / 2 / ω:

| Instance | Lookahead rule | Comparison |
|---|---|---|
| response family (§34.3z¹³²) | 1, 1, 2 for all k ≤ 10 | — |
| term family (§34.3z¹³³) | k + 1, 1, 1 | — |
| stored counterexamples, \|L\| = 12, H = 4 | 1–3 | existential maximal minimum 3–4 |
| nested-nested records | 3–4 | existential maximal minimum 6 |

The lookahead rule may use non-maximal separators, which is why it can beat the existential *maximal* minimum.

**Exact maxima** (`look_sat.py`: TermEnc with the selection replaced by the lookahead literals; every model is
re-simulated):

| Lattice | Lookahead, depth 1 | Lookahead, depth 2 |
|---|---|---|
| chains | H − 1 | — |
| near-pencils, k = 3..6 | 3, 5, 6, 7 | k = 4..6: 4, 5, 6 |
| linear spaces on 6 points | 6, 5 | — |

These are **identical to the plain term rules**: lookahead buys nothing in the worst case.

**Why: the witnesses are forced** (`forced_check.py`).
- Along the SAT witnesses' runs, every bad pair has *exactly one* C-maximal separator at every stage. This holds for
  the depth-1 witnesses at k = 4, 5, 6 and the lookahead witness at k = 5, and for the depth-2 lookahead witness at
  k = 5; the depth-2 witness at k = 6 has one stage with two.
- On the look1 witness at k = 5, the stage-0 pair (⊥, ⊤) has four L-maximal separators.
  - Taking {0,1} or {0,3} alone leaves no bad pair, so the existential minimum is 1.
  - {0,2} lies in D and leaves (l₂, ⊤) bad; {0,4} leaves (⊥, l₄) bad.
  - C at stage 0 contains only {0,4}, and the run zigzags from there.
- A forced run is followed by **every** policy that adds, per bad pair, a nonempty set of C-maximal separators:
  all, one, lookahead, any potential, random choice.

**Forced maxima** (`forced_sat.py`: TermEnc plus "each bad pair has at most one C-maximal separator", re-checked by
`forced_check`). On near-pencils:

| Depth | Forced maximum |
|---|---|
| 1 | k = 4..7: 5, 6, 7, 8 = k + 1 |
| 2 | k = 4..6: 4, 5, 6 = k |
| 3 | k = 4, 5: 4, 5 |

These equal the unrestricted term-rule maxima of §34.3z¹³³. The explicit term family is forced at depth 1, with
meets, for every k = 3..14, with run length k + 1 for k ≥ 5.

**Statement (solver and simulation evidence).** Consider any rule that chooses, for each bad pair, a nonempty set of
maximal separators within the depth-d closure of stage ∪ responses under F, ∨, ∧.
- For d = 1, the worst-case run length on height-3 near-pencils is at least k + 1. This is shown by an explicit
  family, simulated for k ≤ 14.
- For d = 2, 3 it is at least k on the computed sizes (SAT).

So no selection refinement can rescue bounded-depth term rules. The obstruction is that the adversary controls which
coatoms F places in the closure.

**Scope.** Policies that may add *non-maximal* C-separators are not covered: the response separator F(x, y) itself
is one, with maxima 2k − 1. Covering them would require a ∀∃ (QBF) encoding. No Lean changes.

**Reading.** In the separator process, every finitely determined route tested so far fails at height 3:
- response, term-d and lookahead rules, and forced runs;
- the transfer of existential runs, blocked by the stuck test (§34.3z¹³²).

What remains is the existential side: ∃-SB, shortest-run descent and ∃-gap-RP, which hold in all tests. Any proof
through them needs a new transfer idea that does not choose the run inside a finite S.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite.

## 34.3z¹³⁵ Follow-up (no brief): restricted existential descent survives; restriction does not buy universality

**Why restrict.** The existential conjectures concern arbitrary chain-finite L, and Lean already turns any one of
them into Cut (`cut_of_max_gap_drop`, `cut_of_gap_parent`). §§34.3z¹³²–¹³⁴ closed the finitely determined rules. A
direct proof needs a *global* construction of the run. The narrower its choice space, the better.

**Restrictions** (`experiments/separation/restrict_audit.py`), applied per bad pair to its L-maximal separators. Each
falls back to all of them when it would leave none:

| Mode | Allowed separators |
|---|---|
| nonD | those outside D |
| good | those whose addition alone creates no new bad pair |
| both | nonD, then good |

The winning moves of §§34.3z¹³³–¹³⁴ were of these kinds. Two readings are tested:
- *existential*: some maximum-gap-descending run uses only allowed choices;
- *universal* (`_forall`): every such run descends.

**Results** (solver-free exhaustive search per instance; evidence only).

| Instances | Existential, all four modes | Universal |
|---|---|---|
| 8 stage records, 24 stored counterexamples and witnesses of §§34.3z¹²⁶–¹³⁴ | holds everywhere | fails on 18 / 12 / 11 / 11 of the 24 files (all / nonD / good / both) and on 4 / 4 / 3 / 3 of the 8 records |
| mutation hunt, seeds 151–154 (existential only), 184,547 instances with existential minimum ≥ 3 | holds everywhere | — |
| mutation hunt, seeds 155–158, 172,907 instances | holds everywhere | fails in 42.41 % (all, nonD) and 42.31 % (good, both) |

- On the stage records the 'both' filter reduces the mean number of choice combinations per reachable state from
  1.69 to 1.39, and leaves a unique choice in 70.7 % of states. The nonD filter acts on 36 of 92 bad-pair
  occurrences.
- On the stored files the restrictions do help: the six term and lookahead witnesses of §§34.3z¹³³–¹³⁴ become
  universally descending under every restriction. In the mutation hunt, however, nonD never changed the universal
  verdict (equal counts, and nonD ⊆ all), and good changed it in only 0.1 % of instances.

**Anatomy of a universal failure** (`state_graph_fail_a_s111.json`, |L| = 12, H = 4, mode both). Elements are
written as bitmasks.

| Stage | Max gap | Bad pair (x, y) | F(x, y) | Separator added |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 8 | (0, 1111) | 1000 | 1011 (outside D) |
| 1 | 7 | (0, 1011) | 1000 | 1110 (outside D, good) |
| 2 | 5 | (1010, 1110) | 1100 | 1100, the *only* maximal separator, in D |

The stage-2 addition creates the bad pair (1100, 1011) with gap 5 again. Every stage-2 choice was locally admissible;
the failure was fixed one stage earlier. This is the exchange phenomenon of §34.3z¹²⁷ (revision depth 1–3). Neither
D-avoidance nor one-step bad-pair avoidance removes it.

**Reading.**
- *Existential conjecture, sharpened:* "∃ descending run choosing, at each bad pair, maximal separators outside D
  and creating no new bad pair whenever such exist." It survives every test, so any proof of the existential
  conjecture may restrict its choices this way.
- Universal descent is as false under restriction as without it. A proof cannot be a local greedy argument even
  inside the restricted class: it must plan across at least two stages.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite. No
Lean changes.

## 34.3z¹³⁶ Follow-up (no brief): the maximal-separator conjectures are false at edge-height 3; the general-separator form survives with the exact bound H − 1

**Method: exact maxima of the existential minimum** (`experiments/separation/exist_cegar.py`). The question is
whether some monotone F and cut D on a fixed lattice L have existential minimum ≥ k, i.e. every maximal-separator
run needs at least k non-goal stages.
- *Outer loop:* SAT (CaDiCaL) over F (one-hot, monotone) and D (cut).
- *Inner check:* each candidate is checked exactly in Python. If a terminating run of fewer than k stages exists,
  one such run is blocked with the clause

  ¬(bad set of T_i equals the run's for every stage i ∧ every chosen s is ≥ F(x, y) ∧ goal at the last stage).
- *Soundness:* maximality of a separator s of (x, y) is static once F(x, y) ≤ s, so the clause is satisfied by every
  instance with existential minimum ≥ k. UNSAT therefore proves the maximum is < k, and a SAT model is re-verified
  with `exist_min`.
- *Variants:* `--nodesc` blocks descending runs instead, which searches for instances with *no*
  maximum-gap-descending run. `--general` replaces maximal separators by general ones (any s ≥ F(x, y) with
  y ≰ s).

All of the following are solver results on fixed finite lattices, not proofs.

**1. Maximal separators: the existential minimum grows linearly with width at height 3.**

| Lattice | Height | Exact maximum |
|---|---|---|
| chains with 3–6 elements | 2–5 | H − 1 |
| near-pencils, k = 3, …, 9 points (\|L\| = 8, …, 20) | 3 | **2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 = k − 1** |
| linear spaces on 5–7 points (6 lattices) | 3 | 3 |
| closure systems on 5 points (8 lattices) | 5 | 4, 5, 6, ≥ 6 |

The earlier hill-climbing record at H = 3 was 2. Mutations of the stage records never produced the structure below.

**The line-cycle family** (`cycle_family.py`).
- *Lattice:* the near-pencil on k points, with lines lᵢ = {0, i} and long line Λ.
- *Cut:* D = L ∖ {⊤}.
- *Bifunction:* F is the least monotone map with F(⊥, ⊤) = {0} and F(lᵢ, ⊤) = l_{i+1}, indices cyclic.
- *Separators:* the maximal separators of (⊥, ⊤) are the lines. A line is a coatom, so the only separator of
  (lᵢ, ⊤) is l_{i+1}, and that pair has gap 4.
- *Runs:* every maximal run reads 6, 4, 4, … and walks the cycle.

The simulator confirms monotonicity and the cut property for k = 3..9:
- the existential minimum is **k − 1**;
- for k ≥ 4 no maximal run has strictly decreasing maximum gap;
- with general separators the response {0} ends the run at once.

**Minimal counterexample** (`nodesc_sweep.py`, all closure systems on 4 points up to point permutation;
`descent_free_min6.json`).
- *Lattice:* the 6-element lattice ⊥ < a < c₁, c₂, c₃ < ⊤ (M₃ with an extra bottom), height 3.
- *Cut:* D = L ∖ {⊤}.
- *Bifunction:* F(⊥, ⊤) = F(a, ⊤) = a; F(c₁, ⊤) = c₃, F(c₂, ⊤) = c₁, F(c₃, ⊤) = c₂; F = ⊥ elsewhere.
- *The three maximal runs* each read 6, then 4, then 4. The exhaustive tree is in `out_exist_cegar.txt` (`cycle_family.py tree`).
- *Minimality:* every lattice with at most 5 elements has at most 4 join-irreducibles, so it is in the sweep. The
  `--all-heights` sweep up to |L| ≤ 6 finds only this instance's lattice.

Among all 162 closure systems on 4 points of height ≥ 3, 21 carry descent-free instances, with |L| from 6 to 16.

**What is refuted.** These are the finite, stuck-exempt forms tested in §§34.3z¹²¹–¹³⁵, for *maximal* separators
(`Run`, `IsMaxSep`):
- existential maximum-gap descent;
- ∃-gap-RP, equivalent to it by `parent_iff_max_drop`;
- shortest-run descent;
- any width-independent ∃-SB(3).

*Caveat.* The Lean implications (`cut_of_max_gap_drop`, `cut_of_gap_parent`) concern runs in a witness-free L. There
the conjectures are equivalent to Cut, and they are not refuted. The cycle forces F({0}, ⊤) ≤ ⋀ lines = {0}, a lower
witness. What fails is the hope that maximal-separator descent is a general combinatorial mechanism visible on
finite instances.

**2. Lean: the gap implications need only closed stages** (`lean/Mordor/GeneralRun.lean`; standard axioms only).

| Result | Statement |
|---|---|
| `Closed T` | every stage is meet-closed and contains ⊥, ⊤ |
| `cut_of_gap_parent_closed`, `cut_of_max_gap_drop_closed` | the gap parent property, resp. strictly dropping maximum gap (stuck stages exempt), on **any** closed sequence of finite families forces a witness |
| `GStep`, `GRun` | runs whose separators are general separators of the stage's bad pairs, each bad pair receiving one |
| `step_gstep`, `run_grun` | every maximal-separator run is a general run |
| `grun_closed` | general runs are closed |
| `cut_of_terminal_grun` | a general run reaching a terminal or stuck stage forces a witness |
| `cut_of_gap_parent_general`, `cut_of_max_gap_drop_general` | the gap implications for general runs |

So general-separator descent (or any general stage bound) at edge-height 6 implies Cut at edge-height 6, exactly as
the maximal forms did.

**3. General separators: descent never fails, and the exact bound is H − 1.**

*Descent-free search* (`--nodesc --general`). UNSAT means no F, D on the lattice lacks a descending general run.
- **UNSAT on all 162 closure systems on 4 points** of height ≥ 3 (exhaustive).
- UNSAT on 35 of 36 random closure systems on 5 points (heights 4–5); one undecided at 40 s.
- UNSAT on 16 of 30 on 6 points (heights 5–6); 14 undecided at 50 s.
- UNSAT on near-pencils 4 and 7, and on three 7-point linear spaces.
- No descent-free instance was found anywhere.

*Exact maximum existential minimum with general separators:*

| Lattices | Height | Exact maximum |
|---|---|---|
| all 179 closure systems on 4 points | 2, 3, 4 | 1, 2, 3 = **H − 1** on every lattice |
| near-pencils k = 3..7, 6-point linear spaces | 3 | 2 |
| eight 5-point closure systems | 5 | 4 |
| chains | any | H − 1 (lower bound) |

For comparison, maximal separators exceed H − 1 on 9 of the 64 four-point lattices of height 3 and on 36 of the 98
of height 4.

**Conjectures, from these solver results.**
- **GSB(H):** every finite instance of edge-height H has a general-separator run with at most H − 1 non-goal stages.
  This bound is attained by chains.
- **GD(H):** every finite instance has a maximum-gap-descending general run.

These are now the surviving forms. In the witness-free setting they imply Cut via `cut_of_terminal_grun` and
`cut_of_max_gap_drop_general`. The response rule (§34.3z¹³²) shows that no *canonical* general rule achieves the
bound, and the transfer obstacle of §34.3z¹³² still applies to existential general runs.

**Reading.** The hidden variable behind the separator programme's evidence was maximality. Maximal separators
"jump high", and coatoms can be forced into cycles. General separators can stop low: in the cycle family the response
{0} itself separates. The exact bound H − 1 suggests a stage-by-stage height argument.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite.

## 34.3z¹³⁷ Follow-up (no brief): two kinds of choice suffice — the response or a maximal separator

**Question.** In the cycle family of §34.3z¹³⁶ the response F(x, y) wins where every maximal separator fails. Do
general runs need anything beyond these two kinds?

**Class RM** (`exist_cegar.py --respmax`). For each bad pair (x, y), the separator is either the response F(x, y),
when it separates, or a maximal separator. CEGAR soundness needs exact class membership: a response choice is blocked
with the literal F(x, y) = s, a maximal choice with F(x, y) ≤ s (maximality is then static).

**Exact results** (solver results on fixed lattices, not proofs).

| Test | Result |
|---|---|
| descent-free search, max-gap potential, all 162 closure systems on 4 points (height ≥ 3) | **UNSAT on all** |
| descent-free search, 24 random closure systems on 5 points | UNSAT on all |
| exact maximum existential minimum, all 179 closure systems on 4 points (heights 2, 3, 4) | **H − 1** on every lattice |
| exact maximum, eight 5-point closure systems of height 5 | 4 = H − 1 |

These are identical to the general-separator figures of §34.3z¹³⁶. So, as far as tested, **GSB and GD hold already
in RM**: each bad pair needs one of two kinds of choice, low (the response) or high (a maximal separator).

**Which stage potential?** `respmax_audit.py` enumerates all shortest RM runs and records, for each candidate stage
potential Ψ (a function of the bad set, with h the rank), whether *some shortest* run has Ψ strictly decreasing.

| Potential | Random instances (1,145,832, \|L\| ≤ 18, H ≥ 3) | Hard instances (48,762) |
|---|---|---|
| max gap h(y) + H − h(x) | 3 failures (\|L\| = 10, H = 5) | 0 failures |
| max (h(y) − h(x)) | 3 failures (\|L\| = 10, H = 5) | 0 failures |
| max h(y) | 424 failures | 121 failures |
| −min h(x) | 15,837 failures | 43,281 failures |
| **(−min h(x), max h(y)) lexicographic** | **0 failures** | **0 failures** |

- The hard instances are the exact H − 1 CEGAR witnesses on all 4-point closure systems, plus 300 mutations each;
  41,570 of them have shortest length exactly H − 1.
- In none of the 1,194,594 instances does the shortest RM run exceed H − 1 stages.
- *Exact cross-check* (`--nodesc --pot lex` and `--pot diff`). Neither potential has a descent-free RM instance on
  any of the 162 four-point lattices, nor on 11 of 12 (lex; one undecided) and 12 of 12 (diff) random 5-point
  lattices.

The three exact potentials (gap, diff, lex) therefore always admit some descending RM run. Only lex also descends
along a *shortest* run in every instance.

**Reading.**
- The lexicographic potential says the shortest runs can be organized as an outer induction on the lowest bad row
  min h(x). Within a fixed lowest row, the highest bad column max h(y) falls at every stage.
- Its range is bounded, at most (H + 1)² values, so its descent alone would give a stage bound and Cut through
  `cut_of_terminal_grun`. The observed length H − 1 is much smaller.
- A proof attempt would show: at a stage with lowest bad row m and highest column M among the rows at height m,
  responses or maximal separators can be chosen so that either every row at height m is cleared or M drops, without
  creating bad rows below m. This is a concrete local statement to test next, since the earlier exchange failures
  concerned the global maximum gap and not this ordering.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite.
No Lean changes.

## 34.3z¹³⁸ Follow-up (no brief): no local progress lemma, even with the lexicographic potential

**Question.** Call a state T *progressive* for a stage potential Ψ if it is goal, or if some response-or-maximal
(RM) choice gives a goal state or Ψ(T′) < Ψ(T). If every state were progressive, greedy descent would work from
anywhere and a proof could be purely local. Ψ ranges over lex = (−min h(x), max h(y)), the max gap, and
diff = max(h(y) − h(x)).

**Test** (`experiments/separation/local_lex.py`). Two kinds of state are checked:
- every state reachable by RM runs with any choices;
- 20 random meet-closed families per instance.

| Instances | States | Non-progressive: lex | gap | diff |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| hard (exact H − 1 witnesses, 4-point closure systems) | 1,130 reachable | 10 | 46 | 46 |
| random, 3 × 520 s | 4,004,784 reachable | **1,034** | 1,692 | 1,692 |
| random, 3 × 520 s | 35,325,720 random families | 21,219 | 23,891 | 23,891 |

Lex fails least often, but it fails. The smallest failures have 5 elements (random families) and 6 elements
(reachable states).

**The 6-element trap** (`local_lex_fail_min6.json`).

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| L | ⊥, {0}, {2}, {03}, {012}, ⊤; height 3; covers ⊥ ⋖ {0}, {2}; {0} ⋖ {03}, {012}; {2} ⋖ {012}; {03}, {012} ⋖ ⊤ |
| D | {⊥, {0}, {2}, {03}} |
| F (cross cells; all others ⊥) | F(⊥, ⊤) = F({2}, ⊤) = {0}; F({0}, ⊤) = F({03}, ⊤) = {012}; F({0}, {012}) = F({03}, {012}) = {2} |

1. Stage 0: the bad pair (⊥, ⊤). Choosing the maximal separator {03} is a legal greedy move, and lex drops from
   (0, 3) to (−2, 3).
2. Next state: the single bad pair ({03}, ⊤) has F = {012}, which is both the response and the only maximal
   separator.
3. The meet {0} = {03} ∧ {012} enters T, and the new bad row ({0}, {012}) at height 1 *raises* lex to (−1, 2).

Rows created by meets can always fall below the current lowest row. This defeats every potential with a min-row
component, and the gap potentials fail for the same reason (§34.3z¹²⁷). Existential lex descent nevertheless held
on every instance of §34.3z¹³⁷: from ⊥ the right first move avoids the trap.

**Stage-0 kinds at height 3** (`stage_kinds.py`). Over the height-3 hard witnesses and 199 mutations of each
(11,991 instances), these stage-0 kinds begin a terminating run of ≤ 2 stages:

| Working stage-0 kinds | Instances |
|---|---|
| the response, which is a maximal coatom outside D | 11,725 |
| a maximal separator outside D, or the response outside D | 156 |
| a maximal separator outside D, or the response in D | 110 |

In every instance some separator *outside D* works at stage 0. The cycle family of §34.3z¹³⁶ shows that this cannot
hold in general: there every maximal separator of (⊥, ⊤) lies in D and only the response works. The sample is
therefore biased and only indicates a tendency.

**Reading.** Even inside RM with the best potential found, the choice must look ahead, as in §34.3z¹²⁷ and
§34.3z¹³⁵. The surviving statements remain existential: GSB(H) and GD(H) in RM, and lex descent along some shortest
run.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite.
No Lean changes.

## 34.3z¹³⁹ Follow-up (no brief): bounded lookahead is not enough; a bad first move costs width

**k-step progress** (`experiments/separation/lookahead_progress.py`). A state is *k-progressive* if some
response-or-maximal (RM) continuation of at most k stages reaches goal, or a stage potential strictly below the
state's own. The test covers all RM-reachable states of random instances (3 × 520 s, 8,162,339 states) and of the
hard witnesses.

| Potential | Not 1-progressive | Not 2-progressive | Not 3-progressive |
|---|---|---|---|
| lex | 2,122 | 42 | 1 |
| gap | 3,298 | 24 | 1 |

On the hard witnesses every state is 2-progressive. The one exception at k = 3 (`lookahead_fail_k4.json`, |L| = 16,
height 5) needs k = 4 = H − 1 for both potentials.

**Remaining distance** (`remaining_dist.py`). dist(T) is the fewest further RM stages from T to goal, the
`ShortestRun.dist` of this class.
- *Random instances (3,887,762):* max dist over reachable states exceeds H − 1 in 9 instances (7 at H = 3, 2 at
  H = 4), always by 1. The smallest have |L| = 8 (H = 3) and |L| = 11 (H = 4), saved as
  `remaining_dist_fail_h{3,4}.json`.
- *Hard witnesses:* dist ≤ H − 1 at every reachable state.
- **The cycle family** (§34.3z¹³⁶) in RM: dist(start) = 1 (choose the response {0}), but the maximum over reachable
  states is **k − 2** for k = 4..10. After a stage-0 choice of a line, a legal move that lowers lex from (0, 3) to
  (−2, 3), every later choice is forced. Each response F(lᵢ, ⊤) = l_{i+1} is also the only maximal separator, and
  lex stays at (−2, 3) until the cycle closes.

**Consequences.**
- Any greedy procedure that accepts a move because a k-step continuation lowers the potential, for a fixed k, can
  take the trap move. On the cycle family it then needs about k − 2 more stages, whatever the potential among those
  tested. So no bounded-lookahead rule over RM has width-independent run length at height 3. This is the existential
  analogue of the term-rule results of §§34.3z¹³³–¹³⁴.
- "dist ≤ H − 1 from every reachable state" is false: GSB(H) holds, as far as tested, only from the initial state.
  A proof must therefore construct the whole run, or at least its first move, *with knowledge of where it leads*:
  in the cycle family the response is right and every maximal separator is fatal. No statement about arbitrary
  intermediate states can carry the induction.

**Status.** Unchanged: Cut is open at edge-height 6 and on chain-finite lattices with both boundaries infinite.
No Lean changes.

## 34.4 The dialgebra and duality theorems (§32, §33.3)

`Mordor/Dialgebra.lean` proves four results:
- **Transposition:** the least (f, g)-dialgebra is μ(l∘f) when l ⊣ g, and dually the greatest is ν(r∘g) when f ⊣ r.
- **Failure-zone theorem:** for g preserving binary meets, δ₀ is iso iff f(A₀) ∈ Im g. It needs only a meet-semilattice and monotone f.
- **The dual failure-zone theorem.**
- **D5:** order reversal sends the least (f, g)-dialgebra of α to the greatest (gᵒᵈ, fᵒᵈ)-dialgebra of αᵒᵈ.

`Mordor/Orbit.lean` proves three results for the two orbits R₀ = ↓⊥, R_{k+1} = ↓(R_k ∪ a(R_k) ∪ b⁻¹(R_k)) and r₀ = ⊥, r_{k+1} = r_k ∨ a(r_k) ∨ ⋁{y : b(y) ≤ r_k}:
- **`step_lax`:** the lax square, ⋁Θ⁺(D) ≤ κ⁺(⋁D) for every set D.
- **`orbit_lax`:** consequently ⋁R_k ≤ r_k.
- **`orbit_tight`:** ⋁R_k = r_k for every k and every F, on any complete lattice that is top-closed. Here top-closed means every down-set containing ⊥ that misses its own join has join ⊤. No finiteness is needed.

`Mordor/CutDuality.lean` proves the adjunction Φ(D) ∩ U = ∅ ⟺ D ∩ Ψ(U) = ∅ and the k + j law Φᵏ(D) ∩ Ψʲ(U) = ∅ ⟺ D ∩ Ψᵏ⁺ʲ(U) = ∅ ⟺ Φᵏ⁺ʲ(D) ∩ U = ∅. It does so for **any preorder and arbitrary maps a, b**. §33.3 assumed monotone a, b on a finite lattice, and neither assumption is used. So the per-F self-duality of the Cut Lemma also holds on infinite lattices, including D_ω and E_ω.

## 34.4a The frontier, formally (`Frontier.lean`)

```
theorem frontier :
  (CL on L → every chain of L is finite) ∧
  (every chain of L is finite → (∀ F, R(F) join-closed) → CL on L)
```

The first conjunct is Theorem A. The second chains together chain-finite ⟹ ACC + DCC (`wellFoundedGT_of_chainFinite`, `wellFoundedLT_of_chainFinite`), `cutMaps_iff_joinClosed` and `semi_of_cut`.

With §34.3f, the hypothesis is discharged for edge-height ≤ 3. In general, the program's standing conjecture, "CL holds on every chain-finite complete lattice", is reduced in Lean to one unproved statement: **for every monotone F on a chain-finite complete lattice, R(F) is closed under binary joins.** The same reduction gives the stronger semi-commutation form (`semi_of_joinClosed`). Everything else on both sides of the frontier is checked.

## 34.5 What the formalization says about the program

- **Status of the log's proofs.** Machine-checking six results surfaced one wrong step and one gap. This is the class of error behind the earlier errata (§30.2(ii)'s height claim, the pentagon erratum, the §15.3 existence refinement). Proved results should be formalized when they land, not afterwards.
- **The open problem, sharpened.** §34.2 makes the Cut Lemma for a single F on L itself sufficient for semi-CL, so for CL, on L. Every statement on the route from the Cut Lemma to the Finite Commutation Lemma is now checked. The only unproved link between the program's conjectures and its theorems is the Cut Lemma.
- **Next targets for formalization:**
  - ~~the Cut Lemma at edge-height 4 (E_ω)~~: done by verified ground refutation (§34.3g), and edge-height 5 as well (§34.3h);
  - the two-dimensional failure on 2^ω (§23.3), now also a corollary of Theorem A since 2^ω contains infinite chains;
  - a uniform argument for all finite heights. The certificates of §34.3h share one shape. Under WLOG c ∉ D they descend c_{k+1} = F(⊥, c_k) to the first c_k ∈ D, then iterate rows and columns inside the box (c_k, c_{k−1}), e.g. F(c₁, c), F(F(c₁, c), c), F(c₁, F(c₁, c)). The game the tightness heuristic plays is the prover's side of this iteration, and its length is what a uniform proof must bound.

On this container's 4 cores that took 42 CPU-minutes for the 466 modules imported; the Mordor library then builds in under a minute.
